# Manuscript Binding (Front Cover)

->[Image of the Front Cover]<-

This historical manuscript is preserved in a light tan leather binding, showcasing an exceptional level of craftsmanship. The entire surface of the cover is decorated with a dense, symmetrical pattern achieved through <term>blind-tooling</term> <note>a decorative technique where tools are pressed into the leather to create a recessed design without the use of gold leaf or color</note>.

The decoration consists of an all-over <term>arabesque</term> <note>an artistic style characterized by intertwining fluid lines, often representing vines and leaves</note> design. These interlocking scrolling vines and stylized floral motifs are framed within a simple rectangular border that defines the edges of the cover.

The spine, visible on the left, shows significant signs of age. The outer leather has cracked and worn away in several places, revealing the darker structural material underneath. Wear is also evident at the corners and along the outer edges, reflecting the manuscript’s long history and frequent handling over the centuries.

<summary>This page presents the front cover of the manuscript, a light tan leather binding featuring intricate blind-tooled arabesque designs and symmetrical floral motifs.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript binding, leather-bound, blind-tooling, arabesque, floral motif, scrolling vines, embossed leather, book history</keywords>

->[Engraved Bookplate]<-

This page features a diamond-shaped engraved <term>bookplate</term> <note>also known as an "ex-libris," a printed label pasted into a book to show ownership</note>. At its center is a <term>coat of arms</term> <note>a heraldic design unique to a person or family</note> featuring a shield with a checkered border. Inside the shield, a closed knight's helmet sits above three mountain peaks; the central, tallest peak is topped by a small six-pointed star.

Above the shield rests a larger, barred helmet facing left, topped with a <term>crest</term> <note>an ornament appearing above the helmet in heraldry</note> showing a bear's head positioned between two roses. Flowing, leaf-like <term>mantling</term> <note>decorative drapery represented as hanging from the helmet</note> surrounds the design. The large letters **A** and **E** flank the central device.

## plating the Passion of our Lord Jesus Christ <note>original: "pẽsare e cõtemplare" — the tildes over the vowels indicate a shorthand for missing 'n' or 'm' letters, a common space-saving technique in early printing.</note>
## with a unique Treatise on the imitation of
## Christ, newly corrected and illustrated <note>original: "historiata" — this term refers to the inclusion of "histories" or narrative woodcut illustrations that help the reader visualize the scenes described.</note>. <note>The symbol "ꝛ" used in the original is a Tironian "et," a traditional shorthand for the word "and."</note>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page, which introduced the work as a manual for meditating on the Passion of Christ. The author begins here by addressing his Venetian audience and explaining his motivations for writing the book.</meta>

# The Author’s Preface.

Desiring to give our pious and devout Venetian listeners a spiritual and useful gift; and being urgently requested by many of them to provide in writing certain "golden rules" for contemplating the Passion of the Lord, which I preached to them during the last Lent <note>original: "quadragesima" — the forty-day period of fasting and prayer before Easter.</note> in our Church of Charity <note>original: "nra chiesia della Charita" — likely referring to the Santa Maria della Carità in Venice, where the author served as a preacher.</note>; I thought that to satisfy both their and my own holy desire, I should not only communicate those requested Rules to them, but that I should compose in their name a new book on this honorable and worthy subject, whose title should be *The Art of Thinking Well and Contemplating the Passion of Our Lord*. This matter has urged me all the more because, until now, I have never seen such a treatise in print—a thing certainly most 

<column-break/>

displeasing to every pious soul; for I see many vain books of little or no use being published every day in the world. And yet, regarding this sacred art of thinking well and contemplating the Passion of our Savior—in which all our well-being consists—no one writes of it, nor even mentions it. Therefore, wishing to provide charitably for the common good, I have organized and composed this present Treatise anew in the Tuscan vernacular <note>original: "lingua volgare Tosca" — the dialect that would eventually become the standard Italian language.</note>. I do not say the style of Dante, Boccaccio <note>original: "Certaldesca" — referring to Giovanni Boccaccio, the famous author of the *Decameron*, who was from Certaldo.</note>, or Petrarch, but a simple and pure style, as is appropriate for such a work. Here, the pious reader will find new documents, new annotations, new rules, new fruits, new points, and new contemplations, which he has never read nor found in similar vernacular books printed on this subject. Being, therefore, a new work and among the most useful that can be composed, I believe it will be studied and read with great delight and profit by true and spiritual Christians. With this sacred doc[trine]...

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that he wrote this manual in response to the requests of his Venetian congregation, who wanted his Lenten sermons on the Passion in written form. He emphasizes his choice of a "simple and pure" Italian style over the complex literary language of famous poets like Dante or Petrarch to ensure it is accessible to all.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Venice, Lent, Passion of Christ, Tuscan vernacular, Dante, Petrarch, Boccaccio, spiritual practice, religious education</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory argument. The opening word "trine" is the conclusion of "doctrine" from the previous page. The author is explaining that his method is both ancient (rooted in the Apostles) and "new" because of his specific arrangement and style tailored for his Venetian congregation.</meta>

### Preface

...doctrine, all the Saints have obtained Christian perfection. With this, every person—whether unlearned <term>idiota: a layperson or someone without formal theological training</term> or skilled—can more easily than with any other method arrive at the true amendment of their own stained life, and at the fulfillment and perfection of the moral and theological virtues. I affirm to all that this "old" and "new" art of thinking well, meditating, and contemplating the holy Passion of our Redeemer is necessary. I call it "old" because it began in the early Church with the Mother of the Lord and the Apostles. And there never was a true Christian, nor can there be, without some practice of this art. I also call this art "new" because it has been composed by me in a new way and order, though aided by the holy writers; these authors, having written excellently on this subject according to their own understanding, have provided me with ample material and taught me how to compose a similar little work with a different order and varying style, in order to

<column-break/>

be able to entice and draw every creature toward doing good. Therefore, I pray all my devoted Venetian listeners to receive the present little work with the pious affection with which it was composed and dedicated in their name by me. I remind them that of all our sermons—which number about two hundred, preached by us in their great city of Venice during three Lents <note>original: "quadragesime" — the forty-day fast preceding Easter.</note> and two Advents—this present doctrine will be a recapitulation and brief conclusion, serving as a spiritual testament and a memorial gift. It contains within itself the most worthy, honorable, useful, and easy doctrine that can be found. Upon my final departure, I leave and give it to them as a sign of heartfelt love. I believe that just as they have always heard my living voice willingly—by the grace of the Lord and their own good disposition, and not without spiritual fruit—they will likewise read the present book with the same fruit and affection.

a 2

<summary>The author explains that his "art" of meditation is based on apostolic tradition but presented in a new, accessible format. He offers this book to the people of Venice as a summary of his 200 sermons and as a parting gift of "spiritual testament."</summary>
<keywords>Venice, meditation, Passion of Christ, sermons, Lent, Christian perfection, spiritual testament</keywords>

# Preface of
## the Author.

Wishing to give a spiritual and useful gift to our pious and devoted Venetian listeners, and having been urgently requested by many of them to provide in writing some of our "golden rules" for contemplating the Passion of the Lord—which were preached to them during the last Lent <note>original: "quadragesima," the forty-day period of fasting before Easter.</note> in our church of Charity <note>The Church of Santa Maria della Carità in Venice.</note>—I thought I could abundantly satisfy their desire and my own holy wish, if I were to not only communicate those requested rules, but also compose in their name a new book on this honorable and worthy subject. Its title shall be *The Art of Thinking Well and Contemplating the Passion of Our Lord*. This task has urged me all the more because, until now, I have never seen a similar treatise in print—a thing certainly very 

<column-break/>

displeasing to every pious soul. For I see many vain books of little or no use being published in the world every day, yet regarding this sacred art of thinking well and contemplating the Passion of our Savior—in which all our good consists—no one writes, or hardly even mentions it. Therefore, wishing to charitably provide for the common good, I have ordered and composed this present treatise anew in the Tuscan vernacular. I do not mean the language of Dante, or Boccaccio <term>Certaldesca: referring to Giovanni Boccaccio, who was from Certaldo</term>, or Petrarch, but a simple and pure style, as befits such a work. Herein the pious reader will find new documents, new annotations, new rules, new fruits, new points, and new contemplations, which they have never read or found in similar vernacular books printed on this subject. Being, therefore, a new work and among the most useful that can be composed, I believe it will be studied and read with great delight and spiritual fruit by true and spiritual Christians. With this sacred pow... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "potere" (power) or "potenza."</note>

<summary>The author explains he is writing this book at the request of his Venetian congregation to preserve his Lenten sermons on the Passion. He justifies his use of "simple and pure" Tuscan Italian rather than the complex literary styles of Dante or Petrarch to ensure the work is accessible for spiritual growth.</summary>
<keywords>Passion of the Lord, Venice, Lent, Tuscan vernacular, contemplation, spiritual exercises, devotional literature</keywords>

# Preface
<sig>a 2</sig>

->doctrine<- <note>The text continues the word "[dot]trina" from the previous page.</note> through which all the Saints have obtained **Christian perfection**. With this, every person—whether unlearned <note>original: "idiota," meaning one without formal academic training or "plain folk."</note> or expert—can more easily than with any other arrive at the true correction of their own stained life, and at the consummation and perfection of the moral and theological virtues. I affirm to all that this old and new art is necessary: the art of thinking well, meditating, and contemplating the holy **Passion of our Redeemer**. I call it "old" because it had its beginning in the early Church from the Mother of the Lord and from the Apostles. Indeed, there never was a true Christian, nor can there be one, without some practice of this Art. I also call this art "new" because it has been composed by me in a new way and order, aided however by the holy writers. These authors, having written excellently on this subject according to their own understanding, have provided me with ample material and taught me the way to compose such a little work with a different order and various styles, in order to

<column-break/>

be able to entice and draw every creature to good works. I pray, therefore, all my devoted Venetian listeners, that they will receive this present little work with the same pious affection with which it was composed and dedicated in their name by me. I remind them that of all our sermons—which number about two hundred, preached by us in their great city of **Venice** during three Lents <note>original: "quadragesime," the forty days of fasting before Easter.</note> and two Advents—this present doctrine will be a recapitulation and a brief conclusion. It stands in place of a **spiritual testament** and a memorial gift, containing within itself the most worthy, most honorable, most useful, and easiest doctrine that can be found. It is left and given to them at my final departure as a sign of cordial love. I consider that just as they have always willingly heard my living voice—not without spiritual fruit, by the grace of the Lord and their own good disposition—they will also read the present work with the same fruit and affection.

<summary>The author presents his work as a synthesis of approximately two hundred sermons delivered in Venice, describing the contemplation of the Passion as a necessary "art" for spiritual perfection. He frames the book as a "spiritual testament" and a parting gift to his devoted congregation.</summary>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general table of contents to the formal beginning of the treatise. The author, Bonpiero of Lucca, introduces himself and his purpose for writing, specifically targeting common misconceptions and errors in popular devotion regarding the Passion of Christ.</meta>

# Treatise on Thinking Well

a iij

[Illustration: A woodcut illustration of the Agony in the Garden, enclosed within a wide decorative border of repeating square floral or geometric motifs. In the center, Jesus Christ is shown kneeling in prayer, hands joined. To the upper left, an angel descends from clouds, holding a chalice. In the lower right, three disciples are depicted sleeping on the ground. The background shows a garden landscape with trees and a wattle fence.]

¶ Here begins the art of **Thinking Well**, meditating, and contemplating the **Passion of our blessed Lord Jesus Christ**: newly composed by his useless servant <note>original: "inutile suo seruo." This is a standard formula of monastic humility, where the author diminishes his own importance to emphasize that his work is a service to God.</note> **Bonpiero of Lucca**, titled a **Canon Regular of the Lateran** <term>Canon Regular of the Lateran: a member of an ancient religious order of priests who live in a community and serve at a specific cathedral or church while following the Rule of St. Augustine.</term> and the least among the preachers of the divine word: at the request of his humble and devout **Venetian listeners**.

¶ **Part one**, in which are contained twelve beautiful **instructions** <term>Instructions: original "documenti." In this context, these are not legal papers but rather "lessons" or "guiding principles" for spiritual life.</term> and annotations which those who wish to give themselves to the holy meditation on the Passion of the Savior must know.

<column-break/>

So that the devout contemplator of the Passion of the Lord may be well illuminated and entirely free from every dangerous error—especially those that are public and common—we have described many instructions and annotations pertaining to the said history, in which many in these our times err through simplicity or indeed ignorance. Believing many things which are false and erroneous; and some are not only false, but heretical <term>heretical: beliefs or opinions that contradict the official teachings of the Church.</term> and damnable. Wherefore...

<summary>This page serves as the formal title page and introduction to the treatise, identifying Bonpiero of Lucca as the author and establishing that the first section will correct common "ignorant" or "heretical" errors in how people meditate on Christ's Passion.</summary>
<keywords>Bonpiero of Lucca, Canon Regular of the Lateran, Venice, Passion of Christ, meditation, religious instruction, heresy, Agony in the Garden</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the "Twelve Instructions" promised on the previous page. Having established his credentials and the purpose of the work, Bonpiero now moves into the specific theological corrections he believes are necessary for a proper meditation on the Passion. He starts by addressing common misconceptions about the Virgin Mary's behavior.</meta>

<header>Part One</header>

**The first annotation**
concerning those writers or
preachers of this history <note>original: "historia." In this context, it refers to the historical narrative or "story" of the Passion of Christ.</note>: of whom some write
or preach many
vain and childish things,
not at all to be accepted
as true, nor held as such:
just as **Saint Vincent** <note>Likely Saint Vincent Ferrer (1350–1419), a celebrated Dominican preacher known for his sermons on the life of Christ and Mary.</note> reproves
those who ascribe and attribute
to the mother of the Lord
that she, like a woman out of her mind,
because of immeasurable grief,
went wandering from house
to house, and in every place where
her beloved son was led;
and through the cracks and fissures
of doors looked through to
see him; and with the striking
of her hands and with a
loud and mournful voice,
wept bitterly. And
others still write that
at night through the city of
Jerusalem she went searching
for him: and other similar frivolous
things: which greatly detract
from the perfect wisdom,
modesty, and patience of the
irreproachable mother of God,
and consequently diminish
the dignity of Christ.
For if it were as
the aforementioned simply

<column-break/>

write or preach:
it would follow that many
other women, in the loss of
their children, would have
been more modest and steadfast
than the mother of God.
Which thing is very erroneous
and alien to the truth.
For, as the Greek
Doctor **John of Damascus** <note>An 8th-century monk and theologian who was a staunch defender of the veneration of Mary.</note>
says: this Virgin Mary,
being the mother of
almighty God, is surpassed
in perfection by no
creature. **Origen**, **Albert the Great**,
and commonly the sacred theologians
agree. The true histories
of many holy women relate
how they not only steadfastly
supported the deaths of
their children, but they
themselves comforted them
to suffer and die for Christ:
and with their own arms
carried them to martyrdom,
without any display of
clamorous voice, nor any other
gesture that might be a sign
of inconstancy. A similar
and much greater strength
of spirit was in the Virgin Mary,
who more than any
other woman, in every one of her ge- <note>The word "gesti" (gestures/actions) is interrupted by the page break.</note>

E i

<summary>Bonpiero critiques preachers who depict the Virgin Mary as losing her composure and acting hysterically during the Passion, arguing that such "frivolous" stories contradict her perfect wisdom and the historical examples of courageous holy martyrs.</summary>
<keywords>Virgin Mary, Passion of Christ, grief, John of Damascus, Saint Vincent Ferrer, spiritual decorum, martyrdom, maternal strength</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Annotation" from the previous page, clarifying that while the Virgin Mary’s behavior was always governed by wisdom, preachers use emotional imagery to help the faithful connect with her suffering. It then introduces the "Second Annotation" regarding the distinction between scriptural fact and meditative tradition.</meta>

<header>
->Thinking well.<-
</header>
<sig>a iiij</sig>

In every action, she was discreet, prudent, and wise. And with such charity, she desired that her only son, by his bitter death, should redeem human nature <note>The author argues Mary was a willing participant in the divine plan, contrasting her with the "hysterical" depictions he critiqued on the previous page.</note>, so much so that had it been necessary, she herself with her own hands would have carried him to the harsh gallows <note>original: "patibulo." Referring here to the Cross as an instrument of execution.</note> a thousand times.

¶ By this annotation of mine, I do not intend to rebuke those devout and affectionate preachers who, wishing to express the immense and unspeakable grief that the Virgin suffered through the most bitter passion and death of her son—and being unable by human gesture or tongue to declare such great pain—attribute to the person of the Virgin all those compassionate gestures and tearful voices that are commonly used in such excessive sorrows. I do not mean that by this they wish to suggest that the Mother of God, in such gestures, exceeded the bounds of wisdom or discretion; they only demonstrate her immense sorrow, the likes of which was never found in any other pure mortal creature <term>wayfaring creature: (viatrice) A theological term for a human being currently living on Earth, traveling toward their eternal home.</term>.

<column-break/>

And they do this for the greater benefit of the listeners, for whom this knowledge is very useful, so that they too may have the opportunity to more easily weep and feel compassion for the blessed Mother in her immeasurable pain and excessive grief.

**The second Annotation** is that many devout contemplators, or rather faithful listeners, are sometimes deceived because they do not know how to discern, within this sacred history, what the preachers say or the Doctors <note>Doctor: Refers to the "Doctors of the Church," authoritative theologians like St. Augustine or St. Thomas Aquinas whose writings define doctrine.</note> write as true and certain history—which is necessary to believe—from what they say as something merely probable or imagined through contemplation. Therefore, it should be known that the holy Evangelists wrote the history of the Savior's passion briefly, leaving out many unnecessary circumstances, because for them it was enough to testify to the world how Christ, true God and man, had died in

<summary>The author clarifies that emotional preaching about Mary's grief is intended to stir devotion, not to suggest she lacked self-control. He then introduces a distinction between essential Gospel facts and the "probable" details added by later theologians.</summary>

<keywords>Virgin Mary, Passion of Christ, preaching, theology, Doctors of the Church, Gospel history, Wayfaring creature, redemption</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Annotation" regarding the Virgin Mary’s conduct during the Passion and transitions into a discussion of the "Second Annotation," which distinguishes between scriptural fact (Gospel) and the imaginative traditions used by preachers and meditators to stir devotion.</meta>

# First Part.

<column-break/>

...was the harsh cross for human salvation, leaving many other things for us to probably contemplate, or for the Lord God to reveal to His meditators. Therefore, everything that the sacred Evangelists <term>Evangelists: The traditional authors of the four Gospels—Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</term> wrote must be believed as true, certain, and indubitable history. But many other things that have been added by devout meditators <note>original: "Contemplatori." Refers to those who practice "contemplative prayer," a form of mental prayer where one vividly imagines biblical scenes.</note> we ought to receive not as necessary for belief, but only as pious, probable, and devout things, fit to kindle human hearts toward greater charity and divine love. For example, how the Lord in his scourging was bound to the column—which is not mentioned in the Gospel. Or the weeping of the Madonna and the Apostles; in what manner the Cross was placed—that is, on the ground or upon a platform <note>original: "tauolito." Likely referring to a wooden stage or the specific construction of the scaffold.</note>; when and in what place the mother took her leave and the separation from her son was made with such words, such reasons, and such responses. And many other meditations...

<column-break/>

...are made that are not contained in the sacred Gospel text. Divine preachers say such things not as certainties but as probabilities, to give order to their preaching and to be better able, through such particular circumstances, to move the emotions of the listeners <term>Auditors: (Auditori) A traditional term for a congregation or audience listening to a sermon.</term>. Therefore, no one should be scandalized if at times these aforementioned circumstances are contemplated in various ways, and are recounted differently by preachers; because they do not intend to assert or affirm that it was so, but only that it can probably be thought and contemplated that it was so. Hence, it is permissible for every devout meditator, in such things contained outside the Gospel text, to contemplate and think in their own way, provided they do not rashly affirm anything that is contrary to Holy Scripture, or say or think things that are not for edification, or that are irrational and mocking, unworthy of being believed by the wise. ¶ By this Annotation, it is understood that...

<summary>This page defines the boundary between Gospel history and the "pious meditations" added by preachers, explaining that imaginative details are used to move the emotions of the faithful rather than to establish dogma.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the "Second Annotation," defending the use of imaginative detail in meditation by comparing it to the techniques of secular historians, and begins the "Third Annotation," which addresses common artistic and historical errors regarding the Crucifixion, such as the method of binding the thieves and the placement of Christ's wound.</meta>

->## On Thinking Well.<-

Often, devout meditators <note>original: "contemplatori." Those who engage in visual and emotional prayer, imagining themselves present at biblical events.</note> are foolishly and ineptly reviled and rebuked by undevout and skeptical Christians, who mock them for meditating on such uncertain matters. However, secular <term>profani: In this context, authors writing about worldly or "pagan" history rather than sacred scripture.</term> writers are forced to do the same in the histories of the pagans—such as when they describe the speeches made by Caesar or Pompey, along with their appropriate responses. These writers cannot know for certain that those words were spoken so formally. And thus, as stated above, these meditations should be interpreted and understood in the same way.

**Third Annotation:** It should be known that in this sacred history, many capable men have strayed from the truth, along with ignorant painters. For instance, they depict the thieves on the cross not as fastened with nails but as tied with ropes. This is shown to be not only against the holy Doctors <term>Doctors of the Church: Highly influential Christian theologians whose writings are considered authoritative.</term>—especially Augustine and Thomas <note>St. Augustine of Hippo and St. Thomas Aquinas.</note>—but also against the Gospel text of Mark in the

<column-break/>

fifteenth chapter, and Luke in the twenty-third, who say that they were "crucified" with Christ. They do not write that they were tied with ropes, but fastened to the cross; this cannot be properly understood to have been done without nails. Therefore, Augustine, commenting on Saint John, expressly testifies to this, saying:

> Hanging on the wood, fixed by nails through their hands as a sign: they were killed by a prolonged death. <note>original: "Pendentes in ligno clavis ad signum manibus confixi: producta morte necabantur."</note>

Other great men have erred by writing that the Lord was wounded with the lance on the left side. Many Catholic Doctors affirm this to be false, specifically the Master of the Scholastic Histories <note>Peter Comestor (d. 1178), whose "Historia Scholastica" was a standard university textbook for centuries.</note>, John of Damascus, Gabriel Biel, Sylvester of Prierio <note>Sylvester Mazzolini da Prierio (1456–1523), a Dominican theologian known for his works on cases of conscience.</note>, and many others. The custom of the Church agrees with them, as it does not depict Him being pierced from the left side, but from the right. And it also sings that verse which says:

> I saw water flowing from the temple from the right side. <note>original: "Vidi aquam egredientem de templo a latere dextro." A liturgical antiphon often sung during the sprinkling of holy water; here it identifies Christ's body as the "Temple" from which the water of life flows.</note>

<summary>This page justifies the use of imaginative details in spiritual meditation by comparing it to historical writing, and corrects common misconceptions about the Crucifixion regarding the use of nails for the thieves and the location of Christ's wound.</summary>
<keywords>meditation, historiography, Crucifixion, iconography, Saint Augustine, Thomas Aquinas, Peter Comestor, sacred anatomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Third Annotation," which addresses common errors in religious art regarding the Crucifixion, specifically transitioning from the method of binding the thieves to the anatomical placement of Christ's side wound.</meta>

<header>Part One</header>

“And all to whom this water came were saved, etc.” <note>original: "⁊ omnes ad quos peruenit aq̄ iſta: ſalui facti ſunt &c." A reference to Ezekiel 47:9, often interpreted as the life-giving water and blood flowing from the side of Christ.</note>
Furthermore, almost all common painters, as well as other learned men, are criticized today because they believe that our Lord was wounded in the chest with the lance, as they see Him depicted this way. But they should know that He was not wounded in the chest, but lower down, between the soft flesh and the ribs <note>original: "oſſe pettozali" — literally "chest bones."</note>; for since Longinus <term>Longinus: According to tradition, the name of the Roman centurion who pierced Jesus' side with a spear.</term> was on the ground and Jesus Christ was hanging high above, he struck Him in that aforementioned part, extending the cruel lance all the way to the heart. This is proven by Alexander of Hales, Saint Vincent, John of Damascus <note>original: "ſan Vincẽtio Damaſceno." This likely refers to John of Damascus, an influential 8th-century monk and theologian.</note>, Gabriel Biel <term>Gabriel Biel: A prominent 15th-century German scholastic theologian.</term>, and many others, by the authority of Scripture—namely Exodus, chapter twelve—which says:
> “You shall not break a bone of it.” <note>original: "Os non comminuetis ex eo." Referring to the Passover lamb, a prefiguration of Christ.</note>
For when one considers the size of the lance of Longinus—which is now preserved in Saint Peter’s in Rome—everyone affirms that if he had wounded the Lord’s loving chest with it, he would undoubtedly have broken some chest

<column-break/>

bone. This would have contradicted the authority mentioned above: namely, that no bone of the Lord was to be broken or shattered. However, by this we do not intend to disparage the approved custom of the Church and of faithful painters, who all depict our Lord wounded in the chest; they do this not to denote the exact location of the wound, but rather to make it understood that the said lance, in penetrating, reached the loving heart of the Savior. And so, piercing it, he inflicted a cruel wound upon that heart, which even before then had been wounded by His boundless love for us.

### Fourth Annotation:

Many preachers, being overly curious in wanting to know the measure of the pains suffered by our most merciful Lord upon the hard wood of the cross, say and affirm that the pain and suffering of our Savior was greater and more bitter than all other pains combined—of all men, and even of the damned. <meta>catchword: Likewise</meta>

<summary>This page argues that Christ was wounded below the ribs rather than in the chest to fulfill the prophecy that no bone be broken, while also beginning a discussion on the unprecedented intensity of Christ's suffering.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Third Annotation," moving from the physical details of Christ's wound to a theological discussion of the nature and duration of his suffering, specifically refuting the idea that he suffered the pains of the cross throughout his entire life.</meta>

### On Right Thinking

<column-break/>

Some of them have written that the Lord, from the first moment of his conception until his death, always felt all that pain and that grief which he actually suffered on the cross. These opinions are rejected as erroneous and false by good doctors <term>Doctors: Here referring to the "Doctors of the Church," eminent theologians whose teachings are considered authoritative.</term>. This is especially noted by Jean Gerson <term>Jean Gerson: A prominent 14th-century French scholar and chancellor of the University of Paris.</term> in his *Summa*; by Gabriel [Biel] in the third book of the *Sentences*; and by Stephen in his third book, where he says these ideas are "the worst and heretical, as some claim" <note>original: "pessime: e heretice vti a qdã"</note>, namely "that Christ suffered as much in his mother’s womb as on the cross" <note>original: "q̃ tantũ patiebať Xps in vtero mris: quātũ in cruce."</note>. In the course, then, of his life, our Lord—although he always suffered many mental and physical afflictions, as he himself said through Saint Luke: "I have a baptism to be baptized with, and how I am distressed until it is accomplished!" <note>original: "hẽo babtizari: e quõ coartor qusqz pficiať." A reference to Luke 12:50, where Jesus speaks of his impending death.</note>—nevertheless, being a perfect man of the most perfect constitution <term>constitution: In medieval medicine, "complexion" or "constitution" refers to the balance of bodily qualities; Christ was believed to have a perfectly balanced body, making him more sensitive to both pain and joy.</term> and consequently of the most perfect and lively bodily senses, he sometimes rejoiced and delighted himself (though never in a sinful way) when appropriate and suitable objects were present. Such as when he saw, ate, slept, smelled,

<column-break/>

tasted, and touched suitable things. It should not be thought that he did not experience at that time sensory and bodily delight, though not sinful delight. It is enough for us to know, along with the Catholic Doctors, that our most wise Savior, during the time of his Passion, tolerated as much pain and as much grief as pleased his infinite wisdom and as was fitting. And if you desire specific knowledge of this, go to the Angelic Doctor <term>Angelic Doctor: Saint Thomas Aquinas, the most influential philosopher and theologian of the Middle Ages.</term> in the third part [of his *Summa Theologica*], and you will understand that the pain of your Lord surpassed every other specific pain of this present mortal life and was the greatest that ever occurred in this life. However, it is true that the pain of those who are damned in hell, and of those who are assigned to the pains of purgatory, are greater than the pains the Savior suffered; as also the enlightened Doctor Gabriel Biel <term>Gabriel Biel: A late medieval scholastic theologian noted for his work on the *Sentences* of Peter Lombard.</term> in the third book of the *Sentences* clearly affirms.

<summary>This page refutes the "erroneous" belief that Christ suffered the agony of the crucifixion throughout his entire life, arguing instead that he experienced natural human joys. It maintains that while Christ's earthly suffering was the greatest of any mortal, it is surpassed by the pains of Purgatory and Hell.</summary>

# Fifth Annotation

It should be known that many preachers even remain entangled in confusion when they read in the scriptures that our Lord—through the violence of cruel martyrdoms inflicted upon him by the impious and dog-like Jews <note>The author uses the harsh rhetoric typical of 16th-century devotional texts regarding the role of Jewish authorities in the Passion.</note>—surrendered his soul to his Father, dying a cruel and violent death. In this matter, they become confused because it seems to them that these two things cannot coexist: that the blessed Jesus Christ died both voluntarily and violently. This is because it seems to them that "violent" is the opposite of "voluntary." However, in this case, one does not contradict the other—that is, the violent does not contradict the voluntary. Rather, they fit together perfectly: that Christ suffered a death that was at once violent and voluntary. This is because he voluntarily accepted those violent pains from the Jews; that violence was not committed against his will, but against his precious and holy flesh. Through the violence of the sufferings inflicted by the Jews, his flesh was so mistreated and cruelly tormented that, being no longer able to

<column-break/>

sustain the soul, the soul was separated from the body. Therefore, if you desire not to err in this, but to believe rightly as a good Christian, hold these three truths written below. 

The first is that your Lord suffered death voluntarily and spontaneously, because no one could force him to die, since he was God. This is proven by the Evangelist John in the tenth chapter, where Christ, speaking, said:

> I lay down my life of myself, etc. I have the power to lay down my life, and I have the power to take it up again. <note>original: "Ego pono animam meam a me ipso zc. potestatem habeo ponendi animam meam: & iterum sumendi eam." A reference to John 10:17–18.</note>

Isaiah agrees, saying:

> He was offered because he himself willed it, etc. <note>original: "oblatus est quia ipse voluit zc." A reference to Isaiah 53:7.</note>

And Augustine <term>Augustine: St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</term> says in his book *On the Trinity* that the spirit of Christ did not abandon his flesh by force, but voluntarily, because he so willed it. And so it pleased him; he left his flesh when he willed and in the manner that he willed.

**The second Catholic truth** is that our most sweet Lord died a violent death; that is, through the...

It cites scripture and St. Augustine to clarify that while his flesh was forced to expire, his will remained sovereign.</summary>

If that bliss had overflowed into his body, it would have been glorified without any death occurring. And so, through

<column-break/>

any created power, that body could not have been tormented or crucified; consequently, the glorious soul could not have been separated from its glorified body by any creature.

## Sixth Annotation

This note concerns the fact that many heretics, infidels, and even simple Christians believe our faith holds that God suffered death and torment in his divinity. This is entirely false and impossible, because God, as God, cannot suffer. We Christians are not so foolish as to speak such a potent and manifest error, though it is attributed to us by heretics and the Muslim <term>infidels</term> <note>original: "Mahumetanici." The author is defending Christian orthodoxy against the charge of Patripassianism—the heresy that God the Father suffered—or the claim that the Divine Essence itself can be harmed.</note>. We Catholic Christians, therefore, illuminated by our holy and immaculate faith, undoubtedly hold and believe to our greatest honor: that the Son of God, who is true God and equal <note>original: "ęgle," a contraction of "equale."</note> to his eternal Father, took on human flesh for our salvation—that is, our humanity—and in [it...]

<meta>This page continues the author's warning about spiritual discernment. <header>On Thinking Well.</header>

one should be cautious. It seems to me that the time has come which was foretold long ago: that *in the last days there will be an abundance of deceivers* <note>original Latin: "in novissimis diebus abundabunt illusores," a reference to 2 Peter 3:3 or Jude 1:18 regarding those who mock or mislead the faithful in the end times</note>, etc. I confess that I have known many people to whom these cases occur, and I have believed few of them to be loved by the Lord God. I believe that out of ten people who experience such things, you will barely find one who possesses the constancy required of such persons. Therefore, only the humble man easily overcomes all these spiritual temptations—he who does not believe them nor accept them on his own authority, but always submits himself to the judgment of another <note>referring to the "alieno" or external judgment of a spiritual director or confessor</note>. Every time that, through such meditation, you feel more humble in yourself; more desirous of growing in **virtue**; more desirous of the **contempt of the world**; more desirous of the future life; with more love toward your **God** and your neighbor; with more love for Christian perfection; and with more pain, repentance, and hatred of sin—then hold it for certain that you are bearing fruit in this holy

<column-break/>

exercise, and that divine grace is with you. But when you do not feel such effects—even if you were to speak every hour with **God** and with the angels, and even if you had the **stigmata** <term>stigmata: bodily marks, scars, or pains corresponding to the crucifixion wounds of Jesus</term> and the torments of the Passion more than the glorious **Francis** <note>St. Francis of Assisi (c. 1181–1226), the first recorded person to receive the stigmata</note>—hold it for certain that your meditating is not fruitful for your soul, but rather pestiferous and harmful. Therefore, in that case, you must fear greatly, because you do not have the end and the effect of holy meditation, but only have the end and the effect of the deluding **demon**. And if you were to ask me, "Father, I have no visions, nor any apparitions; I only have so much pain in my heart out of compassion for my Lord that I can barely bear it, and I have so many tears that at times they certainly make my head ache, and sometimes in this meditation I remain so weak and so contracted in my nerves that for a good space of time I cannot depart or move, and sometimes I feel a great movement..."

<header>Part One</header>

...to my heart: and I do not know what to do: I would like your advice on what I should do in these cases. I answer you, son, that since <note>original Latin: "ex quo"</note> such effects can proceed from the **Lord God** and from the **devil**, and you not knowing for certain, you must remain in suspense. Pray that the **Lord** always enlightens you to do His will; it is good, in that case, to restrain yourself. This is so that you may persist with reasonable and discreet obedience. Do not worry about wanting to be certain in such cases, because most of the time it is more expedient for you to remain in suspense with **humility** than to know for certain with the danger of pride or of too much lack of trust. If it were useful to you, the **Lord** would let you know everything. It is enough for you that even if such effects were from the **devil**, they will benefit you for the increase of **virtue**, provided you remain perfectly humble. To this end, the **Lord** permits His servants to be tempted in all ways, so that in every way, through His grace, they may be more glorious.

<column-break/>

**Ninth Annotation**: because in this **little treatise** <note>original: "Trattatello"</note> of mine I speak mainly to simple, devout women and men rather than to the learned, I do not wish to fail to give them all those warnings that might be opportune and necessary for their fragile condition. This is especially important since such warnings and instructions were first written and published to the world by worthy doctors <note>the term "doctors" here refers to "Doctors of the Church" or high-ranking theologians</note> of great experience and learning. Therefore, the most devout and Christian theologian **Jean Gerson**, Chancellor of **Paris** <term>Jean Gerson: a famous French theologian (1363–1429) and Chancellor of the University of Paris, known for his guides on how to distinguish between true and false spiritual visions</term>, says that women must be very cautious when meditating upon the nakedness of the flesh of the **Savior**. This is because, by fixing the imagination too strongly upon it through the work of the **Devil**, they could easily fall into some foul and ugly thought of **carnality**. Similarly, a similar danger would also happen to a man, on account of the difference of sex, if the nakedness of the **Virgin Mary** or of another holy martyr were contemplated with strong imagination...

...if someone wanted to consider: and if you were to tell me it seems strange to you that the flesh of the Lord—which is embalmed with the balsam of holy **divinity**—could lead women to such **impure thoughts** <note>original: "dishonesti pensieri"; in this context, "dishonest" specifically refers to unchaste or lustful thoughts</note>. And similarly regarding the Virginal and immaculate flesh of the **Virgin Mary**, of whom it is piously reported and believed that she never excited any person to any carnal movement. I answer you: that the **Lord** has endowed us with the use of **reason**, so that we may reasonably perform every act and work of ours with said reason. It is, therefore, against reason not to consider our own proneness and inclination toward lewdness, and not to flee the dangers to the soul. If we proceed without reason and do not flee the occasions of danger—as reason itself teaches us—the **devil**, interfering, leads us to foul and **impure thoughts**, with the **Lord** permitting this because of our own fault. It is not, therefore, the flesh of our **Savior** nor of the **Virgin Mary**, but the **dev-**

<column-break/>

**-il** and our own **negligence**, together with our **imagination** <note>original: "fantasia," referring to the mental faculty of creating or recalling sensory images</note>, that brings such **temptation** upon us. It brings back to our memory the **private parts** <note>original: "pudendi membri"</note> and the lustful acts of other people already known, and perhaps immodestly loved in the past. And although this warning is useful to everyone, it must nonetheless be observed with greater diligence by beginners than by the "perfect" <note>the "perfect" refers to those advanced in the spiritual life</note>, who, strengthened and made strong by a long habit of **virtue**, cannot be so easily troubled by such unclean thoughts.

...husbands <note>original: "rito," likely completing "marito" (husband) from the previous page</note> and servants, who have grown lukewarm and cooled by the enemy, think that such **spiritual meditation** does not belong to them, and that they cannot make any progress in it. And the greater the temptation, the better the sign. For crafty **Satan** <note>original: "sathanas"</note> places greater obstacles before those from whom he understands he will receive the greatest harm. One cannot achieve great **perfection** without great temptations. And you, who have the care of a family, do not be dismayed by the many **occupations** <note>original: "occupationi"; here referring to daily chores, business, or household duties</note> that press upon you so much; for the **Lord** does not ask of you anything except what you can do, once the condition of your state and your **vocation** <note>In this period, "vocation" referred to one's station in life, such as being a parent, a merchant, or a servant, rather than just a religious calling</note> has been observed; He well understands your duties. Therefore, have a great desire and put it into practice as much as the convenience of your time...

<column-break/>

...allows, and do not fail through negligence to meditate on the **Passion** in the way that is possible for you: for you cannot do a work more fitting to your state, with less effort, with more fruit, and a greater crown.

### Eleventh Annotation

That the devout contemplator of this sacred **Passion** and death of our **Savior** must stay vigilant so that the enemy does not deceive him. Because when the contemplator is seen by the **devil** <note>original: "demonio"</note> to be in great fervor and in great taste <note>original: "gusto," referring to a sense of spiritual sweetness or delight felt during prayer</note> and feeling for the **Passion** of our **Savior**—the diabolical envy being unable to bear such merit in a human and bodily creature—with all his crafty and malignant knowledge, he strives to make him fall into some error, and in some way offend his Creator. And because many times he cannot wound <note>original: "illagare," likely a variant of *piagare* (to wound)</note> him with carnal and palpable sins, he tempts him with **spiritual sins**, which are of greater offense toward God and of le- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "lesser" (minore) or similar in the next section</note>

-># On Good Thinking <-

...[great]er infamy than that among men. He tempts them first, therefore, with **pride**, but does so secretly by suggesting to the **contemplative** that their spiritual joys <note>original: "gusti," literally "tastes," referring to the internal sweetness or consolation felt during prayer</note> are of great merit. He makes them speak willingly about these experiences with their neighbor under the guise of wanting to edify and instruct them in the way of the **Lord**. They do not realize that the devil does this to lead them into **vainglory** <term>vainglory: an older term for "vanagloria," meaning seeking personal praise or feeling a sense of self-importance regarding one's spiritual achievements</term> and to make them lose all their merit; or even to lead them into bad company through which they may be utterly ruined. For many times, through diabolical deception, beginners who improvidently strive to convert a companion to **divine service** are instead themselves converted back and withdrawn into the **worldly life**. Therefore, the new contemplative must not presume so much of themselves but should wait for the time when they are firmly established in **virtue** before they seek the salvation of others at their own peril. Let them follow the example of the tree, which first attends for a long time only to rooting itself well in the earth; and then, once rooted

<column-break/>

and well-grown, produces the desired fruits for others. Another temptation is also commonly very familiar to new contemplatives: as soon as <note>original: "quamprimū," a Latinism meaning "as soon as"</note> they begin to profit and improve in the way of the **Lord God**, they immediately begin to **judge their neighbor**. They think to themselves that those who do not have the grace to meditate on the mysteries of the Lord must be of little merit in the divine sight. They do not consider the hidden judgments of the infinite **wisdom of the Creator**, who distributes His gifts in hidden ways. Often, those practiced in the **active life** <term>active life: (vita attiva) a spiritual path focused on external works of mercy, charity, and labor, often contrasted with the "contemplative life" of silent prayer</term> are of greater merit than those practiced in the **contemplative life**. Oh, how many persuade themselves that they will be greater than others in **Paradise**, yet will be much lesser there! And perhaps, even deprived of eternal glory, they will find themselves among the wicked, held in pains and torments far greater than other damned souls. Therefore, the contemplative must flee such dangerous judgments like a mortal poison.

### Eleventh & last 
### annotation:
And this one makes us understand what the cause is that—since this **mental exercise** of thinking and meditating on the **Passion of the Lord** is so easy and so 

<column-break/>

fruitful—so few are found who exercise themselves in it, and so few are those who produce any significant fruit from it. 
I answer you briefly that nowadays worldly people have the least concern for the **salvation of the soul**; so much are the wretched and utterly unhappy and unfortunate ones occupied in these worldly cares of the body that never, or rarely, do they remember **death** and the future life. 
Alas, how few we see who are solicitous of their salvation! And if a few are found who sometimes wake up and begin some laudable undertaking to save themselves, it rarely happens that they persevere for long. Because, being slowed down by the **flesh**, the **world**, and the **demons**, and regretting every small effort for their salvation, they easily leave the holy life they have begun. They return suddenly—as if disgusted by the **celestial food of manna**—to eat **garlic and onions**, <note>A biblical reference to the Israelites in the desert who, despite being fed by God with miraculous manna, longed for the pungent foods they had in Egypt (Numbers 11:5).</note> seeking their former carnal, worldly, and earthly pleasures...

The cause, then, why so few ascend the summit of the **mountain of contemplation** is nothing other than human fault and negligence, which consists of two things. 

The first is that rarely is this exercise of contemplating and meditating on the **Passion of the Lord** begun with those proper ways and paths necessary for so noble and worthy an undertaking. Just as medicine prescribed by a skilled physician to the sick does not benefit—and indeed often harms—if it is not received with the proper circumstances <note>The author uses "circumstances" in a medical and rhetorical sense, meaning the specific conditions such as timing, mindset, and preparation required for a treatment to work.</note>, so I say that the aforementioned meditation—which by its nature is an excellent medicine for the weak, infirm, and languishing soul—benefits little or nothing if it is not executed and performed with the proper circumstances. Rather, it often greatly harms. 

And if you ask me: "With what cir-

<column-break/>

cumstances ought this mental exercise of thinking on the Passion of our **Redeemer** be performed?" I answer you: that in this chapter I cannot express them in few words; therefore, I reserve explaining this well to you in the fourth part of this present Treatise, where with **twelve golden rules** I will declare to you the method to be held in wishing to fruitfully and usefully begin and pursue this very important aforementioned exercise of contemplating the life and death of the Lord.

The second thing that hinders the soul's ascent to the summit of the mountain of this **Christian meditation** is not remaining firm in purpose, but often desisting and falling short of it. This happens to many who begin this meditating with great fervor and then, little by little, cooling down, they fail from day to day, until in a short time they find themselves cold, lazy, negligent, and lovers of the world <note>original: "amatori del secolo"; literally "lovers of the age," referring to those who prefer worldly, material life over spiritual devotion.</note> and of plea- [sures].

### Part One.

**Perseverance** is therefore necessary, because **not he who has begun, but he who has persevered until the end shall be saved** <note>original Latin: "non qui inceperit: sed qui pseuerauerit vsq; in finem saluus erit." A reference to Matthew 24:13.</note>; for it is perseverance alone that deserves the crown. And if you were to say to me: "Father, it is sometimes necessary, especially for us worldly people, to pause and interrupt not only this, but every other holy practice—both of **prayer** and **meditation**—because the necessity of our **secular living** <note>The author uses "secolare viuere" to refer to the life of laypeople living in the world, as opposed to monks or clerics.</note> constrains us so." To this I answer you—and note well my words—there is no doubt that **he who has done what he could has fulfilled the law** <note>original Latin: "qui fecit quod potuit legem adimpleuit."</note>. In the event that you leave the act of meditating due to many occupations, I do not forbid it; but I do strictly forbid you from interrupting and abandoning your good purpose and your desire—whether actual or habitual—to meditate, if you wish to profit in the way of salvation. Hence, you must know that one can leave **meditation**, **prayer**, **contemplation**, and similar spiritual exercises in two ways: in one way, by leaving the act of them but

<column-break/>

not the purpose or the true desire for them. In the other way, by leaving both: that is, the act and also the purpose of praying, meditating, etc. In the first way, when it is done for a legitimate cause, I do not rebuke you, nor do you fail in the required **perfection** because of it. But in the second case, you are worthy of reproach, because you fall away from perfection without a legitimate cause. For no worldly occupation can hinder you from always maintaining a good purpose to meditate, think, and pray when you are free from worldly and family business. To leave the act of meditation for a legitimate cause is not to turn backward on the spiritual path or in the ascent toward **Christian perfection**. Rather, it is either to remain firm in a good sentiment, or—as I believe—it is a continuous growing and ascending, because before the Lord God **the will is taken for the deed** <note>original Latin: "voluntas pro facto reputatur."</note>. And it is not without great merit to consume

### Part Second

...Passion of the Savior; nevertheless, so as not to be too long-winded, we will recount only twelve of the principal ones, drawn from the devout and holy Doctors.

The first fruit acquired through the devout meditation on the Passion of the Lord is called perfect conversion and penance for one's sins. Wherefore it is to be known that perfect penance contains three parts: that is, first, contrition and repentance for one's sins; second, true and complete confession; third, true and not feigned satisfaction <note>In this context, "satisfaction" refers to the performance of penance or works to repair the harm caused by sin.</note>. A reason can be assigned for these things: because as the scripture says, <note>original Latin: "Deus ignis conſumés eſt"</note> "God is a consuming fire." Since you, as the meditating person, are near the Lord God—who is a consuming fire—it is fitting that you be purged by that fire and every rust of sin be taken from you. This cannot be done without the three aforementioned things, all of which are found by the pious Contemplator in this sacred history of the Passion. First,

<column-break/>

there you have the sign and the memory of the contrition for your sins: when you consider that at the death of your Redeemer the rocks were split and the earth shook. Now, if the Passion of our Lord was of such power that it split the stones and made the earth tremble, will it not be of such virtue that it will make you break your heart—hardened in sins—bringing it to contrition and repentance for every evil deed? And if the earth shook, which was insensible <note>Meaning without feeling or life; inanimate.</note>, will not the sensible and rational man shake much more, remembering that for his great sins it was necessary for the Lord to suffer such pains? And thus with ease you will acquire perfect contrition for your crimes. Then you will be led to holy confession when you contemplate that other point: <note>original Latin: "monumenta aperta ſunt"</note> "and the tombs were opened." The stinking monument and foul-smelling sepulcher is the conscience of the sinner, which by virtue of this holy meditation is opened...

<summary>This page describes the first of twelve spiritual benefits of meditating on Christ's Passion: the achievement of perfect penance. It uses the biblical imagery of the earthquake and the opening of tombs at the Crucifixion to illustrate how a sinner's hardened heart should break in contrition and their conscience should open in confession.</summary>

# On Right Thinking

...revealing every one of his foul sins to the Priest. To satisfaction <note>In a religious context, "satisfaction" refers to the works or penance performed to repair the harm caused by sin and to fulfill one's duty to God.</note> you will be effectively moved when you see your Lord hanging on the Cross, all torn, mangled, and dead for the satisfaction of your sins. You would be entirely blameworthy if—seeing your God fasting, praying, and giving alms, giving Himself entirely, and likewise suffering and dying so cruelly for the evil life you have led—you still did not suffer or labor in fasting, praying, giving alms, and so on, out of a desire to satisfy that high divine majesty. Therefore, if you wish to purge yourself well of sins and overcome them entirely, contemplate your Lord on the Cross. For as Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD) was an influential early Christian theologian.</note> testifies: "Such is the power of the Holy Cross and of the death of our Savior that, if it is placed well before the eyes of the mind through intense meditation, no lust, no concupiscence, no rage, and no sin will be able to

<column-break/>

rule over the soul." Instead, the entire army of sin will immediately be expelled from the soul and powerfully put to flight.

The **second fruit** of the meditation on the Passion is called the **enlightenment of the mind**; because He of whom you think is the highest light, just as He said of Himself: "I am the light of the world." <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 8:12.</note> By this light, then, you will be illustrated and enlightened in this sacred meditation, so that you will know how to order and arrange your whole life: first, regarding the Lord God, how He is to be loved, feared, and obeyed; second, regarding your neighbor and regarding yourself, and in what way you are obligated to conduct yourself with everyone. You will become more learned in this holy practice than if you had studied any other human science for a long time; for the doctrine received from human teachers often induces pride, but this gives you the light of all the virtues—such as charity, humility, faith, hope, wisdom, and the like—and makes you [proficient] in these neces... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "necessarie" (necessary).</note>

# Part Two

...sary and excellent virtues. For it purges the soul of the remnants of sins and of evil desires <note>The original term "concupiscentie" refers to "concupiscence," or strong worldly desires that lead one away from spiritual focus.</note>; and it wonderfully illuminates the mind regarding all things necessary for salvation. And therefore Saint Bernard—or rather the Apostle Paul—boasted of this fullness of knowledge when he said: I resolved to know nothing among you except Jesus Christ and Him crucified. <note>original Latin: "nihil arbitratus sum me scire inter vos: nisi Iesum Christum & hunc crucifixum." This is a famous quote from 1 Corinthians 2:2.</note>

The frequent memory of this <term>sacred Passion</term> <note>The "Passion" refers to the suffering and death of Jesus Christ.</note> will make you a supreme philosopher—not of worldly philosophy, but of the divine and celestial kind. And if you are ignorant, it will make you so learned that, even as a woman, you will surpass the great theologians.

Happy, then, is the one who has well studied and read this "book of life." <note>The "Book of Life" is a common mystical metaphor; here it refers to the body of Christ on the cross, which the believer "reads" to understand God's love.</note> Happy, I say, and filled with every good, will be the one who has given themselves to this holy study for a long time. You cannot do anything that gives you a greater knowledge of the Lord God—that is, how merciful and loving He is toward human nature. And

<column-break/>

that makes you better know and flee the malice of sin. You will come to know your own imperfections more subtly through the frequent meditation on this Passion and death of the Savior than by any other means. 

In this meditation, you will understand how much malignancy and offense is contained in sin, for which it was necessary for the Son of God to suffer and die so cruelly. You will see how great and excessive divine <term>charity</term> <note>In this context, "charity" (carità) means the selfless, sacrificial love of God for humanity.</note> has been, and how dear and precious human souls are, for whose sake He who is the true God willed to be crucified and die, giving for them His own life—a life more valuable and precious than a thousand worlds.

And therefore, concluding this most wonderful second fruit, I only bring forward the authority of the divine Bernard who says:

> Nothing is so effective for purging and sharpening the edge of the mind as the diligent meditation on the wounds of Christ, etc. <note>original Latin: "Nihil adeo efficax ad purgandum: & acuendum aciem mêtis: qm̃ Christi vulnerū sedula meditatio &c."</note>

->Del ben pensare.<-
->On Right Thinking.<-

meditation: it will be easy for you to weep for your own and others' sins. The reason for this is because this history of the Passion and death of the Lord is the most tearful and sorrowful that can ever be found anywhere else: both on the part of the dying person, who was God and man; and because of the circumstances, as there was never in the world such great pain and such great martyrdom; and also on our part, as we, with our demerits and great sins, have been the cause of such a horrible and painful death. Who is the person who would not weep, seeing all creatures weeping for their Creator? The Son of God wept many times; Mary Magdalene wept, and the other devout women; the apostles wept; the Sun and the Moon wept in their own way, as did the stones and the earth. <note>This refers to the biblical accounts of the eclipse and the earthquake that occurred during the Crucifixion.</note> How could it be possible that, among so many cries and tears, a human—who is compassionate by nature and for whose sake all others have wept—should not weep and shed tears bitterly? Certainly, such a person would be harder than stones, more unfeeling than the earth, and crueler than beasts.

<column-break/>

You, therefore, who cannot weep, take this remedy, which you will find more useful for your soul than any other. As it says in Isaiah 12: You will draw water from the fountains of the Savior. <note>original Latin: "haurietis aquas de fontibus Salvatoris."</note> You will be greatly comforted by this grace of tears if, in this sacred meditation, you ask for them with urgent prayers to the weeping Christ. With these tears, you will then be able to frequently wash and cleanse your soul, just as those who wish to appear before the Lord with <term>purity and cleanliness</term> <note>The original "munditia" implies a ritual or spiritual scouring of the soul to make it fit for God's presence.</note> are accustomed to do.

### Fourth fruit and effect

O taste of spiritual sweetness! None can understand, except those who experience it, how much sweetness is caused by this holy and blessed memory of the Passion of our Lord. Saint Augustine knew this well when he said: Your wounds, Lord Jesus, are full of sweetness and love; through these openings, I am permitted to taste how sweet the Lord is. <note>original Latin: "vulnera tua domine Jesu: plena sunt dulcedine: & charitate per has rimas licet mihi gustare: quaz suauis est."</note>

### Part Two

"My God: because in truth, a great abundance of divine sweetness has been given to us within the wounds of Christ." <note>original Latin: "deus meus: quoniam revera data est nobis in vulneribus Christi magna multitudo dulcedinis diuine."</note>

And in the Song of Songs, it is written to this same purpose:

"I sat under the shadow of him whom I desired, and his fruit was sweet to my taste." <note>original Latin: "Sub umbra illius quem desiderabam sedi: & fructus eius dulcis gutturi meo." This biblical verse (Song of Songs 2:3) was traditionally used by medieval writers to describe the soul finding rest and spiritual nourishment under the "shadow" of the Cross.</note>

And if you were to ask me: how can it be that the wounds of the Savior, which make the sinner weep bitterly, also give sweetness? I answer you that the Passion of the Lord does not only lead us to bitter weeping through the consideration of our sins, which were the cause of it. It also provides us with a pleasant taste and great sweetness when we remember that, through the great mercy and love the Savior bears for us, he did not refuse to suffer such a harsh death for our sake. Therefore, the cause of such a Passion, when considered, generates bitter weeping in us; but the resulting effect of our salvation, and the kindness of our Lord clearly demonstrated to us within it, makes us rejoice with great

<column-break/>

joy. The soul feels no small smoothness and sweetness, seeing our salvation procured by such a great Lord at such a dear price and with such great pain.

The fifth fruit is called **petitioning** <note>The original term "impetratione" refers to the act of obtaining something through prayer or request.</note>: whoever wishes to easily obtain the fulfillment of their requests should frequent this most wholesome meditation, in which the soul makes itself entirely intimate and close to the Lord. Throughout the entire time of his mortal life, the Lord was always kind and gracious in granting graces to those in need who asked for them; but at the end of his life, he willed not only to be liberal, but even extravagant <note>The author uses "prodigo" (prodigal) here in a positive sense, meaning Christ was "lavish" or "wasteful" with his mercy.</note>, giving Paradise to the thief for a brief and small request, and praying so sweetly for his cruel but ignorant crucifiers. Thus I say that in every act of devotion, you will always find broad mercy in the divine presence; but especially in this sacred exercise of contemplating and remembering

...an example of all virtues: against each and every vice. Against pride and disobedience, see his precious head bowed in humility and the crown of thorns received through obedience to the Father. Against fraternal hatred, behold the loving prayer made to his dear Father for his crucifiers, when praying with tears he said:
> "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do." <note>original Latin: "Pater ignosce illis: quia nesciunt quid faciunt."</note>

Against avarice, contemplate his nakedness; he gave everything, reserving nothing for himself. Against lust, see the harsh scourges when they cruelly tormented his virginal flesh. Against anger, see him all loving and kind, always silent like a gentle lamb, or answering humanely like a friend; and as a sign of great love, with his sacred side opened by the cruel lance, he shows you his heart, wounded by great charity. Against gluttony, remember that in his great thirst he drank vinegar and gall. Against envy, look upon the groans, the

<column-break/>

tears, and sighs. Against sloth <note>The original term is "accidia" (acedia), a state of spiritual apathy or listlessness often counted among the seven deadly sins.</note>, keep in mind that from the beginning of the Passion he never rested until his sacred feet were fastened to the cross with cruel nails. Behold how clearly the examples of virtues against vices are shown to be found in this divine story; and if you then wish to make great progress in all virtues easily and quickly, exercise yourself diligently in this sacred memory, and you will see that by its power you will soon be filled with every virtue.

**Seventh fruit: called fortitude in tribulations.** As Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note> and Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430), a theologian whose writings shaped much of Western Christianity.</note> say, no remedy is found more effective for bearing all tribulations in peace than the memory of what the Lord suffered for our redemption. Isidore <note>Likely Saint Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), a scholar and bishop known for his vast encyclopedic works.</note>, speaking on this matter, says: nothing is so hard that it does not become sweet and pleasant to endure through the memory of the Savior's passion. Saint [Name] <note>The text cuts off before the full name, likely Saint Jerome or another Doctor of the Church.</note> confirms the same...

# On Thinking Well

...but Saint Bernard. Therefore, if you are poor, remember that the Creator of the universe, though most rich, reduced Himself to such great poverty that He truly said: *"Foxes have dens, and the birds of the sky have nests; but the Son of Man has nowhere to rest His head."* <note>original Latin: "Vulpes foueas habent: & volucres cęli nidunt: filius autem hominis non habet ubi caput suū reclinet"</note> If in your body you feel sharp pains in your head or your bowels, think that your Lord, from head to foot, was so full of torments that He had no healthy part left. The same applies to enduring heat, cold, thirst, hunger, persecutions, insults, infamy, and other similar evils; your Redeemer was not exempt from any of these. You also, wishing to follow in His footsteps, must remain constant and truly patient in every adversity.

The **eighth fruit** for those who frequently remember the death of the Lord: when they reach the end of their life, they will be granted a special sense of security and a particular help in that frightening departure <note>The "frightening departure" refers to the moment of death or the soul's exit from the body.</note>. And this is a fitting thing,

<column-break/>

because since the faithful Christian has, throughout their life, accompanied the Lord as much as possible with their memory and affection—and helped Him with the love of a good soul, desiring to have assisted Him in such great need—the Lord, being entirely grateful, kind, and gracious, will not be unmindful of such loving service. We should believe that He also will deign, in a particular way, to help and assist His friend who is in agony and anxiety at the end of their life. 

We have a notable example of this in His blessed Mother, and Mary Magdalene, and John the Evangelist; because they never abandoned their Lord, they deserved not to be abandoned by Him at the final moment of their own lives. For this reason, by a particular privilege, each of them died a "privileged death," suffering no distress or pain in that transition. But only through the power of...

# ¶ Part Three: in which twelve stimuli will be enumerated, by which every hardened and unrefined person may be encouraged and led to easily weep for the cruel death and bitter passion of our most pious Redeemer

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a decorative rectangular border of repeating floral scrollwork. The scene depicts the Flagellation of Christ. The proud mind of the human creature does not deign to have compassion for its Lord dying on the cross. The hardened soul thinks, in its self-importance <note>original: "gravita," referring to a sense of worldly dignity or seriousness</note>, that it is not fitting to weep and lament in such a case. Blind and insolent worldlings <note>Those preoccupied with worldly status rather than spiritual matters.</note> consider weeping out of brotherly piety and compassion to be an unworthy and unbefitting thing for manly dignity <note>original: "gravita virile"</note>; they judge

it so, saying that this is the duty of lowly women <note>original: "vile feminuccie," a dismissive diminutive used here to reflect the arrogant attitude of the "worldlings."</note> and not of magnificent men. <meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, which refutes the idea that weeping is unmanly or beneath a person of high status. It moves from historical examples of great men weeping to the specific spiritual necessity of weeping for the Passion of Christ.</meta>

# On Thinking Well

the wife of King Darius was moved to tears by compassion. No more valiant warrior or more generous Emperor was ever found than the Roman Caesar, who nonetheless wept for the death of his adversary Pompey the Great. King David was strenuous and bold in his battles, and nonetheless he wept over Saul and Jonathan killed in battle; he did the same over the tomb of Abner, killed by Joab and Abishai, and over Amnon his son, killed by Absalom; and finally, weeping, he said: *My Absalom, my son Absalom* <note>original: "Abſalon mi: fili mi Abſalon"</note>. Of many others who have been reputed great men in the world, I could recite similar histories; they, having heard of the common death and usual misfortunes that occurred, could not restrain themselves from tears. How much more, then, is it fitting for us to weep, cry, and grieve—not for the death of Pompey or any other mere creature, but for that death which was more cruel than any other: unjust,

<column-break/>

wicked—not the death of an earthly king, but of Him who governs and rules the whole world. Not the death of a stinking and foul sinner, but of Him who never committed sin, nor was any guile <note>The original "duolo" here likely refers to "dolo" (deceit/guile), referencing the biblical description of Christ's sinlessness in 1 Peter 2:22: "neither was guile found in his mouth."</note> ever found in His mouth. In order then that every faithful Christian may understand how momentous and necessary it is to weep for this sacred passion, here we will set down twelve stimuli <note>Spiritual "goads" or motivations intended to stir the soul into action or emotion.</note> which will not only soften every hard heart to devout tears, but will force you <term>in a certain way</term> <note>Latin: "quodammodo"</note> to weep at length for this so great martyrdom and torment of our most merciful and loving redeemer. But before I come to these stimuli, for the consolation of many, I want to ask: what can be hoped for from one who cannot weep for this bitter passion of the Savior? I answer you that two kinds of tears are found. Some are corporal, proceeding from the bodily eyes; some others are "cordial" <note>From the heart (Latin: "cor"). This refers to an internal state of sorrow rather than the physical act of crying.</note>, not appearing in the eyes, but from the afflicted and bit[ter]...

->## On Right Meditation.<-
