daily ceremonies: it reminds us of the entire history of this sacred Passion: so that several times a day we may make a tearful and mournful memory of it.
**T**he third stimulus: that whoever does not feel compassion for our Savior while meditating on this, His cruel death: is a sign: that he is separated from Him: and alienated from all His overflowing <term>merit (merito)</term> <note>In Catholic theology, "merit" refers to the "reward" of grace earned by Christ’s sacrifice, which is shared with the faithful.</note>. You have the example in the members of our own body, which feel the suffering <note>original: "passioni"</note> of the whole body as long as they are united and linked to that body. But as soon as the limbs are severed from the body: they no longer feel: and they do not communicate nor participate in the sufferings and pains of the said body. Thus, not having compassion for Christ Jesus: is a sign that the person is not united with Him in charity, nor linked. Consider when a relative or friend of yours dies or suffers: you feel suffering together with them; but if they are not united to you by any bond of kinship or friendship, even if they...

<column-break/>

...endure however much anxiety or pain they might, you have no compassion nor do you feel pain for them: because they are in no way joined to you. This is what Aristotle <note>The text refers to *De Regimine Principum* (On the Government of Princes), a popular medieval political treatise often attributed to Aristotle or Thomas Aquinas.</note> said in the book *On the Government of Princes*: <note>original Latin: "quae simul coniuncta naturaliter gaudent et tristantur de eisdem."</note> *those things joined together naturally rejoice and grieve over the same things*. And Saint Paul confirms this to the Corinthians, saying: <note>original Latin: "si quid patitur unum membrum compatiuntur omnia membra."</note> *if one member suffers, all the members suffer with it*. 

<image-desc>decorative flourish</image-desc>

How much this is a true sign to make you know: if you are united with Christ in love and charity! For if you feel compassion for His bitter piercing <note>Likely referring to the spear-thrust in Christ's side or the nails of the crucifixion.</note>, it is a sign that you love Him with a good heart; but if you do not have compassion for Him, it is a sign that you have no friendship or connection with Him.

...alienation. This is drawn from the apostolic authority of **Saint Paul**, who says to the **Corinthians**: "if you are companions in suffering, you will also be companions in consolation" <note>original: "se farete compagni delle passiōi: sareti anchora cõpagni delle consolationi"; see 2 Corinthians 1:7.</note>. There was a man devoted to the **Cross** and **Passion** of the Lord, who had a habit every time he went to bed, and every time he rose from bed, of saying these words: "Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews, have mercy on me" <note>original Latin: "Jesus nazarenus rex iudeorum; miserere mei."</note>. And thus he would sign himself, making the sign of the holy **Cross**, and saying: "In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Amen." 

It happened that he died unexpectedly of a sudden death, and immediately **demons** were present, wanting to carry him away with them to hell. But by a "good being," all bright and resplendent, who suddenly 

<column-break/>

appeared there, the said malignant spirits were driven away. And then he also immediately disappeared. But the **demons** returned anew, wanting to cruelly seize him. And so the aforementioned bright man—that is, the **angel** of God—returned again against the **demons**, who were complaining that violence was being done to them. This was because this soul had been separated from its body while in <term>mortal sin (mortale peccato)</term> <note>In Catholic teaching, a "mortal" sin is a grave offense that cuts the soul off from God’s state of grace; dying in this state would normally lead to damnation.</note>. <meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page regarding a man who was granted a miraculous return to life to perform penance. ->The beauty of holy thinking.<-

privilege and grace: He has pardoned him: and thus He wills that he return to his body: to his former mortal life: so that for his sins he may perform worthy penance: and so, having been resurrected, he lived a commendable life: and died in a holy state: resting in his Lord eternally, etc.

### Fifth stimulus:
and that he who does not weep for the death of the Lord is deprived of the perfection of all virtues. For it is said by theologians in the third book of the *Sentences* <note>Referring to Peter Lombard's *Sentences*, the standard theology textbook of the Middle Ages, which organized Christian doctrine into four books.</note> that charity is the "form" and perfection of all virtues. But he who has no compassion for our Lord in His many bitter torments and harsh martyrdoms has no charity; and consequently, he has no perfection of virtue. That such a person lacks charity can be proven by the authority of the divine Areopagite, Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite was a Christian theologian and philosopher; in the Middle Ages, his works were considered foundational for mystical theology.</note>, in the book *On the Divine Names*, who says:

> Love is a certain uniting force, binding the lover to the beloved, or into the likeness of the beloved. <note>original Latin: "amor est quedam vis vnitiua copulans amantem in amatum: seu in similitudinem amati."</note>

And from this

<column-break/>

sentiment, the Prince of natural philosophers, Aristotle, does not disagree, in the ninth book of the *Ethics*, where he says: 

> A friend behaves in all things toward a friend as he does toward himself, because a friend is another self. <note>original Latin: "q̄ amicus se habet in omnibus ad amicum tanq̃ ad seipsum: eo q̄ amicus ē alter ego." This famous Aristotelian definition of friendship emphasizes the deep empathy required between true companions.</note>

And therefore, the true friend being transformed into the beloved, he feels all his passions—that is, his joy and his sorrow. And therefore he rejoices with the joyful friend, and grieves with the saddened friend; and if he did not do this, it would be an evident sign that there was no true friendship between them, but a false, feigned, and simulated love.

Part Three

...humble and contrite heart: he weeps on Good Friday for the Passion of the Savior. This aforementioned judgment is strengthened by another very authentic story of Saint Albert <note>Saint Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher who was a teacher of Thomas Aquinas; he was widely cited in the Middle Ages for his mystical visions.</note>: to whom the Savior appeared and revealed that eight things, more than all others that could be done in this world by a human creature, were pleasing and acceptable in His sight; among which one was weeping for His death and Passion. Wherefore He said to him: "Know, my son Albert, that if even a single tear is shed in this life by a Christian out of love for my bitter Passion, it is more pleasing to me than if, after death, they were to shed as many tears for love of me as there are drops in the entire sea; and for weeping over my death, I will give a reward that exceeds and surpasses all past, present, and future goods of this present mortal life." This stimulus is also confirmed by the example of the saved Thief <note>Referring to Saint Dismas, the "Good Thief" who was crucified alongside Jesus and expressed remorse.</note>: because on the cross he had compassion for the suffering Christ, and similarly

<column-break/>

by the authority of Albertus Magnus and Saint Bonaventure <note>A 13th-century Italian medieval scholastic theologian and philosopher, often called the "Seraphic Doctor."</note>, and of many other great theologians, who have said and written: that nothing is so beneficial for the universal satisfaction <note>In a theological sense, "satisfaction" refers to the reparation made for a sin.</note> and cleansing of the human creature—and nothing is so fruitful and useful to the good Christian—as the daily memory and frequent meditation upon this sacred and blessed Passion. If you will also recall the twelve marvelous fruits we have listed above, which these pious and devout contemplators of the life and death of our most merciful Redeemer acquire: they will be to you like twelve spurs and twelve great incentives to begin courageously and to pursue victoriously this most laudable and, more than any other, fruitful and useful practice. I therefore call every faithful Christian anew to this art of meditating on the mysteries of our kind Christ Jesus, and I encourage them with all my heart: let them not wish to lose such a great good.

[The sun has] dressed itself for grief in black and dark garments, marvelously invited: the broken, hard stones and the foul-smelling marble monuments call to you: the whole earth is trembling: and the veil of the temple from top to bottom is all divided and shattered. If, therefore, you do not show signs of grief, you will be more unfeeling than the sun, harder than the stones, colder than marble, firmer than the earth, more arid, blind, and dry than a wall. # On Thinking Well #

[...so that they would not show ingratitude] toward their Lord by not weeping; and although in that state of eternal bliss it is impossible to weep, nevertheless **Saint Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note> says: "I believe what I am telling you, namely, that at the said death they grieved." For just as it was possible for the **Son of God**, in the human form he assumed, to die, so it was possible for the blessed angels to feel sorrow at the death of the **Redeemer**. This opinion is also held by other pious contemplators and preachers. In confirmation of this, the author of the *Clock of Wisdom* <note>original: "Horologio della sapientia," a popular 14th-century devotional book by the mystic Henry Suso.</note>—who was blessed, famous for his miracles, and a member of the celebrated Order of Preachers of **Saint Dominic**, named **Henry** <note>Heinrich Suso (c. 1295–1366).</note>—recounts a vision. While he was praying and feeling great compassion for the death of the Lord, an **Angel** appeared to him, saying: "Know, dear brother, that when your Redeemer hung on the cross, all wounded and reviled, many angels descended from heaven, assumed human bodies, and remaining near the cross, wept with most overflowing

<column-break/>

tears." They did this wishing to fulfill the prophecy of **Jeremiah** in the thirty-third chapter <note>The author attributes this to Jeremiah 33, but the quote "Angeli pacis amare flebunt" actually comes from Isaiah 33:7.</note>, which says: "The angels of peace shall weep bitterly" <note>original: "Angeli pacis amare flebunt"</note>. Consider, therefore, how significant these tears are, shed out of love and compassion for **Christ**; if the angels leave heaven and clothe themselves in these human bodies of ours to be able to perform this pious and necessary duty of weeping, how much more is this act fitting for us? We, by our very nature, have an inclination toward compassionate weeping, and we are spurred and moved to tears from every side.

The **Tenth Stimulus** is the example of all the **Saints**: namely the **Apostles**, **Martyrs**, **Confessors**, **Virgins**, and **Matrons** <term>Matrons: historically, a category of female saints who were married or widowed, distinguished from virgins</term>, all of whom have wept bitterly over the death of the **Savior**. # Of Holy Meditation

...to be cruelly tormented. With you on the cross to be entirely reviled. With you on the cross to be crucified and dead. Through these and similar laments that the holy men and holy women <note>The original uses *santi* and *fante* to distinguish between male and female followers of Christ.</note> made for Christ, may we also be led to imitation and to their example—according to the grace shared with us—to follow suit and do as much as we can.

### The Eleventh Stimulus

The eleventh stimulus is the great weeping performed by that most sweet and chosen mother of Jesus, the Virgin Mary. Her great lamentation would be enough to soften even the fiercest lions. This most disconsolate and sorrowful mother, seeing her only son entirely stripped and naked, hanging on the cross between two thieves, all pale and with a gaunt face, with what weak speech she could manage, and with that hoarse and trembling voice granted to her, weeping, she said:

"Alas, how bitter is this present day! How mournful, how tearful,

<column-break/>

and sad is the present hour. Wretched me, now a widow <note>original: *viduella*; while Mary was the widow of Joseph, this term here emphasizes her spiritual and social desolation.</note>, at this moment forsaken and abandoned by all. How is it possible to live, seeing my only, most innocent son, fixed to the cross between wicked and villainous thieves? My son, light of my eyes! O my son, staff of my old age <term>senectu: a derivative of the Latin *senectus*, meaning old age</term>! O my son, the only hope <note>original: *spanza*, a variant of *speranza* or hope.</note> of my soul! O sweet refreshment for my embittered heart! How quickly I have lost you! O singular solace of my spirit! Where are the many joys that your mother received at your conception? Where is the announcing angel? Where is the greeting of 'full of grace'? <note>A reference to the Archangel Gabriel’s greeting at the Annunciation.</note> Alas, for now I am full of every sorrow. My noble lord, my noble son, my noble spouse <note>In mystical theology, Mary is occasionally referred to as the spouse of God or the Word.</note> is no longer with me. Because he has been cruelly taken from me. Cruelly has he been placed on the cross; cruelly has he been ranked with infamous thieves. Cruelly with the wicked..."

<summary>This page describes the Virgin Mary's intense grief as she witnesses the crucifixion, contrasting her current agony with the joy of the Annunciation.</summary>

### Part Three

He speaks to everyone and says: O man, see what I suffer for you; to you I cry, I who die for you; see the pains with which I am afflicted: see the nails with which I am pierced: there is no sorrow like that by which I am tortured: and though there is such great external pain: yet the internal pain is more intense: I find you to be such an ungrateful sheep <note>original: "O homo vide que pro te patior / ad te clamo qui pro te morior / vide penas quibus afficio: vide clauos quibus confodior: non est dolor sicut ille quo crucior: & cum sit tantus dolor exterior: intus tamen dolor est intensior: tam ingratum ouis te experior"</note>. Consider, O soul, says your sorrowful Lord: that which I suffer for you: there is no pain in the world as great as that which torments me: it is excessive and great: see the punishments given to me: see the nails that pierce me: To you I cry out: for your salvation <term>salvation|the Italian 'salute' refers to both spiritual health and the saving of the soul</term> I die. And even though my external pain is very great as you see: the internal pain is nevertheless much greater: when I recognize you as being so ungrateful and forgetful of such a great benefit. Contemplate, says your Lord: O rational creature: and see that not for my own, but for your sins was I so poorly treated: that in all parts of my body: I feel the greatest

<column-break/>

torment. Oh, how great is the torment of my pierced body: Oh, how cruel were the pricks of my thorns: Oh, how great and immense is the pain of my face: Oh, how great is the pain of my neck: Oh, how great is the pain of my eyes: Oh, what great pain I feel in my pierced hands: Oh, how much torment I feel in all my body: in which no part is found unhurt or healthy. Oh, what bitterness is in my tongue: drenched in vinegar and gall <term>gall|a bitter liquid, often associated with bile, mentioned in the Gospels as being offered to Jesus on the cross</term>. Oh, how many are my groans. Oh, how great is my compassion. Oh, how many are my internal sighs: when I see myself so abandoned by my dear disciples: when I see so many being damned <note>original: *dannarfi*; an archaic form of *dannarsi* (to be damned or lost).</note> without the fruit of my passion <term>passion|from the Latin 'passio' meaning suffering; refers specifically to the final hours of Christ’s life</term>: when I see my beloved mother pierced from one side to the other by the sword of sorrow <note>A reference to the prophecy of Simeon in the Gospel of Luke, predicting Mary's emotional pain.</note>: when I hear her weeping so bitterly and saying: O my son, where do you leave your poor, grieving mother: and me

# On thinking well.

...calling Maria. Oh, how many sharp arrows my heart feels from those maternal voices: the deep sighs of my mother <term>mother|the original 'genitrice' is a formal term for she who gives birth</term>: who bore me of the Holy Spirit: and sweetly fed me with celestial, miraculous milk. **O all you then who pass by along the way, heed and see if there is any sorrow like my sorrow.** <note>original: "O vos igitur omnes qui transitis per via attendite: & videte si est dolor sicut dolor meus." This is a famous quote from the biblical book of Lamentations 1:12, historically used in the "Tenebrae" services of Holy Week to represent Christ's suffering.</note> Oh, all you who pass along the way of this wretched world: pay attention: and see if there is found a single sorrow similar to my sorrow. Oh, you men and women who are sinners, behold where I have been led for the sake of your love. Oh, iron mind. Oh, hard breast. Oh, heart of diamond: your boundless ingratitude gives me greater pain: when I see that you do not remember so many of my benefits: and you feel no grief for my most bitter sorrows: and you value me so little, I who am your God and your savior: that you think little or nothing of me: and of my intolerable martyrdoms suffered for you: you never make a worthy and pious memory, so that even a single tear...

<column-break/>

...you do not care to shed out of compassion for me. Ah, my dear children, have compassion for me who suffers and dies for you. Ah, my beloved brothers, I exhort and pray you for your salvation <term>salvation|the Italian 'salute' refers to both physical health and the eternal saving of the soul</term> that you would weep and cry with me. For so many times, out of love and compassion for you, I have wept and cried. I cried immediately when I was born in the stable: I cried in the manger: I cried in the cradle: I cried at the circumcision: I cried in the desert when I stayed there for forty days without any human food: I cried at the raising of Lazarus: I cried on Palm Sunday: I cried at the separation from my dear mother: I cried at the [Last] Supper: I cried at the prayer <note>referring to the Agony in the Garden of Gethsemane</note>. Oh, how many times on the day of my passion did I cry for you. I cried when I was bound and cruelly taken: I cried when I was struck with a slap across the face: I cried in the house of Caiaphas <term>Caiaphas|the Jewish High Priest who presided over the trial of Jesus</term> while mocked and beaten: I cried, alas, at the column, all scourged—

### Part Three

...shed. I wept when I was crucified, because of the immeasurable pain that I felt then. And I always wept for you, and for your salvation <term>salvation|the Italian 'salute' implies both spiritual rescue and the soul's well-being</term>, and not for my own; for the sins you committed, and not for my own. Now then, ungrateful creature <term>creature|meaning a 'created being', used here to remind the soul of its relationship to the Creator</term>, why do you not have compassion for your creator? Why do you not deign to weep with your God? With your Lord? With your redeemer? Weep, oh sinful soul, for your creator who weeps for you: who

<column-break/>

suffers for you: who for you dies on the cross. Weep for him who is your benefactor, your savior. Weep for him who has loved you so much, that he did not refuse to die for you. If then you will shed but a single tear in weeping with your Savior; if you will wish to weep and suffer together with him; here in this life you shall be endowed with his grace, and in the future, you shall be endowed with his eternal glory.

<meta>This page continues the Second Rule of meditation, which focuses on the necessity of remembering the life and death of Jesus Christ. The author is explaining why mental contemplation is the primary tool for a Christian to access spiritual grace, now that Christ is no longer physically on Earth.</meta>

-> ### On Thinking Well ### <-

[Why] meditation is so useful and profitable to the Christian. I answer you: as it is contained in the book of <term>Natural Theology</term> <note>The *Theologia Naturalis* (c. 1436) by Raymond de Sabunde, a highly influential book that argued God could be known through both the "Book of Nature" and the "Book of Scripture."</note>: that in our sweet, loving Christ Jesus and in His precious and holy death consists all our <term>merit</term> <note>In Catholic theology, "merit" refers to the spiritual standing or "credit" gained through God's grace, which entitles the believer to a reward in heaven.</note>; therefore, whoever wishes to have all our merit and all our good must have Christ within themselves, and receive and keep Him with all their heart.

But because Christ Jesus has passed from this mortal life, while His holy merit lasts forever, and because that same Christ Jesus cannot be held within a man except through memory and contemplation—by which Christ remains with us in a certain way and is present to us—and similarly His harsh death (which, according to the Christian faith, is the first root of every reward and merit of ours) becomes present to us through such memory; and therefore, whoever has such memory of His death also possesses such reward and me-

<column-break/>

rit from Christ. And through such memory, a person makes the life and death of our Redeemer their own and unites it with themselves; and thus, through that memory, the person is vivified <note>Brought to life or spiritually quickened.</note> and made a participant in the merit of the life and death of that same Redeemer. And so, such thinking and such meditating is the entire foundation, the entire root, for being able to receive both merit and reward from that benign and loving Christ <note>original: "xp̃o", a common abbreviation using the Greek letters Chi and Rho.</note>. And therefore, where there is a greater, more intense, and more continuous memory of the life and death of our Savior, there is also found more virtue, more merit, and more reward.

Consider then how necessary and opportune this memory is, if such good is contained within it; speaking of which, the devout **Bernard** <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a mystic and doctor of the Church who emphasized deep, emotional meditation on the sufferings of Jesus.</note> said: that through fixed memory, the person is liberated from sins and restored almost to the state of innocence, and made perfect. Great merit is acquired in a short time, and through this, a person becomes patient, very constant in adversity, and finally proceeds with ease.

-> c. ij <-

...and in a short time become perfect: and acquire every good: and escape every evil: exercise yourself continually in this holy memory. <column-break/>

which He did in that humanity: which are all tangible works, serving as a ladder for ascending to the contemplation of that incomprehensible divinity. Wherefore Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential theologians in the Western Church.</note> says: that the Son of God, being an invisible spirit, took on human flesh and made Himself visible to our bodily eyes, so that through His precious flesh, He might finally lead us—who are carnal <note>In this context, "carnal" refers to being human and physical, rather than spiritual or abstract.</note>—back to God Himself. And with the easy beginning of this carnal and human meditation, He might make us ascend to the height of divine contemplation. Who is there, then, who cannot think upon and remember the sufferings of Christ: His fasting, His baptism, His hunger, His thirst, His Last Supper, His prayer in the garden, His being bound and captured, His being scourged, crucified, and killed? These are all mysteries that are tangible, sensible, and physical, and consequently easy for every person to think about. Just as easily as one holds in memo-

-> # On Good Thinking # <-

...histories and fables of the Pagans <note>original: "Gentili," referring to non-Christian or classical Greek and Roman myths.</note> without any fruit? Why then should this sacred history, on which our every good depends, not be kept in mind much more easily? Truly, I see no excuse for it: for either a little or a lot can be meditated and thought upon by everyone. Therefore, I exhort everyone to begin this so useful, necessary, and easy <term>exercise</term> <note>A disciplined spiritual practice designed to focus the mind on God.</note>, promising them that it will always seem more delightful and easier the further they progress.

The third rule is that, for your part, you make the proper preparation as far as you are able, so that you can sufficiently perform this exercise. And this preparation consists not only of humility, <term>contrition</term> <note>A state of feeling sincere remorse and penitence for one's sins.</note>, and prayer (as was said above), but also in other virtues and other things, such as choosing the right time, place, and manner, and in clothing oneself in certain specific virtues that are especially well-proportioned to this exercise.

<column-break/>

Thus, you must flee as much as possible from all the pleasures of the flesh to which you are not obligated, and all the pomp of banquets and finery, following a modest abstinence in food and discreet attire, using simple food and humble clothing. This is so that, with the Apostle Paul, you may truly say: *The world is crucified to me, and I to the world* <note>original: "Mihi mūdus crucifixus est: & ego mundo." A quote from Galatians 6:14 expressing total devotion to God over worldly matters.</note>.

He who wishes to feel the suffering of Christ within himself through experience and taste must strive to resemble Him in human life as much as his status allows, and for this reason, flee all worldly consolations and every immoderate game and laughter. Therefore, Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a prominent medieval mystic and monk known for his devotion to the humanity of Christ.</note> says: *The cross of Christ is contrary to every carnal or worldly pleasure; and similarly, every worldly and carnal pleasure is firmly an enemy to that Cross.* And then the Apostle said: *But God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, by whom the world is crucified unto me, and I unto the world* <note>original: "Mihi aut̃ absit gliari nisi in cruce dñi nři Jesu xpi: p quē mihi mundus crucifixus est: & ego mūdo." Another reference to Galatians 6:14, emphasizing that the believer's only pride should be in Christ's sacrifice.</note>. And Bernard confirms—

Then, at the time of meditation, do not take them all together, but one by one with deliberation <note>original: "morosita," meaning a slow, lingering pace.</note> and a space of time, "chewing" them well. Think in the manner we will describe below, always making the Lord present to you with your imagination, and imagining every thing no differently than if it were actually being done in your presence.

divinity. ¶ We can therefore say that there are seven principal ends with which one can legitimately and fruitfully commemorate this holy Passion. The first end: is to fulfill the commandment of the Lord, who invites us to this most holy memory when he says:

> "Do not forget the grace of your guarantor" <note>original: "Gratiam fideiufforis tui ne obliuiscaris"; from Sirach 29:19, here meaning the "grace" of Christ who paid the debt of humanity.</note>
and from this you will acquire a greater store of every good. The fifth end: is that by considering the wondrous works of the Lord we are led to a great admiration. Wherefore David says in the Psalm:
> "And I will meditate on your wondrous works" <note>original: "Et exercebor in mirabilibus tuis"; from Psalm 119:27.</note>
And the fruit of this is that often there follows an <term>alienation</term> <note>In this context, a mystical state of ecstasy where the soul is "alienated" or detached from physical senses to focus entirely on God.</note> from the senses and a great wonder in the human mind. is: the fruit, then, of this last principal end is nothing other than to grow in perfect charity and union with the Lord God. Therefore, every article and part of the Passion can be meditated upon with these seven ends, seven intentions, and the aforementioned fruits. The **sixth rule** is called **meditative commemoration**, of which it is written in Saint Matthew, the twenty-sixth chapter:
> "Do this in remembrance of me." <note>original: "Hoc facite in meam cōmemorationem"; while the author attributes this to Matthew 26 (the chapter covering the Last Supper), the specific phrasing is most famously found in Luke 22:19 and 1 Corinthians 11:24.</note>

And this commemoration must be done not in a rush, but with delay and lingering. Therefore, it is necessary first to read or hear this sacred history of the Passion and store it in your memory; then, at the appropriate times when you wish to commemorate it, you should reflect upon it and treat it with your mind. It is not necessary to try to rethink the whole thing at once; it is enough to **ruminate** and **chew** <note>The author uses the metaphor of digestion (ruminating) to describe deep, slow spiritual reflection.</note> on one, two, or three <term>articles</term> <note>Specific points or episodes within the narrative of the Passion.</note> at a time, with whatever taste and feeling is possible for you. ### Part Four ###

See then with how much diligence this commemoration must be made, because it is commanded in **Deuteronomy**: 
> Take care
> diligently: lest at any time you
> forget the Lord your God <note>original: "caue diligenter: ne aliquando obliuiscaris Domini dei tui"</note>; 
and in the **Book of Tobit** it is said: 
> At all times
> have God in your
> mind <note>original: "Omni tempore in mente habeto deum"</note>.

**The Seventh Rule** is called **compassion**: because wishing, beyond simple commemoration, to still achieve greater fruit and merit, we must with every art and effort provoke and excite compassion within ourselves. This is the "taste" through which we are moved by compassion to pious tears and deep sighs for the Lord. This compassion is very pleasing to our **Redeemer**, and therefore he wishes to lead us to it with insistence when he proposes to us that saying of **Jeremiah**: 
> O all you
> who pass by the way: at-
> tend and see: if there is any
> sorrow like my sorrow <note>original: "O vos omnes qui transitis per viam: attendite ɀ videte: si est dolor: sicut dolor me⁹"</note>. 
And he greatly laments those who have no compassion for him, when

<column-break/>

he says: 
> I waited for someone
> who would grieve with me and there was no one: and

> found no one <note>original: "sustinui qui simul contristaretur ɀ non fuit ɀ qui consolaretur ɀ non inueni"</note>. 
Wishing, then, in our commemoration, to feel compassion for the Lord, and to excite and move ourselves to weep for his bitter **Passion**, one must consider seven things:
1. First, the **suffering person**: that is, who is the one suffering?
2. Second, the **pains**: and the torments which he suffers.
3. Third, the **persons** from whom he suffers.
4. Fourth, consider **for whom** he suffers.
5. Fifth, consider the **manner** of the suffering.
6. Sixth, the **place** where he suffers.
7. Seventh, the **time** in which he suffers.

First, then, having simply commemorated according to the history <note>Referring to the factual narrative of the Gospels.</note> one **Article** <term>Article</term> <note>A specific episode or "station" of the Passion story, such as the Flagellation or the Crucifixion.</note> or more of the Passion—for example, the article and the moment of the **Crucifixion**—and having thought upon that with some delay and made it present to the imagination as much as possible, imagining it as if you were present

## The Beauty of Good Thinking.

...true God, creator of heaven and earth: most powerful, most just, most wise; he who for your sake took on **human flesh** and became obedient even unto death. Wherefore **Saint Paul** says:

> Who, although he was in the form of God, did not consider it a thing to be grasped to be equal to God. And yet he emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, even unto the death of the cross. <note>original Latin: "Qui cum in forma dei esset: nõ rapinam arbitratus est esse: se equalem deo. Et tamen exinanivit semetipsũ: formã servi accipiens usqꝫ ad morte3 crucis." This passage is from Philippians 2:6-8.</note>

It is a great sorrow when a lowly peasant is placed on a cross; it is greater when it is a citizen, because of the nobility of his nature and his **complexion** <term>complexion: referring to the "complexio," the medical balance of bodily humors that determined a person's physical and mental sensitivity.</term>; but it is much greater still when he surpasses every other nature in nobility and tenderness—as did the true God and a man more than most sweet and most holy.

Secondly, consider the **quality of the torments** along with the circumstances that are cruelly inflicted upon your Lord. And you will find them to be most bitter and most cruel. For they were throughout his whole precious body, which had no part that was not entirely 

<column-break/>

tormented. <margin>From the sole of the foot even unto the top of the head, there was no soundness in him. <note>original Latin: "A planta pedis usqꝫ ad verticem capitis: non fuit in eo sanitas." This is a reference to Isaiah 1:6.</note></margin> Attend also generally to his **senses**: that is, in seeing, in hearing, in smelling, in tasting, and in touching.

O, how great was his pain in seeing his beloved mother so sorrowful, and his dear disciples all dispersed and so afflicted. O, how much pain it was for him to hear the groans and the weeping of those devout women. O, what great affliction the stench of those foul corpses gave to his sense of smell, which were in that place punished by justice <note>Refers to Golgotha, the "Place of the Skull," traditionally associated with the execution and remains of criminals.</note>.

O, how great was the pain of touch in the parts of his body, and especially in the most sensitive parts such as the hands and feet, which more than the others were wounded and pierced through with nails. O, how much bitterness his sense of taste felt in drinking the gall and the vinegar. His pain was also universal because he was most shamefully stripped naked in the sight of the people and of his

For this reason, so that he might suffer more, the mitigation of his present sorrows was removed from the higher part of his soul. <note>The author refers to a common medieval theological idea that Christ, in his divinity, chose to "block" the comfort of the Beatific Vision from his human consciousness during the Passion to ensure he felt the full weight of suffering.</note> And therefore he says through the prophet:

How shameful and disgraceful was your passion and death: placed on the gallows of evildoers between two ignominious thieves, fixed to the cross by the crucifiers, mocked by those standing around, reviled and rejected by every generation of people. Alas, who is he who is not moved by so many of his sorrows? Who is he who can restrain himself from tears? Seeing the man without sin suffer so much, contemplating the author of nature so debased.

# On Thinking Well.

[abandon] your Lord, harshly afflicted and tormented. For you will find that from his compatriots and from those upon whom he had conferred great benefits, he received reproaches, insults, injuries, and pain. Oh, how great is the grief when you see yourself treated so cruelly by your own! The Lord was persecuted by the religious and the learned, who had a reputation for holiness and doctrine (such as the scribes and pharisees). Certainly, it is a harder thing to receive persecution from such people than from laypeople or others who are considered to be without doctrine and religion. Grief increases marvelously when those you have benefited insult you. Thus this Jewish people was very ungrateful and cruel to its Lord. For, beyond the general benefits, he honored them with many beautiful privileges and gifts, choosing them as his own peculiar people to whom alone he gave the knowledge of the true God. But

<column-break/>

giving them many great prophets who spoke with the Lord God, promising them that from their nation would be born the Messiah, Savior of the universe. which was in a way <note>original Latin: *quodammodo*</note> without measure due to the wicked and cruel manner maintained by the Jews: the manner then was with an unjust trial: And without any just cause: and thus as the Poet <note>The "Poet" refers to Ovid, a classical Roman author often quoted in medieval and Renaissance texts to provide moral weight.</note> says:

### Part Four

...you can call yourself the most unhappy of all men. <gloss>ʘ</gloss> Do you not know how much the Lord seeks from you this compassion, which He values so highly? Speaking to Saint <term>Mechtilde</term><note>Mechtilde of Hackeborn (1241–1298) was a Saxon Benedictine nun and mystic known for her "Book of Special Grace," which recorded her visions of Christ.</note>, He said: "Know that the tears shed for my **Passion** delight me so much and are so pleasing to me, that every time someone devotedly sheds even a single tear for it, it pleases me so much, and I receive it just as if that person had suffered for me." Recognizing the necessity of this **Seventh Rule** and its great usefulness, it seemed to me that I should be more detailed in it than in others, even though I have intentionally left out many other emotional and moving reasons. Now, in the following rules, we will be more concise, following brevity as much as we are able and know how.
The **Eighth Rule** is called **Imitation**; for in it is contained the doctrine by which we are instructed and led to follow and imitate the most holy virtues of our Savior,

<column-break/>

who suffered bitter pains and a cruel death to give us an example of virtue, so that we might have the method and the way to imitate Him for the salvation of our souls. And for this reason, He lovingly invited us, saying in the **Gospel**:

> If anyone wishes to come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me. <note>original Latin: "Si quis vult venire post me abneget semetipsum: et tollat crucem suam et sequatur me." From Matthew 16:24.</note>

And know that this **meditation** and memory of His death would not please His Divine Majesty if it were not accompanied by our continuous desire to imitate Him, as far as it is possible for us. Therefore, you must, devout soul, contemplate this **Passion** also for this purpose: so that you may imitate and follow the examples of your Savior. And this should not seem strange to you at all; rather, it is supremely glorious and appropriate, because His death and Passion would be of no use to a Christian without **imitation**, and without imitation, it can never save anyone. And if you should ask me in what way...

### The ninth rule: regarding the giving of thanks.
Because it teaches us to thank our benign Lord. For this benefit: and for every other gift of His granted to us:

<column-break/>

This commemoration and meditation of the passion would not please the Lord: if it were not done with gratitude of soul: with the offering of thanks. Therefore the pious contemplator should never depart from his meditation: until he has thanked the Lord with burning love for the stages commemorated: and in exchange for those, made his benign, humble offerings to Him. To this Saint Paul encourages us, saying: >Give thanks in all things through Jesus Christ, etc.< <note>original Latin: "In omnibus gratias agite per Jesum Christum &c." Referencing Sirach 29:15.</note> Thank then your Lord who has sustained so many hard torments and sharp pains for your salvation: and who has proposed so many examples of virtue to you: and who has granted you the taste: and feeling of His passion with the desire to imitate Him. And because you are not sufficient of yourself to be able to thank and praise Him for so many gifts: [it is] enough <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("ba..." for "basta") at the end of the page.</note>

# Part Four

...true God and man. Wherefore the Psalm says: *Thy testimonies are wonderful: therefore my soul has searched them out* <note>original Latin: "Mirabilia testimonia tua: ideo scrutata est anima mea." From Psalm 119:129.</note>. And in another place, the same author says: *I will meditate on your wonders* <note>original Latin: "Et exercebor in mirabilibus tuis." From Psalm 119:27.</note>. It is wonderfully beneficial to the contemplative soul to consider with admiration the stupendous works of the Lord. And because the mysteries of His sacred passion are so worthy of awe and admiration—indeed, as **Pope Saint Leo** <note>Leo the Great (c. 400–461), a Doctor of the Church known for his influential writings on the nature of Christ.</note> says: *Nothing is more wonderful, and among all the works of God's mercy which from the beginning were bestowed for our salvation, nothing is found more sublime or more astounding than the sacrament of the Lord's Passion.* <note>original Latin: "Nihil mirabilius. Nihilqʒ inter omnia opera misericordie dei que ab initio saluti nostre sunt impensa reperitur sublimius: & stupendius: sacramento dominice passionis."</note> 

And another learned theologian says even more: that the Lord never performed a more wonderful or more astounding work than that of His passion and death upon the cross, nor in all eternity will a greater, more admirable, or more unusual deed ever be done. Into this wonder will that person easily be led who, with attention, wishes...

<column-break/>

...to consider the immensity of the sufferer: who is <unclear>for</unclear> infinite good, infinite power, infinite virtue, and infinite glory, infinite wisdom; the greatness of the martyrdom and tortures received; and the obstinate and abominable iniquity and malice of those who afflicted Him; and the incredible ingratitude, ignorance, and baseness of the human rational creature, for whom alone He wished to receive so many hardships for their salvation. 

Who is there that would not be completely stunned by such pains, when thinking that the highest God has suffered such hard pains for us, who are almost a "highest evil" <note>This refers to the theological concept of *Summum Malum*, the total deprivation of good found in sin, contrasted here with God as the *Summum Bonum* (Highest Good).</note>? That the most powerful should be conquered by the lowliest death? That He who creates wine and bread suffers hunger and thirst? And He who turns the high heavens endures heat and cold? And that He who returns life to the deceased should be slain by such a wonderfully vile and ignominious death? Oh, how stupendous this thing is: to see immortal Life die, and infinite Power suffer...

### On Thinking Well 46

...ct: infinite glory: to receive ignominy: Oh, how great is He who suffers? Oh, how powerful is the one dying? Oh, how weak is the one killing? Oh, infinite charity of the creator. Oh, infinite ingratitude of the creature. Who does not wonder? Who is not amazed? When man sins wickedly, and the creator suffers humbly? Man becomes proud and disobedient: but the Lord of the world becomes humble: and even unto the cross always obedient. And therefore a saint exclaims, saying: *Oh newest and highest! Oh humble and sublime! Oh reproach of men and glory of angels!* <note>original Latin: "O nouissimum: & altissimum. O humilem & sublimē. O opprobrium hominum: & gloriam angelorum." The author uses these opposites to highlight the dual nature of Christ as both God and a suffering man.</note> No one is more sublime than Christ. And no one is more humble than Him. No one more glorious than Him: no one more ignominious than Him. No one more happy and blessed than Him: no one more tortured and tormented than Him. Who is he who ever saw such a thing? Who can say they ever heard such a thing? A similar thing was never

<column-break/>

thought of? A thing more horrendous and more abominable was never found? That the impious servant with his wicked and villainous hands: his Lord: who is infinite virtue: has so cruelly wounded: so painfully treated and crucified. Oh cruelty. Oh piety. Oh greatness. Oh smallness. Oh charity. Oh impiety. Oh excessive good. Oh excessive evil. Oh supreme glory. Oh supreme ignominy. Oh supreme virtue. Oh supreme weakness. <term>weakness</term> <note>original: "imbecillita." In this historical context, this refers to physical frailty or the state of being vulnerable, rather than a lack of intelligence.</note> Oh example of infinite mercy. Oh example of infinite cruelty. Oh example of supreme virtue. Oh example of supreme iniquity: the vile creature kills the creator: The darkness obscures the infinite light. Attend, oh you who are more contemplative, to these unheard-of things. Take heed, oh you human people, to these tidings, never more heard. Marvel, oh heavens: be amazed, oh angels of paradise: do not cease to be astonished, oh earth with all the elements. Consider, all of you, your creator: who hangs dead upon the cross.

### Part Four

...every good.

**The fifth truth** manifested by the passion of Christ is that hell is horrible: because it is full of filth, misery, pain, and calamity.

**The sixth truth**, etc., is that virtue is supremely praiseworthy: because it is attractive, beautiful, precious, and fruitful.

**The seventh truth**, etc., is that sin is very abominable: because to wish to purge it, one needs a great price, a great sacrifice, and a very difficult medicine. If you desire to have greater knowledge of the seven aforementioned truths manifested through the passion of the Savior, go read the teachings of the <term>seraphic</term> <note>original: "seraphico." A title specifically associated with St. Bonaventure within the Franciscan tradition, implying fiery love and angelic wisdom.</note> **Saint Bonaventure**; there you will be abundantly satisfied. Here I have thought it best to pass over them briefly because it is enough for us to know by how many beautiful truths we have been illuminated through the power of the Savior’s passion. And therefore, together with the Church, we can all joyfully say: <note>original Latin: "Nos autem gloriari oportet: in cruce dñi nři Jesu Christi: i quo est salus: vita: & resurrectō nostra per quem saluati: & liberati sumus."</note> "But it behooves us

<column-break/>

to glory in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, in whom is our salvation, life, and resurrection; through whom we have been saved and set free." love: and by his own spontaneous will. Therefore, although in all these three ways our loving God seeks to draw man to his love, nonetheless the third way—in this sacred history of death and passion—has been practiced by our loving Lord most perfectly, more than the other ways. Showing in all the parts and points of it that he burns and blazes with love toward his beloved and cherished human creature, and desires nothing other than the good, the utility, and the glory of said creature. And he makes them understand further that he wanted nothing else from them except sweet and gentle love. He craves nothing else, nor does he seek anything else in his many bitter pains, except that he be much cherished and loved by them for their own benefit. This is testified in Deuteronomy, in the fourth chapter <note>Though the text cites the fourth chapter, this quote is a variation of Deuteronomy 10:12.</note>, where, after many and numbered benefits, he adds, saying:

innumerable gifts that the Lord has granted you; who, therefore, requires nothing else from you, except that you love him and wish him well. The first condition: that the older a love is, the more perfect it is. But the Lord has loved you from eternity <note>original: "ab eterno," a theological term meaning before time began.</note> and therefore, this love of his cannot be older nor more perfect. Wherefore Jeremiah says:

# Part four
a

->on love.<-

The second condition of the Lord’s love toward the human creature is that he has loved us not for any reward or personal utility received from us, or that he hoped to receive, nor for any merit of ours: but freely and benignly: solely through his own nobility and spontaneous will. He chose to offer us such free and generous charity as he has done.
The third condition is that his love has not only been eternal and free, but it has been most useful to us. Indeed, innumerable benefits and fruits have come to us from his entire holy life, and especially from his sacred Passion and death. Oh, how wretched and unhappy we would be if such great charity had not been shown to us by our dear Lord.
The fourth condition is that he has loved us with a unique and special love. Therefore, he would have died and suffered as much for a single

<column-break/>

soul as for infinite souls. And even now, if it were necessary, he would suffer and die for the health and redemption of a single soul. Oh, charity of the Lord, how great you are! Oh, love, how unique and particular you have been to me, for whom alone, if it were necessary, you are still prepared to receive so many insults and a harsh death.
The fifth condition is that he loved us with a dutiful and fitting love: because he did not look to himself, but held all his regard primarily for the Father’s honor. And this love was discerning <term>discreet: derived from the Latin *discretio*, meaning the ability to distinguish or separate. Here, it refers to God's ability to separate the sinner from the sin.</term>, loving human nature while hating the fault; likewise, he loved us with a sweet and wise love, as Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a French abbot and a major figure in the revitalization of French monasticism.</note> says: "finally, I love human nature" <note>original: "tandem amo la natura humana"</note>, to reconcile it with his angry heavenly Father, making satisfaction for the many offenses committed against his honor.
The sixth condition was that he loved us with love

# Part four

->On thinking well.<-

...love <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "wise love."</note> that was neither mediocre nor small. ❡ But it was most intense and most vehement, giving us as many signs of this as could possibly be given in every way.

**T**he seventh condition was that He loved us perseveringly and constantly: never through labors, nor sweats, nor pains, nor torments, however grave and bitter they were, did He draw back; rather, the further He went, the more clear signs of love He showed. Wherefore He, speaking thus to His so beloved human creature, can say: O man, if you desire ❡ to see my great, excessive, and persevering love toward you, consider the works that I have done for you. Behold the bloody sweats <note>A reference to the Agony in the Garden of Gethsemane.</note>. Behold the pains and the scourges. First, I came from the bosom of my eternal Father from heaven to earth, and with you, as your sweet lover, seeking only your salvation, I lived for thirty-three years. And always as your lov-

<column-break/>

-ing servant in every act of mine I served you, so much so that at the last, for your love, for thirty denarii <term>denarii: an ancient Roman silver coin; here referring to the "thirty pieces of silver" for which Judas Iscariot betrayed Jesus.</term>, I allowed myself to be sold like a vile thing. And I granted my precious mouth to the wicked traitor in the false kiss <note>original: "osculo falso"; refers to the Kiss of Judas.</note>.
❡ O man: look at what I have done for you: my ears, accustomed to hearing the angelic songs, alas, how many calumnies and false testimonies and blasphemies they heard for you; with how much shame I was bound like a thief and presented to those wicked judges. Defamed by so many false accusations: how many times was I cruelly beaten and scourged for you. How many times ignominiously stripped of my clothes and dressed again. How many times for you, O my dear soul, was I all bathed in blood. How many were the insults; how many the beatings. How cruelly my precious head was crowned with piercing thorns: behold how many signs of love I have shown you; and if you are still not content, see...

# Part four

...my weak shoulders to carry that great and heavy wood <note>The Cross.</note> for you. See me with my beloved mother: falling to the ground like a failing creature. And if it still does not seem to you that I have done great things for you, come to Mount **Calvary** <note>original: "Laluario", a variant of Caluario/Calvario.</note>: and there you will see me like a wicked and wretched thief: fixed on the cross with cruel nails, and cruelly wounded with five most cruel **wounds** <note>The "Five Holy Wounds" received during the Crucifixion.</note> for you. And with my excessive torment, I was raised into the air. And because no greater sign of love can be shown than giving away one's goods, fame, honor, friends, and relatives—and even more, for a friend to give one's own life—see me with a strong and constant love, having fought manfully for you until the very end, and having willingly given my own life. And if I had had a thousand souls and a thousand

<column-break/>

lives, so great was the love that I bore for you, that for that love I would have most willingly laid them all down. Like one drunk with love, I left everything for you; as if forgotten of myself and my own, I gave everything of mine to you. And then, when I knew not what else to do, I offered myself to you as food and drink <note>A reference to the Institution of the Eucharist at the Last Supper.</note>. What greater flame and fire of love can you see? If, therefore, you are not induced and moved to love me in return by these great signs, without doubt you are harder than a lion, more cruel than a tiger, and more obstinate than any man. See, my pious reader, how in this sacred history of the Passion of the Lord you find so many signs of **delight** <term>dilettione: a formal term for spiritual love or affection.</term> and **charity** <term>carita: the highest form of love; the theological virtue of selfless love for God and neighbor.</term> that you cannot find more or greater in any other history. Therefore, if you desire to arrive easily and quickly at this loving union with your Lord, to become perfect in charity, and to feel your heart burn and glow, you can do no better in every way than to exercise yourself in this **meditation**. And know...

->g ij<-

...know that this love is the end and goal of all this exercise. For your commemorating and thinking, your desire to have **compassion** <term>compassione: literally "suffering with"; the act of sharing in the emotional and physical pain of Christ.</term> and tears for the Lord; your desire to imitate the life and ways of your Savior; your desire to thank Him; and your desire to exult and admire this holy passion and death—all this must not be done for any other effect except to acquire this greatness of love and this perfection of charity, this loving union and **melting** <note>original: "liquefattione"; a mystical term for the heart becoming soft and fluid under the heat of divine love.</note> of the heart toward your sweetest and most merciful **Redeemer** <term>Redentore: Redeemer; a title for Jesus Christ referring to His "buying back" of humanity from sin.</term>. In this love, as I have said, consists our every good. For in this life and the next, it leads the creature to such a high and sublime state and degree that it transforms the creature into the Creator with such excellence that the creature, so united and transformed, can in truth be called not only a child of God, but God himself. Oh, most wonderful dignity! Oh, unthinkable excellence! Oh, un-

<column-break/>

-heard-of reward! Who would have ever thought this? That remembering the aforementioned Passion, with these **twelve golden rules** <note>A specific meditative framework provided by the author to guide the reader through the Passion.</note> that I have described to you, would contain within itself such fruit and such good. I say, therefore, that with this way of meditating, which I have taught you with these twelve newly ordered rules, you will be able to come to such dignity and height that you may be called god. For as the apostle Paul well says to the Corinthians: **He who joins himself to God is one spirit with Him** <note>original: "Qui adheret deo: vnus spiritus est cum eo"; 1 Corinthians 6:17.</note>. Whoever approaches God and unites themselves to Him through love becomes one spirit with Him. Therefore (as the **holy doctors** <note>The "Doctors of the Church," influential theologians and saints whose writings carry great authority.</note> infer), one becomes one and the same god with Him. And through the evangelist John, the Savior said, praying to His Father: **I pray that they may be one, just as we are** <note>original: "Rogo vt vnus sint sicut & nos"; John 17:11.</note>; and then again: **You, Father, are in me, and I in you, that they also may be in us** <note>original: "Tu pater in me: & ego in te: vt ipsi in nobis sint"; John 17:21.</note>. If you wish then to be one and the same thing with the Father and with the Son, think on these...

### Part Four

...this blessed passion, in which you will find the ardent fire of love: through it, you will be transformed entirely into God by love, and you will have become a god on earth in a true sense <note>original: "a buō sentimento"; meaning with a right spiritual understanding or through actual mystical experience.</note>. Therefore, I mean every word of ours: what greater thing could I have told you? What greater utility could I have expressed to you? To what greater degree, nobility, and height could I have led you by means of this meditation? Do not lose, therefore, such great good: begin this worthy and most excellent art. Follow with all your effort this holy remembering and pondering the death of your Lord; for if you persevere in it according to the ways and paths taught in these **twelve rules**, you will more easily reach the desired end of this **Christian perfection** <note>A state of spiritual maturity where the soul is fully aligned with God's will.</note>, and you will arrive at this loving union and transformation. This path is more effective than if you had performed any other great austerity or penance of the **hair shirt** <term>cilitio: a garment made of rough animal hair worn against the skin to cause discomfort as a form of religious penance.</term>, of fasting, of abstinence, or of pilgrimages. And when you, devout soul,

<column-break/>

shall have arrived at this sublimity, and shall have surpassed every human dignity and excellence—being amorously and entirely united with your Lord—you will feel within yourself, according to the measure of your love, the divine properties and conditions. These include a true wisdom and a true light, a true peace and a true heartfelt quiet, a true way of praying, a true way of loving, a true knowing, a true understanding, a true enduring, a true humbling of yourself, a true believing, and a true knowledge. And finally, while living on earth, in the midst of anxieties you will always be consoled. In the midst of darkness you will be entirely illuminated. In the midst of ice you will be entirely set on fire and inflamed. Then, with a loving and sweet heart, you will say to the Lord:

...so late, oh beloved: and my dear love. || **Through the streets and the squares I will seek him whom my soul loves** <note>original: "Per vias: et plateas queram: quem diligit anima mea." And by these and similar loving words, nothing else is signified but the great love of God and the great desire for death held by the person who has arrived at this high degree of loving union.

You should know, my devoted reader, that in each of the aforementioned articles: first I will set down the pure text of the Gospel; then upon that, I will describe the meditation or contemplation, according to what divine grace and goodness will grant me. But before I give a beginning to such a compassionate and sorrowful history, I invite you, O my dear reader, to prepare yourself and

<column-break/>

so dispose yourself that you may draw the proper fruit from the said contemplations. I also exhort you to awaken yourself in this act more than in any other worldly exercise: calling all your soul, all your heart, all your senses, and all your limbs to grieve and make bitter lament for your Lord, who has endured so many harsh sorrows and so many hard pains for you, etc. You shall say, therefore, thus: "O my soul, awaken now, and delay no longer. Let us go to contemplate and remember those inestimable languors and those excessive sorrows. Why are you not moved, O my hardened heart? Why do you not weep, O my eyes? Why do you not lament such great injustice done to your innocent Savior? O my mute tongue, why are you silent? Why do you not speak? Why have you become silent in this so horrible a case? Why do you not cry out with a loud voice together with Isaiah, saying: 'Surely he has borne our infirmities and carried our sorrows'?" <note>original: "Vere languores nostros ipse tulit: & dolores nostros ipse [portavit]" from Isaiah 53:4.</note>

# Good Pious Thinking 55

...salem, and thus remaining in the temple, he preached until evening and performed many miracles, healing the blind and the lame. But in the evening, as he had not been invited to dinner by anyone, he returned with his disciples to Bethany. And as some believe, he ate nothing that Sunday evening, but remained entirely occupied in teaching and instructing his disciples about the kingdom of Heaven.

Whereupon, very early the following Monday morning, while returning to Jerusalem along the road—which was a distance of two miles—the Savior felt hunger. And seeing a fig tree <note>original: "piede de fico"</note> full of leaves but without fruit, he cursed it and made it wither immediately. Having entered the city of Jerusalem and then the temple, all filled with fervor for human salvation, he preached extensively to the crowd. Then in the evening, he returned to his usual dwelling in Bethany, to the house of Martha and Mary Magdalene.

disputes—seeing him grow in fame and reputation, both for his admirable doctrine and for the stupendous love he demonstrated in these last days more than in all the time past, as well as for the great signs he performed and the harsh rebukes he uttered against them, [sought to destroy him].

Up to this point, I have briefly recited the text of the Gospel for you; now, I will immediately add the contemplation regarding the histories narrated, and especially concerning the wicked sale of the Savior.

### First Meditation or Contemplation on the wicked sale of the Savior and other things contained in the Gospel text cited above.

Come with me, O devout Christians: let us go to the tomb of Lazarus in Bethany. And then we will go to Jerusalem and return again to Bethany, to the house of our dear Mary Magdalene. Let us consider all the loving gestures and the honeyed words of our pious Jesus, who is all burning with love. When I raised him from death to life, I said: *O poor Jesus, how soon you will be grieving, when through the violence of martyrdom you will exchange your life for death.* When I said that they should let him go wherever he pleased, I thought that they would not do the same for me; but rather the complete opposite, namely: *bind him tightly, and see that he does not escape you.* At that time, I saw myself among my dear friends, most cordially cherished by them. # On Meditating Well

...a joyful procession: I was first constrained to weep when my dear disciples served me with such great joy and love, stripping off their own clothes and decorating those lowly animals, the she-ass and the young foal, with them. I thought that soon they would abandon me with great sorrow, and I would be taken from them, naked and poor. And just as the Jewish people bestowed six great honors upon me in that memorable triumphant procession, so I knew that soon they would inflict six great insults, the complete opposite, upon me. For on Palm Sunday I rode the young ass with great celebration; but on Friday <note>original: "el Venere," referring to Friday.</note> with great shame they made me "ride" the hard wood of the cross. In the first procession, they stripped off their own garments to spread them on the ground and honor me; but in the second, which was Holy Friday <note>original: "el Venere santo," the traditional term for Good Friday.</note>, they shamefully stripped me, depriving me of all my honored garments. In the first, with

<column-break/>

branches of palms and olives and other trees, they came to meet me with great celebration and honor; but in the second, they pierced my delicate head with the cruelest and sharpest thorns. In the first, they confessed me as their King and Lord with great joy. In the second, saying, "We have no king but Caesar" <note>original Latin: "regem non habemus nisi Cesarem"</note>, they denied it entirely. In the first, they adored me as their Savior when they said, "Hosanna to the son of David" <note>original Latin: "Osanna filio David"</note>, which means: *Save us, we pray, O son of David.* In the second, they said the opposite; they denied this when, with a loud voice and to my great dishonor, they said: "He saved others; himself he cannot save" <note>original Latin: "Alios salvos fecit: se ipsum non potest salvum facere"</note>. In the first, they blessed me, saying: "Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord," etc. <note>original Latin: "Benedictus qui venit in nomine domini zce."</note>. In the second, to my great shame, they cursed me with blasphemies, calling me a seducer, a deceiver, a wine-drinker, one possessed by a demon, and an evildoer. Therefore, for all these reasons, I wept on that day...

Thus I preached in the temple all day, which was Monday, performing signs and miracles, consoling the afflicted, and healing the sick with excessive love, calling all sinners to penance and to mercy. Not being invited by anyone—entirely famished and weary—I returned to Bethany; with my devoted and beloved family, I ate a light supper. # On Pious Meditation
-> b ij <-

conceived and born of the Virgin by the Holy Spirit: And by whom was He certainly sold through betrayal? By one of His treacherous disciples: To whom He had shown great signs of love: making him His intimate Apostle <note>The text emphasizes Judas's status as a "familiar" or close companion to heighten the sense of betrayal.</note>. And giving him the governance of His noble apostolic family, making him a preacher: Giving him the grace of performing miracles. Embracing him sweetly many times. Revealing to him His high secrets: making him a partaker of His heavenly and salvific doctrine. Putting all His money <note>original: "peculio," referring to the common purse or small savings of the group.</note> into his hands: and making him the dispenser of all the alms that were given to Him and to His disciples. Nor did He ever wish to grieve him in anything: O Judas, then, how abominable has your sin been: And to whom did you sell this gracious Master of yours? Certainly to those who were all contaminated by deadly and poisonous hatred against Him: To those who so cruelly thirsted for His precious blood:

<column-break/>

and His virginal flesh so that they could torture it in their own way: for they would have promised and given him not only thirty denarii <note>original: "dinari."</note>: but thousands of ducats <note>The author uses "ducats," a contemporary currency of the Renaissance, to help the reader grasp the scale of the bribe.</note>. Therefore, because on this day of Wednesday <note>This refers to "Spy Wednesday," the day Judas is traditionally said to have conspired with the Sanhedrin.</note> our most merciful Lord was so shamefully and iniquitously sold: so as not to be entirely ungrateful for such a great benefit, this day will always be celebrated by us with a special memory as a day of weeping and lamentation: in which a more specific beginning was given to our redemption, etc.

### Meditation on the harsh departure the pious Jesus made from his beloved mother: and from Martha, Mary Magdalene, and his other Devout daughters.

I feel my heart fail with pain: I feel my afflicted soul entirely languish. It finds no rest: it tastes no repose: my whole spirit is mournful: bitterly I weep and sigh: out of compassion for my sweet love Jesus: and for my sweet, loving mother: left in that bitter & harsh parting. In Bethany <note>original: "Bethania"</note> in the house of Magdalene, she fainted from immense sorrow: O faithful soul: what are you doing? O devout contemplators, why do you not run? O my beloved daughters: come with me: & let us help this so miserable mother <note>original: "meſchina madre," a term of deep pathos in early modern Italian, suggesting a state of pitiable suffering</note>: let us succor her piously with our loving services <note>original: "obſequy," referring to attentive care or rites of devotion</note>. Reverently holding her virginal arms. Loosening her chaste

<column-break/>

and modest breast. Comforting her with some life-giving sprinkled liquid <note>likely water or a restorative floral essence intended to revive her from a faint</note>. Drying her icy, cold sweat. For once she has returned to herself, we shall ask her what sorrowful thing has occurred: and she, being entirely gracious and pious, will tell us everything as best she can. Wait then, miserable mother, I would (if it pleases you) understand: the manner: and know the process: of that so bitter departure: which your only son made from you. I hear her respond with her weak voice: and say to me: "Dear devout spirits: who, through your desire, wish to feel greater sorrow and deeper groans in your loving hearts for me and my loving son. You desire to know that which happened to me in the harsh parting of my only son, beloved Jesus: Know: that having returned late on Holy Monday <note>original: "lunidi ſãto"</note>: my love, all famished, weary, and tired: once we had eaten, as one who was full of fear and suspicion: I wished to question <note>The catchword "iterrogat" indicates the start of the Virgin's inquiry to her son on the following page.</note>

->Part Five<-

...revealing his circumstances: and so as not to afflict his poor mother so much, it did not seem right to him then to reveal everything to me. And so, staying there through Tuesday and Wednesday <note>original: "Martedi & el Mercoie"</note>, he gave us all that little bit of comfort that can be given in such unpleasant circumstances, with such an abundance of healthy doctrine <note>original: "salutare dottrina," meaning teachings conducive to the salvation of the soul</note> that nothing like it was ever seen in the world. But on Wednesday evening, being at the last supper—which he shared with me, and with Martha, and with Magdalene—I could not help but watch my only love constantly: I carefully considered every little word and every gesture of his. Wherefore, seeing that he had eaten almost nothing, and that he turned quite pale while sighing frequently, I thought of nothing within myself but bad news hidden from me. And because of this, our entire holy company <note>original: "sacro collegio," likely referring here to the inner circle of disciples and the women at Bethany</note> being afflicted and sad, after the supper, with few words, everyone went to their usual place, so that there was weeping in every corner of that house. And I prayed my love that for a while

<column-break/>

he might rest, so that afterward he could speak better with me. But because of the immeasurable sadness, neither the one nor the other could close an eye. Wherefore I, overcome by the force of excessive love, went to my benign son, begging him that he would clarify the mind of his disconsolate and afflicted Mother regarding his death, knowing that no future thing could be hidden from him. Then my loving son embraced me very tightly, and began to weep and say: "O my mother, loved by me more than any other mother was ever loved: how will it be possible for me to give you such news of the cruel and horrendous death that I must soon undergo for human salvation?" <note>original: "salute humana," referring to the theological concept of the redemption of mankind</note> Then, by these words, my heart was pierced by a cruel wound, and I fell almost as if dead; but having recovered somewhat through the power of my son, I prayed him again that he would continue his speaking. And so proceeding...

But now, the day ordained from eternity <note>original: "ab eterno"</note> by my heavenly father being near—the day in which the Son of Man, to complete and perfect the entire sacred mystery of human redemption <term>redētione: the act of saving humanity from sin through Christ's sacrifice</term>, must receive the most atrocious and cruel death that was ever found in the world. My being conceived of the Holy Spirit in you, O dear mother, and ineffably born, and having suffered many anxieties and hardships <note>original: "affanni"</note> throughout the whole course of my life—such as fleeing into Egypt, staying in the harsh desert without food, suffering hunger and thirst, making journeys while preaching, and receiving insults and villainy—these are not enough. # Part Five

...in such a way that I could not live in such great anguish; it would be a great contentment for me to die with Him. And therefore, supplicating my loving son that he would grant me this singular grace, with great wisdom and constancy he answered me: ❧ "My most sweet and most wise mother, I cannot grant you this grace, because it has been prophesied that I alone must die in this way. Wherefore the prophet says: **'I have trodden the winepress alone, and from the nations there was no man with me'** <note>original: "Torcular calcavi solus: & de gentibus non est vir mecum"</note>, etc. And this would also be fitting, because it would give occasion to heretics <term>hæretici: those whose beliefs contradict the established doctrine of the Church</term> to think that my death was not sufficient for human redemption <term>redentione: the act of saving humanity from sin through Christ's sacrifice</term>. And also for another reason: it is fitting that you, dear and beloved mother, live after me, because all my disciples will fail in faith—which is to remain alone in you, who know all my teaching <term>dottrina: the body of teachings or principles of the faith</term> and are confirmed in grace. Therefore, they will turn to you in every doubt of theirs to be

<column-break/>

instructed, and also to be consoled by you in their many tribulations. Then, making entreaty to Him that he would at least moderate or diminish his measured pains of death, He answered me, saying: "It is written: **according to the measure of the sin, so shall be the number of the stripes** <note>original: "secundum mensuram delicti erit, et plagarum modus"</note>. My death will be an excellent and superabundant medicine for the innumerable sins of human nature. Therefore, just as the offenses committed—and those yet to be committed—against my Father are in a sense innumerable and of infinite gravity, it is fitting that my death be the most painful, the most desperate, and the most shameful that ever was in the world; so that divine works may not lack their due praiseworthy qualities—that is, that they be not only suitable and sufficient, but also superabundant for human reparation <term>reparatoē: the restoration of humanity to God's grace</term>. And because, my beloved mother, foreseen sorrows hurt less, I want to tell you now in brief the principal bodily pains that your only son must..."

-># Beautiful Meditations<-

[full] of sweetness. Tomorrow, tasting gall and vinegar, it will be full of bitterness <note>original: "amaritudine"</note>. My shoulders, which now feel so strong and sturdy, tomorrow, being made extremely weak, will be forced to carry the very heavy wood of the cross in the presence of all the people. My hands and my arms are now whole and healthy. Alas, tomorrow they will be stretched out and fixed upon the cross with cruel nails. See now my feet and my side without any injury. Tomorrow my feet, like my hands, will be nailed; and my sacred side will be pierced by a cruel spear. But what more needs to be said? You see now all my beautiful body, healthy and fair. Tomorrow, from head to foot, you will see it all wounded, beaten, and ruined, just as Isaiah well prophesied when he said: "From the sole of the foot even to the head, there is no soundness in it" <note>original: "A planta pedis: usque ad verticem capitis: non est in eo sanitas" — Isaiah 1:6</note>. And because it is now time for me to go to Jerusalem to celebrate the Passover <note>original: "fare la pascha"</note> and the Last Supper with my disciples, I must therefore at this moment take my leave <note>original: "pigliare licentia"</note> from you, my sweetest and most gentle mother, as one who will never again in this life speak with you. Now think of how many wounds and how many cruel knives pierced that virginal heart of the disconsolate and wretched mother, when she heard such words from her weeping and piteous son. Then she took her loving son, sweetly embracing and holding him tight, and with what voice she could manage in her great distress, she said: "O my dear son, O my sweet son, how bitter this news is to your wretched mother! Alas!"
