### Part Five

that I die of grief. Ah, poor Mary: what will you do without your love? Where will your consolations be now? And amidst these and similar laments ran Mary Magdalene, and Martha, and all the disciples; and hearing of the situation, they all began to weep and sigh. And so poor Jesus—who more than any other son bore a tender love for his mother—now, completely sorrowful, wept greatly. The poor mother of sorrows fell to the ground as if dead <note>original: "stramortita," describing a deep faint or state of shock</note>. Martha and Magdalene, crying out in loud voices, lamented; the apostles all wept. There was nothing there but tears and weeping. Even the walls seemed to weep at such a departure. And so, with everyone weeping, the kind and loving Jesus—who in all his time had been sweet, kind, loving, and gentle to his blessed mother and to all his household and friends—now in this final moment showed to everyone even more signs

<column-break/>

of great love. "Because he loved his own, he loved them to the end" <note>original Latin: "Quia dilexisset suos: in fine dilexit eos," a reference to John 13:1</note>. And so, the pious Lord wishing to hasten the departure, having thanked his blessed mother for all the favors that he had received from her in the space of thirty-three years that they had been together, and promising her that she would soon be consoled and amply rewarded for every distress of hers, he placed his hands around her neck with the greatest tenderness of love and embraced his loving mother, saying to her: "O my dear mother <note>original: "genitrice," a formal yet intimate term for one who gave birth</note>, O my sweet and gentle mother: this is the last kiss that I give you; this is the last embrace <note>term: "amplexo" | an archaic word for a formal or solemn embrace</note>; these are the last caresses; this is the last conversation. This prayer, from John 17, is often called the "High Priestly Prayer."</note>

### Meditation or contemplation on the preceding stories of the **Gospel Text** <term>Testo euangelico: referring to the narrative found in the four Gospels of the New Testament</term>, and first upon the **Supper** <term>cena: specifically the Last Supper</term>.

I become completely astonished; I am all in awe; I am entirely full of great wonder; I am completely surround-

<column-break/>

ed by great amazement when I contemplate the last supper of my Lord, which was adorned with so many admirable conditions that no other like it was ever seen. There I see God eating, and man performing things beyond nature. There I contemplate God weeping and crying, and man foreseeing future events. There I hear doctrines not human, but celestial. There I remain on every side bound and captured. There I do not know what to say to myself, nor how to speak. There I see an end given to the **ancient and Old Testament** <term>antico testamento: the era of the Old Covenant/Hebrew Bible, which the author sees as being fulfilled and replaced by the New Covenant during this meal</term>. There I await the renewal of the law and the commandments. There I see unheard-of things that by the worldly wise are esteemed as entirely impossible. There I contemplate **mysteries** <term>misterij: divine truths that are revealed by God but remain beyond full human comprehension</term> that cannot be contemplated. I shall speak of things that cannot be meditated upon. I see that which cannot be seen. I believe that which cannot appear here. ...speak. I see God giving new commandments. I see God preaching. I see God dressed in mortal flesh: speaking as a man, eating as a man, weeping as a man. I see the creator of heaven and earth prostrate and annihilated. I see him all burning, set ablaze by his great desire for our salvation. Here I see Christ, at once God and man, **passible** <term>passible: capable of feeling pain or suffering</term> and **impassible** <term>impassible: incapable of suffering or injury, referring to his divine nature</term>, mortal and immortal. I see him whole and healthy. And I see him giving his precious blood as a drink and his precious flesh as food, first to himself and then to all his chosen disciples. Who is he that, hearing such things, does not marvel? Who is he that, without special divine grace, can believe such things? # On Meditating Well

Apostolic family: for after the passion of the Lord, they returned and gathered there. In this house, this admirable supper was celebrated, and all the legal precepts <note>the requirements of the Mosaic Law regarding the Passover lamb and ritual</note> were fulfilled. And the sacrament of the altar <term>the Eucharist</term> was instituted, and all the wonderful things mentioned above were done. And after the resurrection, the Lord appeared to the Apostles in that place; and in the same house, the Holy Spirit came in the form of fire upon the disciples, and so on. So much so that one may well exclaim and say: O happy house! O happy master! O happy Mount Zion! Which may well be called a rich mountain and a firm mountain <note>original: *monte pingue: e monte coagulato*, traditional biblical imagery from Psalm 68 describing a fertile and solid mountain of God</note>:
> "In which it is well-pleasing to God to dwell" <note>original Latin: *In quo bene placitum est Deo habitare in eo*</note>.
About this mountain, David prophesied when he said:
> "The Lord has chosen Zion; he has chosen her for his habitation" <note>original Latin: *Elegit Dominus Sion; elegit eam in habitationem sibi*</note>.
In this place there are two chapels where the **Canons Regular** <term>priests living in community under a religious rule</term> lived (as the Carthusian <note>likely a reference to the theologian Denis the Carthusian</note> testifies) under one Abbot, following the rule of our holy father **Saint Augustine**. <column-break/>

pel was built in the **Cenacle** <term>the Upper Room</term>, the upper part of the house where the Lord dined with the disciples and the Apostles received the Holy Spirit. The other chapel was constructed in the lower part of the said house, where the Lord washed the feet of his disciples, and so on. In all these places, we shall follow our merciful Jesus and his loving Apostles. Once the preparation of the supper was diligently made, the merciful Lord (having left the master of the house) withdrew with his beloved and dear Apostolic family into the Cenacle to celebrate the Passover with them. And so, having washed their hands and given the blessing, they all sat at the table; this table—as is believed and as is shown in **Saint John Lateran in Rome** <note>the cathedral church of Rome, where a relic of the table of the Last Supper is traditionally kept</note>—was not long in shape, but square, and so large that thirteen people could comfortably sit there. To the first Apostle **Peter**, whom He had established in His mind as the leader <note>The original "principe" (prince) denotes Peter's leadership role among the Twelve.</note> of the Apostles, He wished to wash the feet. And he [Peter] utterly refused, saying: <note>original Latin: "non lavabis mihi pedes in aeternum"</note> "You shall never wash my feet." You do not know now what I am doing, but you will understand it well later, etc. But why does the discerning Apostle make such great resistance to his sweetest and dear **Master**? Does he not know that one must obey Him, being **God**, and one must believe that He does everything well, being infinite wisdom? # On Right Thinking #

Therefore, consider your dignity. Do not allow yourself to be conquered by sin any longer. No longer wish to be a servant of the demon. ### Part five

You are that kindling <note>original: "esca." While it can mean "bait," in this context of igniting a flame, it refers to the tinder or fuel that catches fire.</note> which produces a flame of love in the cold breast. You are that most savory nourishment which fills the human heart with every sweetness and delight. You are that food which restores and comforts the sinner. You are that which, on the path of salvation, <term>salvation: the Italian "salute" can mean both physical health and spiritual salvation; here it refers to the soul's journey toward God.</term> strengthens, fortifies, and invigorates him. You are that which extinguishes every lust. You are that which inflames the rational soul with love. You are that which makes it languish for the Lord. You are that which—having become perfect, completely transformed, and united to the Lord God by the power of love—makes it leave behind all bodily senses, and though living in the flesh, die to the world entirely. Who ever heard of such a food? Who ever found such a nourishment? Who ever conceived of such a banquet? Where the creature eats the flesh of the Creator. Where man devours the bread of angels. Where ice is resolved into fire. Where hell returns to health. Where the one who had perished <note>The transcription "esorto" is likely a typo for "estinto" (the deceased) or "morto" (the dead).</note> recovers life for himself.
<meta>catchword: accu-</meta>

<column-break/>

Let us all run, then, to this so greatly desired Supper. Come quickly, O Christendom, to this so sweet food; rouse your steps—you who are lazy and sleepy—for a heavenly meal is prepared for you. But first, let us ensure that, like the apostles, we are well washed and clean. For just as this wondrous food brings death to the wicked, it grants life to the good; let us praise the Savior with all our heart. Let us give thanks for such a great benefit with all our mind. Let us keep this memorial of his passion always fixed in our heart. Let us say to him with sweetness of heart: O kind Jesus. O sweet Jesus. O gentle Jesus. How kind and generous you have been toward human nature. Was it not enough for you to have created heaven and earth for us? Was it not enough to have given us the ministry of angels? Was it not enough to have humbled yourself from heaven down to earth? Was it not enough to have taken on human flesh? And being...

### ¶ Contemplation on the fourth article of the Passion: namely, on the final departure the Lord made from his disciples; on the prayer made in the garden; and on his cruel arrest and binding, and how he was led away with the greatest irreverence and cruelty by the impious Jewish crowd.

Having then risen to their feet: and courageously: they were scolded and rebuked by the Lord for coming armed as if against a lowly thief. **Peter**, wanting to show his great love toward his beloved master: drawing out his knife, struck the servant of the **High Priest** <note>original: "Pontifice"</note>: cutting off his right ear: Which

<column-break/>

was rebuked by the pious **Jesus**: because it was a time for suffering: and not for resisting: And thus, when the mob wanted to seize **Peter**, this was not permitted by the **Savior**: "For of those whom you gave me: I have not lost: even one of them" <note>original: "quia quos dedisti mihi: non perdidi: ex eis quemquā"</note>: but in every act: and gesture: wishing to show himself humble: kind and patient: And also to be true **God**. Having drawn near to the aforementioned servant, he healed him immediately of the severed ear: which thing being believed by the impious: that he had done it through magic arts. They did not change from their malignant purpose: therefore, having been answered by the Lord: that this was his hour and the power of darkness, the good **Jesus**, permitting it, was by the impious and wicked mob cruelly taken: and bound. And all the **Disciples**, bewildered and lost, left him whom they loved so much in the sacrilegious hands: of the **Jews**: Here I invite you to weep: **O Christian soul**: here I call to you: **O devout contemplator**...

<summary>This page describes the arrest of Jesus in the Garden, highlighting his divine power when he causes the soldiers to fall with a word, Peter's defense of him by cutting off Malchus's ear, and Christ's ultimate submission to being bound as his disciples flee.</summary>

### Part Five

Redeemer <note>original: "tore", completing the word from the previous page</note>. Here, overcome by great sorrow, I cry out and exclaim to all rational creatures: Here I am amazed: Here I remain utterly astonished: Here my voice and my breath fail me: Here I feel that which was never before heard or felt: 

An unheard-of thing: a sacrilege never before committed: The lowly creature takes and binds his omnipotent **Creator**: the impious servant conquers and overcomes his invincible Lord: the weak one holds and restrains him whom the entire world could not contain: 

A mournful spectacle: a lamentable event: a compassionate history: To see the cruel wolves rise up against the gentle shepherd; to see a gentle **Lamb** among so many roaring lions. 

My **Jesus**, how I see you now being ill-treated: O merciful Lord: how gladly I would help you: when I see you being taken by polluted hands: Being bound by the wicked: Being prostrated on the ground by the sacrilegious: By the cruel

<column-break/>

ones, having an iron chain placed around your neck: By the malicious, having your hands tied behind you like a rogue: By the shameful mob, being so vilely treated. 

Alas, for I see you being violently dragged by your hair: your gracious hands swollen by the hard ropes and all turned black and bruised. Alas, for I see my merciful **Savior**, constrained and bound like a thief, being led to his death: I see him fall to the ground, all pale and weak: I see him being soaked in the brook of **Kidron** <note>The Brook Kidron, or "Cedron," is the stream Jesus crossed to reach the Garden of Gethsemane; tradition holds he was pushed into the water during his arrest.</note>: I see him treated worse than a traitor: Around his neck he has an iron chain: on his hands he has hard and cruel ropes: on his feet he has no shoes to defend him from the hard stones: rather, I contemplate them all bare and bloody. 

Alas, how great must have been the sorrow of the afflicted **disciples**, when they saw their abandoned Master being led away so vilely and cruelly...

# On Thinking Well.

...ing <note>original: "nare", likely completing "bestemmiare" (to blaspheme) or "vociferare" (to cry out) from the previous page</note>: when they heard the injurious and scornful voices of the wicked against their merciful Lord. Oh, how sad, grieving, and disconsolate they remained when they saw themselves left like little orphans without their sweet father. Oh, how many times they turned their eyes back to look, if they might see him further. Oh, how many lamentable voices! Oh, how many cries! Oh, how many groans! Oh, how many sighs—when, out of fear, they departed and abandoned their loving and sweet Teacher. Not knowing what to do, nor what remedy to take to help and rescue him, they wept and cried out with a loud voice: "Ah, benign Father, where do we see you? Ah, dear Master, where do we leave you? O sweet **Jesus**: how miserably we have abandoned you! O merciful shepherd: where will your poor, lost sheep go now?" And so, prostrated on the ground, they sent their bitter

<column-break/>

voices to heaven; and their sorrow increased immeasurably when they saw such irreverence and shame done to their most beloved Lord; when they realized such cruelty was being used against him; when they watched him, alone like a lamb without resistance and without a voice, among so many cruel **Wolves**, ascend the mountain and cross the torrent <note>This refers to the Brook Kidron mentioned on the previous page</note> with great impetus and fury; when they saw him walking toward death without a cloak and with his head uncovered, his hands bound and his neck squeezed with such irreverence, and with such speed and haste that he could hardly gasp or breathe. O poor Mother <note>Addressing the Virgin Mary</note>, if you saw your son now—so abandoned by his disciples, so cruelly taken and bound, so irreverently led to death like a thief—how great would your sorrow be? What a cruel knife would pierce your soul? <note>A reference to the prophecy of Simeon in Luke 2:35</note> What torment would your immaculate heart feel? Oh, how bitter this news will be to you, which soon...

<summary>The text depicts the profound grief and guilt of the disciples as they flee from Jesus's arrest, followed by a vivid description of Christ's physical humiliation and an address to the Virgin Mary's anticipated sorrow.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the meditation on the Passion of Christ. The previous page ended with the disciples fleeing in fear after the arrest of Jesus in the Garden of Gethsemane, leaving him in the hands of the soldiers.</meta>

->Part Five<-

...you will feel. Oh, who is so hardened that at such an extreme moment <note>original: "articulo"; referring to a critical point in time or the moment of death</note> they are not entirely moved to tears? Who is he that does not waste away from grief? Who is he that, in return for so much suffering, does not wish, out of love for his Lord, to do something? And what better thing is there to do then, O dear Reader, than to be a faithful imitator of your Savior? He, therefore, in his imminent and excessive tribulations, prayed for a long time, and always placed the divine will

<column-break/>

before his own. You shall do the same: in your great troubles, you shall always have recourse to holy prayer <term>oratione: a formal spiritual practice of speaking with God</term>, always submitting yourself to the will of your creator. Always in the face of disgrace and the persecutions you receive, you shall be constant and patient, without any lamentation, because your creator was "led like a sheep to the slaughter; and as a lamb before its shearer is silent, so he opened not his mouth," etc. <note>original: "Sicut ouis ad occiſionem ductus eſt: & quaſi agnus coram tondente ſe obmutuit: nec aperuit oſuum &c." A quote from Isaiah 53:7.</note>

### Fifth Article regarding the many most cruel injuries done to the Lord: And how he was led from the garden to the house of Annas; and from Annas to Caiaphas; and from Caiaphas to Pilate; and from Pilate to Herod; and from Herod back to Pilate; and in all the aforementioned places he was vilely treated, mocked, and many times beaten and cruelly scourged. The text of the holy Evangelists follows.

# Part Five

# Part Five

"If you wish, according to the custom that exists at the time of the Passover, that I release to you a bound <term>malefactor: an evildoer or criminal</term>, I will give you Jesus, King of the Jews." <note>The transcription "rediu dei" appears to be a condensed form of "Re dei Giudei," meaning King of the Jews.</note> Then all the Jews, shouting, said: "We do not want you to release Jesus to us, but rather Barabbas," who was a thief, and had been placed in prison for murder and <term>sedition: an uprising or rebellion against the state</term>. Pilate said: "What then shall I do with Jesus?" They answered him: "Let him be crucified." Pilate said: "What evil has he done that I should crucify him?" And thus, desiring to free him, and knowing that the Jews had delivered him into his hands out of envy, he said: "I will chastise him and then let him go." Then he ordered his <term>ministers: here referring to officials or attendants</term> to scourge him. So, all the soldiers being called together, Jesus was stripped of his clothes in their midst and was most cruelly scourged by them all. And a crown of thorns was placed upon his head, and a <term>reed: a stalk used here as a mock scepter</term> in his right hand; and they struck him on the cheeks and spat upon

<column-break/>

him, striking his head with the reed. And kneeling mockingly before him, they <term>adored: in this context, offered mock worship to</term> him and ridiculed him, saying: "God save you, King of the Jews!" And so they dressed him in a purple garment, and with the crown of thorns on his head and the reed in his hand, Pilate led him out to the Jews and said: "Behold the man! <note>This is the famous "Ecce Homo" scene.</note> I have brought him out here to you, so that you may know that I have found no cause for death in him." Then the <term>pontiffs: high priests</term> and the ministers cried out more than ever that he should be crucified by any means. Seeing this, Judas the traitor, realizing that Jesus was condemned and repenting of his betrayal, returned the <term>thirty pieces of silver: the payment Judas received for his betrayal</term> to the chief priests and the elders, throwing them into the temple and saying: "I have sinned by betraying innocent blood." And so, having departed, he put a rope to his throat and hanged himself. And the Jews then, with that money, bought a field to bury foreigners, called the **Field of Blood** <note>original: "campo del sangue"</note> even until this present day.

### Contemplation on the Gospel Text of the Fifth Article: regarding the many insults, mocks, beatings, and the flagellation inflicted upon the Lord in the houses of Annas, Caiaphas, Pilate, and Herod.

In the first chapter of Lamentations: "Weeping, she wept in the night, and her tears are on her cheeks: and there is no one to comfort her among all those dear to her." <note>original Latin: "Trenorum primo plorans plorauit in nocte: & lachrime eius in maxillis eius: & non est qui prosoletur eam: ex omnibus caris eius." This refers to Lamentations 1:2, traditionally associated with the sorrow of Mary.</note>
The poor mother of the Lord was staying in the house of John <note>original: "giouani," referring to St. John the Apostle</note>. The night weighed heavily upon her only son; and desiring to hear something of him, weeping with tears she said: "Alas, I feel myself entirely languishing. Where is my son now? Where is my love to be found at this moment? Oh my son, who will be the one to give me some news of you?" And behold, suddenly she hears a knocking at the door. And her heart was wounded with great fear, fearing to hear of some unpleasant event having happened to her son. And the door having been opened, there entered

<column-break/>

inside: nine Disciples <note>The number nine excludes Judas the traitor and likely Peter and John, who followed Jesus to the high priest’s courtyard.</note>, all astonished, tearful, and sorrowful. She asks where they have left her sweet son. They respond with flowing tears, being more dead than alive: "O Mother, we have bad news of your son; oh, wretched are we, for we have all miserably abandoned him and left him among cruel wolves. We dined together and ate the Paschal Lamb; having washed the feet of us all, we went to the garden, where after the long prayer made, sweating living blood <note>A reference to the 'Agony in the Garden' where Jesus's sweat became as drops of blood.</note> from the great agony, the Jews came with a great armed crowd, and your son was cruelly taken and bound. Wherefore we, all dismayed, have left him among the Jews like a gentle Lamb among roaring lions. And having remained like wretched little sheep without a shepherd, not knowing what to do, we have come to you, sweet Mother." These words, which were like so many knives to the heart, having been heard by the Virgin Mary, she fell

### Part Five

Then, day having already broken, although the distressed Mary was tormented by unspeakable grief—so much so that she could hardly speak or walk—she was nonetheless overcome by boundless love. Accompanied by Mary Magdalene, her other beloved sisters, and her dear nephew <note>original: "nepote." In this context, it refers to John the Evangelist, often considered a close kinsman of Mary.</note> John, she went solicitously to see her beloved love before he died. She reached the house of Pilate just as he, having had Jesus entirely scourged, showed him to the Jews dressed in purple, crowned with thorns, and all torn apart. 

Now think, devout soul, what was the pain of that wretched mother when she saw her beloved son placed in such tortures and shames <note>original: "opprobrij." Refers to the public disgrace and insults hurled at Christ.</note>. She looked at him with her tearful and weeping eyes, saying: "O my son, who has treated you so cruelly? O light of my eyes, how you are now all darkened! O my beauty, how you are deformed! O my sweet love, where is your golden hair?

<column-break/>

Where is the beauty <note>original: "ornato," meaning the comeliness or adornment of his features.</note> of your white face? O my blessed son and sacred pledge <note>original: "pigno sacro." Mary views her son as a divine promise or treasure entrusted to her.</note> who used to be the life of my soul: how now you are all changed into the bitterness of my heart. O joys! O gladness! O pleasantness <note>original: "icondita," likely a derivative of the Latin "iucunditas," meaning delight or sweetness.</note> of my mind: how you are now all turned into sadness and bitterness of my soul. O my sweet son: how bitter you have become to me; how painful you have become to me. O my son, alas, all mine: why can I not help you? Why can I not embrace you? Why can I not at least kiss you? You have wounded my heart. You have pierced my soul. You have injured my entire spirit. O poor Mary! O unhappy mother: how great is your grief; alas, I am failing entirely from the pain." Making these and other unspeakable laments, the poor mother collapsed with grief; her bodily strength failed her; her breath was cut off; and she completely fainted <note>original: "meno si venia," literally "she came to less," describing a swoon or loss of consciousness.</note>. Who could ever describe the pain...

# On right thinking.

...until the cruel death of the cross, unjustly sentenced. And although the governor <note>original: "preside" — referring to Pontius Pilate.</note> washes his hands, and having repeated it a full seven times, affirmed that the sweetest and most pious Jesus was innocent, and that he found no cause in him for the intended death; you, O hardened and cruel people—entirely deprived of the light of the intellect because of your great sins—to give courage to Pilate, do not fear to condemn him to the cross. You call this greatest sin upon yourself and upon your wretched and miserable children, believing that such an excess of sin could forever pass unpunished.
> But not so the wicked, not so; for they are like the dust which the wind drives away from the face of the earth. <note>original Latin: "Sed non sic impy non sic quoniam tanquam puluis quem proijcit ventus a facie terre." <column-break/>

you have stirred up all your fury. Therefore, instead of the universal health <note>original: "sanita" — while literally health, in this theological context it implies "salvation" or spiritual wholeness.</note> that you should have attained through him, you will soon receive your universal ruin and final extermination. Now I turn to you, O false and impudent judge Pilate: are you not now ashamed <note>original: "confuso" — literally "confounded," but used here to mean feeling the weight of shame or moral confusion.</note> to condemn him whom you have so many times, in the presence of all the stubborn commoners, confessed to be innocent? Where is your justice? Where is equity? Where is reason? Where is the respect for God? Where is the honor of the world? Who would have ever thought that in a governor of such a noble city, there would reign such impudence and such ignorance, that he would think he could wash away the gravest stain <note>original: "macula."</note> of such a murder with water? Thus is he endangered, and thus does he fall into ruin—he who governs himself according to the world, and not according to the rectitude of justice. Who has convinced you? ### Part Five

...justify the innocence of the holy and just man and condemn him to death as an unjust man? Where have you learned to give sentence against one who has not confessed, nor has yet been convicted by suitable witnesses? Where is the delay and the assigned term <note>a fixed period of time allowed for a legal response</note> that is used in every praetorium <term>Praetorium: the judicial hall or residence of a Roman governor</term>, where some shadow of justice is found? How many days have you assigned him to be able to defend himself and state his case? Are you not aware of the order of justice? Do you not understand that defense should be denied to no one, because it is of natural law <note>original Latin: "de iure naturali"</note>? Do you not see that in a single night he is captured, tormented, accused, and sentenced without any term or order of reason? Everything is done with fury and rage, without any appearance or form of judgment. How do you think, O foolish Pilate, to be able to wash with water your so defiled soul, which all the water of the ocean sea could not wash? It will be a just thing, then, that also

<column-break/>

you, together with the wicked Jews, shall be deservedly punished and avenged. I leave you then as a treacherous, unjust, and iniquitous man, etc. And here I turn to the friends of the condemned Jesus, and with them I make my bitter lamentation. O mother of my God <note>referring to the Virgin Mary</note>, how much I see you grieved and in immeasurable pain; in your affliction until this hour you could have had some hope that your only son, in some way escaping death, might be liberated. But now there is no more remedy for his life; he is condemned to death multiple times. In the house of Annas <note>the former High Priest</note> he was beaten by the servant of the priest <note>original: "seruore dal pat[riarca/pontefice]"</note> and mistreated against all justice; at the house of Caiaphas, without any just cause, those rogue leaders have condemned him to death; at the house of Herod, as a pretender and a fool, they have entirely reviled him. At the house of Pilate he was, against every order of justice, sentenced to the Cross and to death. Who is he then who

# On the Good Use
<note>The header "Del ben reusare" likely refers to the "good use" or "proper application" of these spiritual meditations for the reader's soul.</note>

...was not moved to such cruelty. Who is he who can think of how great a wound the soul of the poor mother <note>referring to the Virgin Mary</note> received when she heard that the wicked had obtained their depraved desire; when she knew the iniquitous sentence of death was given against her only and beloved son? Alas, what do you think that unhappy and wretched mother said, when she could find no further remedy for her great grief? She turned her eyes to heaven, imploring supreme help; then she said to John <note>John the Apostle</note>: "What shall we do, orphans <term>Pupilli: a term for fatherless children or wards; here used to describe the disciples' state after Christ's death</term> without a father? What will the mother do without her only loving son?" Then she said: "O Magdalene, behold your love, who no longer has anyone to speak a word to him, or to say: 'The impious have obtained victory over the innocent; the Creator has been unjustly condemned by his creature <note>A common theological paradox: God the Creator being judged by humans, who are his creations</note>.' That great judge who is to judge the living and the dead has been iniquitously judged by the vile and perfidious judge, Pilate."

¶ O inexpiable wickedness!

<column-break/>

O unspeakable iniquity! Why does the sun not darken? Why does the earth not tremble? How can the elements endure such enormous injustice: that the true God should be condemned to death by an earthly and corrupt judge as if he were a depraved and wicked man? But since heaven is silent and the earth does not move at this moment, I, miserable as I am, and willing, shall not be silent. But speaking to my sweet Jesus of this iniquitous and infernal sentence, tolerated so patiently for my salvation, I will thank Him with all my heart and all my mind, saying:

¶ O merciful Lord. O gentle Redeemer. I praise You. I glorify You. I confess that You are my God, my supreme good, my supreme glory. I thank You that to free me from infernal death, You did not hold in horror being condemned to death by Pilate, You who are innocent. I pray You, give me grace that at the final judgment <note>referring to the Last Judgment at the end of the world</note>, when You as judge shall come to judge...

### Fifth Part

...the living and the dead: do not enter into judgment with your servant, for in your sight no living person shall be justified <note>A reference to Psalm 143:2, emphasizing human humility before divine perfection</note>: but let me say to you: To the King of ages, immortal and invisible God, be honor and glory forever and ever, amen. <note>original Latin: "Regi seculorum immortali & invisibili Deo: honor & gloria: in secula seculorum amen"</note>

**Seventh Article: how the merciful Jesus was led to Mount Calvary; and there given gall to drink; and crucified between two thieves. The Gospel text follows.**

Once the sentence was given, the soldiers immediately took the Lord and led him into the praetorium <term>Praetorium: the headquarters or palace of a Roman governor</term>, and there, after they had mocked him, they stripped him of the purple robe and dressed him in his own clothes. Placing the cross upon his shoulders, they led him out of the city to Mount Calvary. And as they led him, they seized Simon of Cyrene, the father of Alexander and Rufus, who was coming from the countryside, and they forced him to carry the cross behind Jesus. Whereupon a great multitude of people and women followed him, who for love of him lamented and wept.

<column-break/>

Turning to them, the Lord said: "Daughters of Jerusalem, do not weep for me. But weep for yourselves and for your children; for behold, the days are coming soon in which they will say: Blessed are the barren, and the wombs that have not conceived, and blessed are the breasts that have not nursed. Then they will begin to say to the mountains: 'Fall upon us!' And to the hills: 'Cover us!' For if they do these things in the green wood, what will be done in the dry?" <note>A biblical metaphor where the "green wood" represents the innocent Christ and the "dry wood" represents the guilty who are more easily consumed by fire/judgment</note>

There were two other wicked men led with him to be killed. And when they reached the place called Golgotha—which is to say, the Place of the Skulls—they gave Jesus wine to drink mixed with myrrh and gall; having tasted it, he would not drink it. And in this place they crucified Jesus and the two thieves with him: one on the right and the other on the left, with Jesus in the midst of them. And in this way was fulfilled and verified...

m ii ¶

...the scripture which says: "and he was counted among the wicked." <note>original Latin: "cum iniquis deputatus est," citing Isaiah 53:12, a prophecy traditionally seen as foretelling the crucifixion of Jesus between two thieves.</note>

### ¶ Contemplation on the seventh article of the Gospel history of the Savior’s passion: how the pious Jesus, once the sentence was given, was shamed and humiliated in various ways, and led with the greatest pain and sorrow to Mount Calvary and crucified.

In the fifty-third chapter of Isaiah: "He was wounded for our iniquities; he was bruised for our sins; the punishment of our peace was upon him, and by his bruises we are healed." <note>original Latin: "vulneratus est propter iniquitates nostras: attritus est: propter scelera nostra: disciplina pacis nostrę super eum cuius liuore sanati sumus." This famous passage from the Hebrew Bible is the foundation for the author's meditation on Christ's physical suffering.</note> Great has been the pain of my Jesus and of his pious mother up to this point. But now, every torment has increased beyond measure for both of them. Alas, there is no more debate whether my honey-sweet <term>Mellifluo: Literally "flowing with honey," a common medieval and Renaissance term used to describe the sweetness of divine love and the character of Jesus.</term> and loving Jesus is worthy of death. There is no more struggle. There is no more litigation; there is no longer anyone to defend the just cause of the innocent son

<column-break/>

of Mary. Everyone consents: and let it be even to the shameful death of the cross. Truly, no one thinks anymore that he might escape. There is no more hope that he can flee the horrific gallows of the cross. What then shall we miserable sinners do, for whom the kind and loving Lord has been so unjustly judged, so cruelly wounded, so variously bruised and injured? Certainly, that sinner would be very impious and hard-hearted who, at this moment requiring such compassion for the Lord and for his sweet mother—both placed in such agony—did not wish to weep and shed tears. I invite every sinner, therefore, to come with me behind our most loving Savior, and follow him to Mount Calvary, together with the afflicted John, with the mournful Magdalene, and with the distressed and poor Mary. Come and consider well, O sinful soul, everything that...

# Part Five

...follows: for once that wicked and iniquitous sentence was given, it was immediately proclaimed by the sound of a trumpet throughout the city of Jerusalem: how, according to the truth of justice, Jesus—that great preacher—had been sentenced to death. This was because he had been convicted and found to be a great scoundrel <term>ribaldo: A term for a rogue or villain, here used to contrast his public reputation as a preacher with his legal condemnation.</term>, a man of ill-fortune, a seducer of the common people, arrogant and proud; who, being born of poor people, wanted to usurp the kingdom of the Jews; and being a hypocrite, wanted to be considered the son of God. Think how much tumult and gathering of people occurred upon hearing such a thing. While everyone rushed to Pilate’s house to see this great spectacle, the poor Jesus was led back inside the hall of justice <term>loco de la ragione: Literally the "place of reason," referring to the tribunal or court.</term> by the impious ministers, where he was once again mocked, scorned, and reviled by them. So that he might be better recognized by all as a wicked and perverse man, they immediately stripped him of the foreign purple robe and in his own garments

<column-break/>

clothed him. Here, O sinful soul, you can imagine how much excessive pain this act caused: in which all the wounds of his sacred body were renewed, and once again, like a fountain of living blood, they appeared falling to the ground from every part of his precious flesh. And without any human pity, they placed a large and heavy cross upon his weary and weak shoulders, with a rope around his neck, having tied one hand behind his back. This was so that with the other he could carry his gibbet <term>patibulo: The cross-piece or the instrument of execution.</term>, while continually wearing the cruel crown of thorns on his head. Now, thus treated, confused, so reviled, and rejected on every side, they led out your Savior in the sight of all the people, who reproached him with loud voices, saying: "Behold the scoundrel! Behold the traitor who deceived us all!" They had placed him in the middle of two thieves, whom they led together to Mount Calvary to execute <term>giustificarli: Historically, "to justify" someone could mean to carry out a legal sentence of execution upon them.</term> them; and for these, it was the custom...

# On Right Thinking

**Del ben pensare**

...lamentable shrieks: they filled the air with tearful cries; and finding for her grieving and embittered heart no restoration; the wretched women, not knowing what to do, remained as if stunned by the immeasurable pain of seeing their beloved love, Jesus crucified, overcome. Now I turn to you, sinful soul: are you deaf? Have you not heard that for you the sweet Jesus is cruelly crucified? Have you not accompanied Him with me to Mount Calvary? Have you not well considered His every act? Have you not wept with Mary? Have you not shed tears with Magdalene? Have you not cried out in a loud voice with the holy women? Have you not lamented the cruelty of the Jews; the impiety of the officials <note>original: "ministri," referring to the executioners or soldiers</note>; the hardness of the nails? Have you not thought to be grateful for such great love? But if you do not feel remorse in such shame done for you to your Lord—in so many torments received by that gentle lamb of God for your love—I say that you are

<column-break/>

more hardened than the treacherous Jews; you are more impious than the diabolical officials; you are more cruel than the iron nails. Return then to yourself, O faithful soul, and see that for your enormous sins, the Son of Mary has been enormously tormented. Because of your hardness, the Son of God has been harshly fixed <note>original: "confitto," meaning fastened or driven through with nails</note> to the cross. For the sins of your hands, the holy hands of the sweet Jesus have been pierced with nails. For your offenses of the feet, your Savior has been harshly nailed by His feet upon the cross. You have committed the sin, and your good Jesus has performed the harsh and difficult penance for it. You felt delight in your flesh and your own taste, and your Lord has tasted in His mouth the bitterness of gall <note>original: "fiele," the bitter substance offered to Christ on the cross</note>, and throughout His whole person, harsh torments. Why are you not moved to pity? Why do you not leave behind so many errors? Why are you so hard toward your Lord? O heartless one! O iron-hearted! O cruel one! Become now at last...

...of the Jews: which writing, being in the Greek, Hebrew, and Latin languages, was read by many; for the place where the Lord was crucified was near the City. The High Priests of the Jews therefore said to Pilate: "Do not write 'King of the Jews,' but rather that he said, 'I am King of the Jews.'" Pilate responded: "What I have written, I wish to remain written." <note>original: "Quello ho scritto: voglio fia scritto," an Italian rendering of the Latin "Quod scripsi, scripsi"</note> The soldiers, having crucified him, divided his garments among themselves, making four parts: giving each soldier his share of said garments. And [they took] the Tunic, which was made without seams from the top, being well-wrought throughout. <note>The "seamless garment" was traditionally interpreted by theologians as a symbol of the unity of the Church.</note> These soldiers said to one another: "Let us not divide it, and let us not tear this garment, but let us cast lots for it, so that it belongs only to the one to whom it falls by lot." And so the prophecy of the scripture was fulfilled which says: "They divided my garments among them, and for my clothing...

<column-break/>

...they cast lots." Those who passed by, shaking their heads, said: "Aha! You who would destroy the Temple of God and rebuild it in three days, save yourself now! If you are the Son of God, come down from the Cross!" Likewise, the chief priests with the scribes and Pharisees said to one another: "He saved others, but he cannot save himself. If he is the King of Israel, let him come down now from the Cross, so that seeing this, we may believe him. He trusted in God; let Him deliver him now if He is pleased with him, for he said that he is the Son of God." And so the people stood watching, and along with the leaders they mocked him. And similarly, the soldiers mocked him. And approaching him, offering him vinegar, they said: "If you are the King of the Jews, save yourself." And one of the crucified thieves <term>latroni</term> <note>The Italian "latroni" implies highwaymen or bandits, traditionally the "bad thief" and the "good thief."</note>, speaking villainy, blasphemed Jesus, saying: "If you are the Christ <note>original: "xpo," a scribal abbreviation using the Greek letters Chi and Rho to represent Christ.</note>, save yourself and us." We indeed receive justice according to what our evil deeds have deserved. But this man has never done any evil."

## ¶ Contemplation on the eighth Article of the Passion: concerning the elevation of the Cross into the air; and the many mockeries, insults, and lamentations made against the kind Jesus Christ by the Jewish and ungrateful people of Jerusalem.

[A woodcut illustration divided into two vertical panels. The left panel shows the Flight into Egypt, featuring Mary seated on a donkey holding the infant Jesus, with Joseph leading the animal by a rope and carrying a staff. The right panel depicts the Crucifixion, showing Jesus on the cross flanked by Mary and Saint John the Evangelist. The entire illustration is bordered on the left and right by vertical strips of decorative floral or geometric patterns.]

Zechariah, the tenth chapter <note>The text cites chapter 10, though this prophecy is traditionally found in Zechariah 13:6.</note>: "What are these wounds in the middle of your hands?" And he will say, "With these I was wounded in the house of those who loved me." <note>original Latin: "Quid sunt plage iste i medio manuti tuarū? et dicet his plagat⁹ sum in domo eorum qui diligebant me."</note> Run with a swift step: hasten as much as you can, O Christian soul; go to Mount Calvary outside of Jerusalem near the gate: and there, with all your heart, lift up your eyes and see if ever in the world

<column-break/>

you saw a similar deed done. See if you ever saw such great injustice committed against an innocent one; see if you ever saw such great cruelty exercised against a person. Oh me! How can you stand there without bursting from grief when you see the sweetest Lord on the cross, with his feet and hands so cruelly pierced <term>pierced: "confitto," literally "fixed with nails"</term>; when you see him all bruised and bloodied?

<header>On thinking well</header>

...when you see him raised in the air: all broken and shattered: when you see [blood] like three torrents flowing so abundantly from the principal wounds: that is, from the two hands and the feet: when you see that precious and well-formed body supporting itself upon hard nails. When you see him naked and stripped, having no place left where he can rest his holy, thorn-crowned head. When you see him so impiously refreshed <note>The author uses "refocillato" (refreshed/revived) ironically, as the gall and myrrh were intended to mock or further afflict him.</note> with bitter gall and harsh myrrh. Rather, you will see him rejected by every person. When you see his precious garments divided and shared among the diabolical ministers: when you see him so vilely mocked and derided: when, as they shake their heads, you hear those wicked ones say with reproach: "This is the one who wanted to destroy the temple and rebuild it in three days; this is the one who saved others, and yet now he cannot free himself in any way. This is the one who called himself the Son of God, and now he cannot descend from the cross." Certainly you will not

<column-break/>

be able to contain yourself from crying out in a loud voice, saying:
> O sweetest Jesus: O most gentle Lamb of God: O my most sweet Jesus. O most holy Son of God, how great is the love you have shown us in your so many extreme pains. Who is he who can express how great is the pain of your heart? How great is the suffering of your body? How great is the compassion for your poor mother: oh, what a cruel spectacle is this? Oh, what a horrific image <note>original: "stampa," referring to the scene impressed upon the viewer's sight</note> is now set before me? Who is so cruel that, in this so tearful a case, does not become all sweet and pious? Who is he who does not desire to die with him? Who is he who does not desire to be crucified with him? Who is he who does not consider agonizing <note>original: "spasmare," referring to the "Spasimo," the intense physical and emotional agony often depicted as a fainting fit of the Virgin Mary</note> with Mary? Bursting with grief with Mary Magdalene? Fainting <note>original: "mancare," meaning to fail, swoon, or lose strength</note> entirely from pain and torment with John and the other devout women? O wretched me, what can I do in this case? I feel my heart wounded from every side. My [Jesus] causes me to die of grief...

### Part Five

Sweet love, Jesus, so full of **passion** <note>The word "passionato" here refers specifically to Christ's Passion—his suffering and death—but also implies his intense love for humanity.</note>. -># On Thinking Well.<-

What have I done to you? In what have I ever troubled you? Answer me, I who am your **Creator**: Answer me, I who am your **Redeemer**: to you alone, my chosen people, I revealed the knowledge of my divinity. To you alone, through the **Prophets**, I made it understood that there was only one Creator; one God. To you alone I sent the law from Heaven. To you alone I multiplied the voices of the Angels and of the Prophets; for you alone I performed miracles unheard of in the world; for you alone, to liberate you from the harsh slavery of **Pharaoh**, I struck **Egypt**; and that Pharaoh I harshly scourged; for you I opened the sea, making you pass safely through the middle of it; for you I submerged in it that great army of Pharaoh that was pursuing you. To you in the desert I gave the **manna** <note>In the Bible, manna was the miraculous "bread from heaven" provided to the Israelites during their forty years in the desert.</note> from heaven: a wonderful and flavorful food. To you, miraculously in the solitude, I made a living spring gush from the hardest stone. You, finally, I led into the **land of promise** <note>A reference to Canaan, the land promised to the Israelites in the Old Testament.</note>: which flows with milk and honey; what more

<column-break/>

further could I have done for this chosen **vineyard** <note>The "vineyard" is a common biblical metaphor for the people of God, particularly in the Book of Isaiah.</note> that I have not done for it? Having therefore placed these remembered and many other benefits upon you: I expected that you would be grateful, fruitful, and generous. But you are a wicked and perverse generation. Is this what you render to the Lord, foolish and unwise people? <note>original Latin: "Heccine reddis dño popule stulte: e isipiēs"</note> This exchange you return to your Lord, O foolish and unwise people: Am I not your father who created and made you? O ungrateful vineyard. O cruel vineyard: how unjust you are toward me, who expected to gather sweet grapes from you, but I have harvested nothing but sour wild grapes. O ungrateful vineyard, into how much bitterness and displeasure you have turned me; instead of giving me thanks: you arranged for **Barabbas** the thief to be saved, and condemned me to death. You crowned me with piercing **thorns**, which with a thousand pricks have bloodied my precious head. O impious people: [I am] he who has restored your dead to life, enlightened the blind, straightened the lame...

<summary>In this passage, Christ delivers the "Reproaches," contrasting his historical miracles and the liberation of the Israelites from Egypt with the cruelty and ingratitude he receives during his trial and crucifixion.</summary>
<keywords>Reproaches, Exodus, Pharaoh, Manna, Promised Land, Barabbas, Ingratitude of the Vineyard, Passion of Christ</keywords>

# Part Five

<columns>

...spirit. And the earth shook; and the rocks were split; and the tombs were opened. And many bodies of the saints who were dead rose from the tombs: and entering into the holy city, they appeared to many. Because of these things, the centurion <note>A Roman officer who, according to the Gospels, recognized Jesus's divinity at the moment of his death.</note> and those who were with him as guards of Jesus, having seen the earthquake and the things that were happening, and having heard that he had expired with such a great cry, were greatly afraid, saying: "Truly this man was the Son of God." And so the Centurion glorified God, saying: "Truly this man was righteous." And the whole crowd of those who were present together at this spectacle, seeing the things that followed, beating their breasts, returned home. ¶ Contemplation upon the ninth article of the seven honey-sweet words spoken by our kind and sweet Jesus upon the cross: And first of the [last] three words: and the weeping performed by the Marys.

Friends, at the end of the lives of the dying, are usually drawn by the bond of love or kinship to desire to hear some loving word of charity. Those who are afflicted and troubled usually utter words of blasphemy and harsh lamentations in their great torments. Those who are distressed and persecuted usually call upon heaven and earth with harsh and cruel words to take vengeance against their persecutors; inviting to their mouths always words of impatience, of evil curses, and great lamentations are heard to resound. The pious Jesus, being at the threshold of death in the midst of his [sorrows]... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with a contrast between the behavior of worldly men in pain and the patience of Christ.</note>

</columns>

...dear friends and even of his enemies, placed in greater persecution and pain than any man was ever in the world: it will be a useful thing to see in this Article <note>original: "Articolo". In devotional literature, an "Article" refers to a specific point of meditation or a section of a religious treatise.</note> how he behaved lovingly with his friends and with his persecutors. Let us see then if ever greater cruelty was used against a person; let us see if ever anyone was placed in greater pain and torment. Let us see if ever so much injustice was exercised against a creature. Let us see if ever against a man or woman so much ingratitude was used. Your Lord stands: with hands and feet cruelly nailed upon the cross; with vinegar, gall, and myrrh <note>Gall and myrrh were often mixed with vinegar to create a bitter drink, offered to the dying either as a crude anesthetic or as a further act of mockery.</note> they have embittered his tongue and taste; they have iniquitously stripped him of his holy garments; they have torn his precious head with sharp thorns; they have reckoned him like a prince of thieves between two evildoers <note>original: "malfattori". This refers to the two thieves crucified alongside Jesus.</note>. By the most vile ministers, by the princes, by the Scribes, by the Pharisees, by those seated <note>Likely referring to those sitting in judgment or the elders of the Sanhedrin.</note>, by the priests...

<column-break/>

...reunited, and by all the people he is blasphemed, cursed, mocked, and reviled. They stand around him in his many martyrdoms <note>original: "martyrij". Here referring to the various stages and types of suffering Jesus endured.</note> with continuous insults and villainies. Those wicked, fierce, and cruel ones do not cease, being more than tigers in the great impiety and cruelty they use. Yet they thirst for his blood and desire his death; they ensure every dishonor is done to him; full of hatred, of rage, of toxin, and of poison, they do all they can to torment him, to torture him, to extinguish and to annihilate him. And thus the kind Jesus <note>original: "Giesu"</note>, like a gentle lamb <note>original: "mansueto agnello". A common Christological symbol of innocence and patient sacrifice.</note>, stands without any lament, without any angry word, among cruel wolves. He is not heard lamenting. He is not heard cursing. He is not heard blaspheming. But full of outward and inward tortures, surrounded on every side by most cruel and unjust persecutions, saturated with reproaches and crowned with thorns, bound with nails, all...

</columns>

### Part Five

<columns>

...bruised: all torn apart: all wounded: covered in sores: bloodied: from head to foot all bleeding. Nevertheless, like a sweet little lamb: the entirely pious and merciful Jesus speaks loving words toward his enemies, the likes of which were never heard in such a horrific situation. You do not hear from that sweet mouth words of complaint, nor of excuses, nor of vengeance, nor of curses: But sugar-coated words: of prayers, of forgiveness, of salvation: like one who does not feel his own torments. He does not value his own injuries; he takes no account of the insults <note>original: "improperij". These are the ritualized taunts or reproaches directed at Christ during the Passion.</note> hurled at him. But because inside he burns with love: he blazes with charity: he consumes himself with the desire for human salvation <note>original: "salute". In this period, the word carries the dual meaning of "health" and "spiritual salvation."</note> and redemption. Therefore, as if forgetting and unmindful of every insult, reproach, and villainy done to him—not remembering himself nor his dear apostles, and even leaving his loving mother to languish, who with all her companions was waiting for a wor-

<column-break/>

-d from her dying son more than any other earthly thing. Turning to his eternal Father with a fullness of fervor: our celestial teacher uttered upon the pulpit <note>original: "cathedra". Traditionally the seat of a bishop or professor; here the author envisions the cross as a scholar's chair from which Christ teaches his final lesson.</note> of the cross a word of such charity and such doctrine: that none greater was ever heard in the world. He said then, praying and supplicating for his enemy persecutors and crucifiers, this first word: that is, "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do" <note>original Latin: "pater ignosce illi quia nesciunt quid facit". This is the first of the "Seven Last Words" of Christ.</note>. "O my sweetest Father, O my most merciful Father, forgive them: because they sin ignorantly and do not know what they do." O words more divine than human! O furnace of love more celestial than earthly! O sweetest tongue! O sweetest mouth! O gentle Jesus. <note>typographic ornament</note> By this: you clearly demonstrate a patience <note>The OCR "pza" is likely an abbreviation for "pazienza" (patience) or "potenza" (power).</note> beyond every intellect, mind, and pious soul: Who is he who is not moved by such sweetness? Who is he who, toward his enemies, by this example, is not [moved to mercy]? <note>typographic ornament</note>

->## On Thinking Well<-

<columns>

[...sweetly?] Who is he who could seek more vengeance? In this we shall recognize if we are true Christians: if we wish to follow our true leader and teacher with a peaceful and kind heart, forgiving those who offend us. Usually, the afflicted—the more pain they feel and the more they suffer from what hurts them—the greater is the lament they produce. Thus, the loving Jesus, knowing the sin of his crucifiers to be the greatest that ever was, and grieving more for the offense against God than for his own pain, made his first word one of appeasing his offended Father. Therefore he said: Father, forgive them <note>original: "pater ignosce illis"</note>. And so that the Father might more easily be inclined toward pardon and remission <note>The theological term for the forgiveness of sins.</note>, he added the excuse of their ignorance, saying: for they know not what they do <note>original: "quia nesciunt quid faciunt"</note>. Having heard this so-sweet word, the sorrowing mother said in her heart: "O my son, you speak to your crucifiers; you pray for them. Now, why do you not speak to your mother?

<column-break/>

Now, why do you not pray for her who is completely failing from sorrow?" The thief who was on his right hand, having understood the plea the Lord had made for his cruel crucifiers, was enlightened by a great faith, warmed by great love, and enlivened by holy hope. He said to the honey-sweet <note>original: "melifluo". A common devotional term describing speech or divinity that is as sweet and life-giving as honey.</note> Jesus: Lord, remember me when you come into your kingdom <note>original: "Memento mei domine dum veneris in regnum tuum"</note>. To whom Jesus replied, saying: Amen, I say to you, today you shall be with me in paradise <note>original: "Amen dico tibi hodie mecum eris in paradiso"</note>. In truth I tell you, that today you will be with me in paradise. O good thief: how happy you have been made! With such a brief prayer at the very end of your life, you were made worthy of paradise. O divine grace! O heavenly mercy! Who ever drank of such a grace? To whom did such a great good ever happen? The mother of Jesus stood near the cross, and there with unspeakable sighs and bitter tears she said: "O my son, and hope of my heart: Now what—"

</columns>

Do you not look at me, who gave you life? You are merciful to the cruel crucifiers; you are generous to the crucified thief. <column-break/>

placed in such great distress? Perhaps in his compassionate heart, the merciful son must have said: "O my mother, you are to me another harsh cross, when I see your heart wounded by such great sorrow. O my beloved mother <term>genitrice</term> <note>A formal Latinate term for "she who gave birth."</note>, your weeping torments me more than my many wounds do. O my most beloved mother, how it pains me that you can no longer receive joys from your son in this life. Few words will you be able to hear from me, for I am near death. And even those few will not mitigate your pain, but will increase it. O poor mother, how soon your poor son will be taken from you; soon my company will be taken from you; soon you will be deprived of my presence. What words, then, do you want me to say to you for comfort? If I call you 'Mother,' I will make your maternal heart burst with tenderness and sorrow. And if I do not make you worthy of some small word of mine, and if I do not give you, in this my last..."

-># On Right Thinking<-

...faint and fail: O sinful soul, why do you not move to compassion in such great laments? Why do you not shed a single tear in such great weeping? Who is so hardened that they do not weep now with the sorrowing Mary? O, how unhappy is he who is not seized by pity for sweet Jesus, placed in such agony at the end of his life.

O hard breast! O diamond-like heart: break now at such sobs. Awaken to compassion in such torments. Weep with your Creator, who now finally weeps for you. He wept for you in the manger <note>original: "presepio"</note>; for you he wept at the circumcision. For you he wept in the desert. For you he wept infinite times in his life: and now in this bitter passion, from the garden <note>The OCR "lozto" refers to "l’orto," the Garden of Gethsemane.</note> even unto the cross, he has always wept for you. And now, being at the final boundary of life, praying for you, he weeps bitterly. And can you not shed one tear with him? O pitiless creature. O iron mind: accompany yourself with Mary, with John, and with Magdalene; and well reflect upon this entire tearful and bitter process.

Place yourself in the midst of this so noble company, and do not depart from them at all, until you have first received from them the gift of weeping or mourning, and from all of them you have felt great heartache <term>cordoglio</term> and bitter compassion.

# Part Five

...having earnestly prayed to the Father; and to the thief to whom he had promised paradise; and to the disciple to whom he had given his mother; and to the mother, to whom he had assigned the new son. As great and cruel torments grew without measure throughout the glorious body and throughout the soul of the loving Jesus, he saw that the end of his life was drawing very near. Wishing to demonstrate the reality <note>original: "verita," here referring to the physical reality and vulnerability</note> of his flesh in his immense interior and exterior pain, he turned within himself and, secretly praying to his eternal Father for himself, he said: "O God, my Father, look now upon me, and see how the humanity of your obedient son is entirely engulfed in extreme pains. I see being committed against you by my brothers. ¶ The horrific infernal pains assigned by justice to poor sinners torture me above all else. ¶ But much more am I tormented by the loss of the eternal goods prepared for them in paradise (if they had not offended you). Great is my distress, my most holy and most loving Father, ¶ when I see so many martyrdoms and cruel persecutions that are to be inflicted upon the holy Martyrs for our love. ¶ How much pain my heart feels for the sins of my disciples! O my God: why have you so completely abandoned me? # On Right Thinking

You are called <note>A small circular floral ornament appears here in the original text, likely to signify a meditative pause.</note> devout soul, to see and to deeply consider his passage from this miserable and deceptive world. You are invited to be present at the harshest and most cruel death of the most merciful Jesus. Run, then, and delay no more if you wish to see alive the one who loves you so much. Run dressed in brown and black garments <note>Traditional colors of mourning.</note>. Go, and lead with you all devout Christians to accompany poor Mary, who now stands at the cross awaiting only the death of her only son. Who is there who would not have compassion for such a mother in such an extreme case? Who is there who, in that final step and amidst such excessive pains, would not grieve with the poor, abandoned, and dying Jesus? Until now, the all-loving Jesus, though he has been in the greatest torments <term>cruciati: a term derived from the Latin 'cruciatus,' referring specifically to the tortures of the cross</term>, has always refreshed us with some sweet word of his. But now, because the pains have increased beyond measure, all remains silent, because the hour of death has come; no longer can that blessed

<column-break/>

soul dwell with its precious body; it must be that through such violence of harsh torments, it departs from us. Alas, how cruel will that Christian be who, called to this so precious, admirable, and cruel death, would not wish to be present! O, how ungrateful will he be who, in such agony, anxiety, and pain, will not weep with his loving Jesus, who with such charity calls us to consider and see his great and unheard-of pain that he suffers for us in this extreme point of life! Indeed, he who does not weep would be harder than stone; indeed, he who is not moved by such anxiety would be more cruel than any beast. All the angels run to this cruel spectacle. The eternal Father commands all the blessed spirits to pay due honor to his dying son. All of heaven is seen to be moved to perform the honorable funeral rites <note>original: "exequie," referring to the formal liturgical rites for the dead.</note> for such a great Lord. The angelic choirs weep out of compassion for their creator. They appear...

<summary>This page serves as a dramatic invitation to the reader to witness the final moments of Jesus's life, urging the "devout soul" to join the Virgin Mary in mourning. It emphasizes the silence of Christ as his physical suffering reaches its peak and calls upon even the angels to observe the solemnity of his death.</summary>

everyone around the cross: with wonder: and unheard-of amazement: Contemplating every gesture of the agonizing: and dying **Jesus**. Seeing him completely consumed and in sorrow. strong and powerful, do not believe me otherwise: for the Gospel text says: that while the languishing **Jesus** was completely failing—no longer living or speaking—wishing to demonstrate that his heavenly virtue had not failed, with a great clamor and with a vigorous and high voice, miraculously crying out, he spoke his final word. <note>original: "pater in manus tuas commendo spiritum meum"</note> Father, into your hands I commend my spirit; that is: Father, into your hands I recommend my soul. And having said these aforementioned words, and having bowed his head, he gave up his holy soul to his eternal Father. And with that bow thus made—as I believe—toward his dear mother, he asked a final leave from her, as if wishing to say by that sign: "O my sweetest mother, O my most gentle mother, be at peace. Be comforted in the Lord; I go to the other world. I leave you at this moment. You will no longer see me alive with mortal life." And so the sacred body of **Jesus** remained upon the Cross. A stupendous and honorable lamentation was made

<column-break/>

for him, by the will of the eternal Father, by all creatures. Oh, how great a lament was made by the innumerable angelic hosts who had come there for that purpose. The heavenly bodies, unable to tolerate such a great sin, hid their light. The earth trembled. The tombs opened. The stones were split apart. The veil of the temple was torn. The dead bodies were resurrected. The Centurion confessed that he was truly the Son of God. The crowd beat their breasts; the soldiers surrendered themselves to their guilt; the entire machinery of the world <term>machina: a common Renaissance metaphor describing the universe as a complex, divinely-constructed engine or fabric</term> suffered with its Creator who was dead in human flesh. ...suffered in the world. I have seen him in such anxiety, in such agony, pain, and suffering that it could not be expressed with a human tongue. Therefore, my living son, placed in such bitterness, could well cry out and say, weeping with tears: *All you who pass along the way, etc.* <note>original: "vos omnes qui transitis per viam &c." — This is a reference to Lamentations 1:12, a verse used extensively in the Roman Catholic liturgy for Tenebrae and Holy Week to symbolize the suffering of Christ.</note> "All you who pass along the way of this wretched world: Look and consider if any sorrow like mine was ever found upon the earth." And I, poor disconsolate and wretched mother, who have felt every one of his afflictions with excessive and unspeakable pain—no differently than if I had received all his martyrdoms and torments in my own flesh—finding myself now deprived of all the good that was my only beloved son to me. Seeing him before my eyes so miserably deceased and dead, that he was barely able to say even one word to his distressed mother. How much heartache is 

<column-break/>

found in poor Mary, afflicted, pained, and sad. How much melancholy, how much sadness, how much pain, how much bitterness, how much torment is found in the heart of the wretched, pained Mary! Never was a pure creature so exhausted; never was a mother found more troubled; never was a woman seen so distressed. Therefore, even I—my son having expired and died, and being fixed to the Cross, and I not even being able to touch him—being placed in such affliction and heartache, can truly say: *All you who pass by the way, attend and see if there is any sorrow like my sorrow.* <note>original: "vos omnes qui transitis per viam attendite et videte si est dolor sicut dolor meus." — Mary here adopts the words of the prophet Jeremiah, which were historically interpreted as the voice of the crucified Christ, and applies them to her own maternal experience of the Passion.</note> "All you who pass along the way of this wretched and deceitful world: see and consider if a sorrow can be found similar to the sorrow of poor and disconsolate Mary," who, because of the measureless torment she suffered, has lost all strength and, as if lifeless and fainting, lies prostrate on the ground, completely spent and weakened without

# Fifth Part

## ¶ Contemplation on
## the eleventh Article of
## the Passion of the Savior,
## which contains the cruel spear-

¶ In the second chapter of Lamentations: <note>original: "Threnorum secundo capitulo"</note> To what shall I compare you? Or to what shall I liken you, O daughter of Jerusalem? To what shall I equal you, so that I may comfort you, O virgin daughter of Zion? For your heartbreak is as great as the sea; who shall heal you? Let your tears flow like a torrent day and night; give yourself no rest, and let not the pupil of your eye be silent. <note>This is a translation of Lamentations 2:13 and 2:18, traditionally used in the liturgy to express the sorrows of Mary.</note>

The sorrowful Mother, with her weeping company, had remained at the Cross; all the others had returned to the city of Jerusalem after they had seen that the Lord was dead. The unfortunate Mother did not know what to do, because she could not take her dead son down from the 

<column-break/>

cross, and to abandon him was impossible. Not knowing what remedy to take, she turned to harsh lamentation and tearful prayers. She prayed then to the Eternal Father that He would grant her His help in this extreme moment, for she could not satisfy her need to weep and lament anew, looking almost without ceasing upon her sweetest son hanging on the cross between two thieves, thus abandoned by all. The unhappy mother, transformed by her great grief entirely into her dead son, laments the cruelty of the Jews, who would not return her son to her, so torn and mistreated. She laments the nails that held him pierced. She laments the cross, which out of pity did not bow down to her. <note>A common poetic theme in medieval devotion where the inanimate cross is asked to show mercy by lowering its arms so the mother can reach her child.</note> She laments the crown of thorns, which remained fixed in his precious head. Oh, how immeasurable was Mary’s grief! Oh, how afflicted was that...

# Of Good Thinking

...her virginal body, which—do not believe otherwise—since the Last Supper, which he held on Wednesday <note>The text specifies Wednesday ("Mercore"), reflecting a tradition where the Last Supper occurred earlier in the week than the standard Maundy Thursday.</note> evening with his son in Bethany, had never been restored by any bodily food. Therefore, not by human power, but divine, was that sweet mother preserved in life amidst such excessive sorrows and labors, and in such a long fast. By now, it would be time for some compassion to be shown to her; by now, heaven and earth should be moved to pity to help her. By now, her great martyrdoms <term>martyrij: here referring to her intense spiritual and physical suffering, rather than death</term> should have an end. Because, as it is written in Joel, in the second chapter:

> Before his face the earth has trembled, the heavens have moved; the sun and the moon are darkened, and the stars have withdrawn their splendor; and the Lord has given his voice, etc. <note>original: "A facie eius contremuit terra: moti sunt celi: sol z luna obtenebrati sūt z stelle retraxerunt splendorem suum: z dñs dedit vocem suā zc." This is a quote from Joel 2:10-11, interpreted here as a cosmic reaction to Christ's death.</note>

That is to say, before the livid and dead face of the Lord, the earth has trembled and the heavens have been moved. The Sun and the Moon have been darkened, and the Stars have pulled back their splendor, and the Lord has given his

<column-break/>

voice, etc. But in all these things, no help is given to the completely fainting and anguished mother. Her sorrows do not diminish; on the contrary, from hour to hour they miserably increase. And to show that this is true: Note what follows. Thus, while the Virgin Mary was standing and lamenting in this way, she turned her eyes toward the city. Behold, she sees armed men coming once more, and ascending the mountain with fury. Because of this, the poor women were moved by great fear, not knowing for what purpose so many people were coming. They thought that perhaps they wanted to perform some new cruelty upon the sacred body of the deceased Jesus. Wherefore the pious mother, entirely terrified, begged them that they would no longer be cruel toward her dear son. These men, not deigning to answer her at all, immediately began to break and shatter the legs of the crucified thieves. And having finished killing them, they took them down from the crosses and threw them aside...

# Good Meditations

Mary, what a heart was yours when you saw the fruit of your womb hanging on the cross, struck by such a ruthless wound, and saw his precious blood pouring out from every side? I believe there is no human intellect that can comprehend such a measure of sorrow; your love was immense, and therefore your sorrow was also immense. The loss and deprivation was of an inestimable treasure, and therefore the torment of your heart was unspeakable. Not once alone was your soul wounded by the knife of great sorrow, but your loving heart was pierced by a mortal wound as many times as you saw any cruelty used against your loving and only son. But to you I speak, O hardened sinner! Now I turn to you, O sinful soul! Why are you not moved by such a cruel spectacle? Do you not consider that for you the Lord of the universe hangs upon the harsh wood of the cross? Now, do you not know that for you so many times has

<column-break/>

the Mother of God been convulsed with agony? Lift up then, at last, your eyes from such a heavy and deathly sleep. Contemplate with a taste of love your sweet, dead Redeemer. Run before he is taken down from the cross. Accompany that poor, distressed Mother. See if you can then, in this hour of overflowing mercy, obtain a full remission for your great sins. See if your frozen heart can be warmed at all by such a flame of love. Consider if ever, by the virtue of love, you have seen a similar thing done. Contemplate whether any love can be compared to this. Place then in your memory this great sign of love. Make for yourself a bundle—as if of bitter myrrh—of this so hard and bitter passion, and together with Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian monk and mystic who wrote extensively on the emotional intensity of Christ's Passion.</note> and other inflamed saints, you will say the words of the Song of Songs from the first chapter: *A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall lie between my breasts.* <note>original: "Fasciculus mirrhe dilectus meus: inter ubera mea commorabitur." From the Song of Songs 1:13, interpreted here as a metaphor for keeping the memory of Christ’s suffering close to one's heart.</note> Ma-

<meta>catchword: Ma-</meta>

<summary>This page reflects on the immense sorrow of Mary during the Crucifixion and calls upon the sinner to wake from their spiritual slumber, urging them to keep the memory of Christ's bitter Passion close to their heart like a bundle of myrrh.</summary>
<keywords>Virgin Mary, Crucifixion, Passion of Christ, repentance, Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, Song of Songs, mercy, divine love</keywords>
