# Part Five

[A bu]ndle of myrrh my beloved shall be to me: <note>This is a reference to the Song of Solomon 1:13, a verse often used in medieval devotion to signify keeping the memory of Christ’s suffering close to one's heart.</note> and he shall always stay in the midst of my breast. From this, I desire to be entirely renewed in charity. From this, I hope my heart will be entirely set on fire. O sweet Jesus, who for me are so cruelly wounded: wound my stubborn heart, I pray you. Make me, Jesus, languish for your love. Make me, for you, always sighing and weeping. Kindle my soul with your great fire. Warm my spirit with your great flame; enamor the very marrow <term>medulle: the innermost or most essential part of one's being</term> of my senses with your excessive love. O sweet Jesus! O gentle love! O fragrant wound! O honey-sweet sore! Bathe me in that your precious and holy blood. Wash me with that most sweet and most clear water <note>A reference to the blood and water that flowed from Christ's side when pierced by the lance.</note>. Crown my proud head with that your crown of thorns. Transfix my vain feet and my sinful hands with those your iron and hard nails. Put me inside your sacred side. Grant that with you I may always be, in soul

<column-break/>

and in body, crucified. Grant that my heart may be with you. By you, my sweet love, may it be always wounded, so that I never consign to oblivion <note>original: "obuione" (oblivion/forgetfulness)</note> so many benefits granted by you to me. O sacred head, how I see you for me all pierced by thorns and crowned with a stinging crown. O blessed and sacred feet: how I see you, because of my great sins, pierced and punctured by cruel nails. O pure and immaculate hands: how I contemplate you transfixed with those hard nails. O sacred and white breast, by what a cruel wound I see you opened wide. O virginal and delicate limbs: how, for my sake, I look upon you all beaten and torn. O loving Jesus, when I look at you thus crucified and dead for me, I would wish to dissolve entirely into tears. I would wish to burn and blaze entirely with your holy love. Always I would wish to remember you. Always I would wish to commemorate your wounds. Always I would wish to have you in my memory...

...remains imprinted. I always wish to have you carved into my heart. ⁋ Unheard of benefits: ⁋ Graces always to be remembered: O precious blood, O most sweet wounds: O most sweet nails: O most sweet cross: O most noble crown: Come, all of you, to me and let each of you perform your full office <note>In this context, 'office' refers to the specific spiritual function or duty that each instrument of the Passion (the nails, the crown, etc.) performs on the soul.</note> within me. For I desire to be nailed by the nails, crowned by the crown. Wounded by the wounds. Crucified by the cross: To be cleansed by the blood. To be bathed by the tears: to be tormented by the pains and torments of my Jesus. I already begin to warm up. <column-break/>

-ness; I am overcome by such love: the bonds of my evil life are broken. The divine arrows have penetrated my hard heart. My intestines and my viscera are warmed by the celestial and supernal <note>original: "superna" (heavenly or from above)</note> flame. ⁋ O arrow of love, how sweetly you know how to wound the heart. ⁋ O divine bow, how powerful you are to pierce every hard and iron mind. ⁋ Jesus, it can well be said that with your sweet love you have shattered the stones: broken the rocks: warmed the ice: set the snow on fire: and everything sweetly and with gentleness drawn to yourself. Happy is he who feels wounded by you. Blessed is he who feels warmed by you. Content is he who feels enamored of you. Consoled is he who, languishing for you and consuming himself, despises and leaves every worldly consolation. Wise is he who with all his heart rejoices and sings:
> My soul refuses to be comforted except by you, my God <note>original: "Renuit cōsolari anima mea nisi de te deus meus" — a variation of Psalm 77:2.</note>; I want you: I desire you: I love you: I long for you with my whole mind: so that I may be with you and live with you, blessed forever. Amen.

It ends with a Latin-derived declaration of the soul's total desire for God over worldly comfort.</summary>

# Part Five

Contemplation on the same eleventh article of the passion of the Savior: which describes how the kind Jesus was taken down from the cross. Jeremiah, chapter six. "Make for yourself mourning as for an only son, a bitter lamentation." <note>original Latin: "Luctum unigeniti fac tibi planctum amarũ"</note>. Ecclesiasticus, chapter thirty-eight. "Shed tears over the dead, and as one who has suffered terribly, begin to weep." <note>original Latin: "In mortuum produc lachrymas & quasi dira passus incipe plorare"</note>.

Already the sun was setting towards the west, and no human help appeared to be able to take the sacred body of Jesus down from the cross and to bury it with the due honor and reverence. Everyone had departed from there. Only the poor Mary with her holy company had remained there, without any relief, full of tears, groans, and sighs, all weak

<column-break/>

for having not yet eaten or drunk. And she had wept so many tears that almost no moisture remained in her weary body. ¶ In this state of necessity, that poor and wretched mother <note>The original "mesina" is likely "meschina," meaning wretched or pitiable in a way that evokes the reader's sympathy.</note> found herself at that hour. The day was ending, but her harsh lamentations did not end. Her physical strength was failing, but the tears and the great weeping did not fail. She stood there all anxious, not knowing what to do or what counsel to take. Behold, you see coming some noble men of the city along the mountain; wherefore, at the first sight, suspecting some new evil, her heart received a great wound. But she was immediately comforted by John, who said to her: "Do not fear, O dear mother. For I recognize these men to be our friends." And thus, as soon as they arrived, they were known to be Joseph of Arimathea and Nicodemus: just and noble men, and previously secret disciples of the Lord, but now in this situation made manifest.

Indeed, because Joseph had boldly gone—moved by compassion for this pious mother—to Pilate; and having obtained the body of Jesus as a gift, and being accompanied then by Nicodemus and others, they came bringing myrrh and aloes, nearly a hundred pounds <note>This large quantity—about 32 kilograms—reflects the account in the Gospel of John, emphasizing a burial of royal dignity.</note>, along with the tools to be able to take down the sacred body of Jesus from the cross and return it into the power of his disconsolate mother, and then honorably send it to the new and glorious tomb. When these noble men had arrived with their company, they showed great reverence to the grieving Mary, greeting and consoling her with affectionate words and sweet offers, letting her understand that they had been secret disciples of her son, but that now they wished to make up for their failings. For they had determined to be ready to risk their lives, their wealth, and everything they could for the honor of her son and for her—whom they always wished to regard as their own good mother—nor would they ever fail her in

<column-break/>

whatever they could provide. And their speech was not even finished when, looking upon such great cruelty used against their dear master and this so worthy mother, moved to compassion, they began to weep and cry; and they grieved all the more because they recognized they had committed an error in having been so timid in defending and helping their just Lord. But being urged by his Mother to quickly take down that sacred body, they placed the ladders against the Cross; with as much reverence and haste as they could, they took him down and placed him upon a white linen cloth spread out on the ground. There, immediately, the wretched mother <note>The Italian "meschina" here carries the sense of "pitiable" or "heart-breaking," intended to evoke the reader's sympathy for Mary's state.</note>, overcome by great desire, kissed and embraced him. And so, kneeling together with Nicodemus and all the devout company, she adored him and thanked him for the hard torments and bitter death suffered and received for human redemption. Then, everyone gathered around the said sacred body, some at the head, some at the hands, and some at the feet.

### Part Five

...some from one side, and some from the other. They all began to make together a new and great lamentation. That afflicted mother said—having taken into her virginal lap the holy head of her only son: "O precious head, how cruelly you have been pierced by stinging thorns. Nazarene hair <note>A reference to the tradition that Christ, as a 'Nazarene,' wore his hair long and beautiful, often associated with the 'Nazirite' vow of holiness.</note>, which were such an ornament to this head: who has thus uprooted and torn you out?" Then, kissing that bruised face with great bitterness, she said: "O my face, which used to be so graceful and beautiful: how you are now made dark and deformed. O face full of graces; o angelic face; divine face, how I see you now all failing, discolored, and dead. O my eyes, which shone for your mother more than the stars: how I see in you now every light extinguished." Then, repeating her loving and sorrowful kisses upon his cold mouth, she said: "O my holy mouth. My celestial mouth. O my sweetened mouth: with how much harshness and

<column-break/>

bitterness you have been filled. O blessed tongue: which announced to the lost world with such charity the saving and evangelical law: how are you now made entirely mute and silent? Why do you no longer speak to your so sorrowful mother? Is this now that sweet mouth which preached so gently to all those in tribulation? Are these now those hands which worked so many miracles? Where has your so joyful appearance gone? Where is the face so delightful, gracious, and beautiful?" Then, kissing his hands, she said: "White, candid hands, how you have been wickedly pierced with hard nails, and all torn." Thus, clasping her loving son and every beaten limb of his, with tearful gazes—finding herself handling him with her virginal hands and kissing all that venerable and sacred body with her blessed mouth—she moved from one part to another. Now the precious brow. Now the holy face. Now the delicate mouth. Now the sweet hands. No... <note>original: "Ho", a catchword for the next page's first word "Hora" (Now).</note>

### Part Five

...[you knelt] with such delight at his **holy feet**: no longer will you be defended by him **against** <note>OCR reads "ꝑtra", likely a shorthand or error for "contra" (against).</note> the disciple and the Pharisee. Who will now be my Lord? Who will be my refuge? Who will be my breath? Oh, how sorrowful I am! Oh, how unhappy I am! How can I live without my life? How can I breathe without my soul? How will it ever be possible for me to depart from here, leaving all my "good" behind? Alas, for I can no longer stay with my Master! Alas, for I shall no longer be called the **beloved disciple**!"

# On Pious Meditation

...people of the Lord. Never again will I be comforted in this way. I will never laugh again in this life, for I have lost my **teacher**, and my only love has been taken from me." And so, all those other **holy women** also wept bitterly. While such lamentable voices were heard from afar, behold, the sorrowful **Peter** arrives. Seeing his so-beloved Master thus dead, naked, and stretched out upon the ground, and hearing all the others making such harsh cries and sobs, he could not restrain himself from crying out in a loud voice from the immeasurable pain he felt: "Alas, unhappy me! Alas, wretched me! O poor Peter! O disgraced Peter! O treacherous Jews! How impious and cruel you have been to treat my **gentle Master** so badly!" Then, falling to his knees, he adored that sacred body and asked for mercy for his **great committed error** <note>This refers to Peter's denial of Jesus during the trial, a source of profound guilt in Passion narratives.</note>. Then, stretched out on the ground, he prayed that the Lord would let him die with Him. Oh, how much grace

<column-break/>

it would have been for him if he could have been dead and buried with his **sweet Master**. The anguished Peter longed to embrace his love, Jesus, and to kiss those holy limbs; but he did not dare, having denied Him so many times and even basely abandoned Him. But the **Mother**, who was all love, recognizing this burning desire of his, invited him and gave him the opportunity to do so. And thus the inflamed Peter, taking those sacred and venerable limbs with great reverence, kissed them with a great fire of love and bathed them with a flood of tears. Who could ever explain the great heartache of the **Apostle Peter**? Time fails me to do so, nor is my pen <note>original: "calamo," literally a reed used as a writing instrument.</note> sufficient for such a task; therefore, you, devout contemplator, will strive for yourself in your **silence** to feel with your heart what my pen cannot explain to you.

# Part Five

Not only **Peter**, but all the other disciples were present at this mournful sight; arriving one after the other at such a cruel spectacle, they constantly renewed their pains and sighs. And thus, by divine disposition, they all gathered together in such weeping and such lamentation that the like was never done in the world, nor shall the like ever be done again. Nor can I imagine that, out of reverence for the Lord, there were not present innumerable squads of **angels**, who all together with the **Mother of God**, and with the **apostles**, and with the **Marys**, and with **Joseph** and **Nicodemus**, and with all the elements and celestial bodies <note>In medieval theology, it was believed that all of nature—the sun, moon, and elements—suffered and "wept" during the Crucifixion.</note>, did not weep for the cruel death of their Creator. This was fitting to honor the Mother and the Son. It was appropriate for her to lament her only-begotten, for it was written by the prophet:
> "Make mourning as for an only son, a most bitter lamentation." <note>original Latin: "luctum vnigeniti fac tibi planctum amarum" — a reference to Jeremiah 6:26.</note>

In the death of your only son, there shall be mourning and bitter weeping. And so for the **apostles** and for the devout women

<column-break/>

this same thing was fitting, because the wise **Ecclesiasticus** <note>Also known as the Book of Sirach.</note> says:
> "Bring forth tears for the dead, and begin to wail as if you had suffered dreadfully." <note>original Latin: "In mortuum produc lachrymas: quasi dira passus incipe plorare" — Sirach 38:16.</note>

If it is wise for us to weep for the natural and ordinary death of our dear fathers, mothers, children, relatives, and brothers, how much more is it our duty to weep for Him who is our **God**, our **Creator**, our **Father**, and our **Redeemer**? He who is more than a father, more than a mother, more than a son, brother, or relative. He who, not by a light or natural cause, but by a most cruel and atrocious death, has been wrongly killed and condemned for us—He who never committed a sin. He who had come here to earth from heaven for our salvation. O human mind, how cruel and fierce you have become, if at least in this final instance you do not honor with some pious tears your **Savior** who died so lovingly for you! O ungrateful man! O obstinate man! Why is it so heavy for you to weep...

<sig>q</sig>

<vocab>Passion, Mary, Peter, Nicodemus, Joseph, Ecclesiasticus, weeping, tears, Redeemer</vocab>

Why can you not deeply sigh with **John**? Why can you not groan with **Joseph** and **Nicodemus**? Why can you not roar <note>A term often used in medieval devotion to describe the loud, visceral cries of Saint Peter's repentance.</note> with **Peter** the apostle, and lament with the women? Why can you not grieve with those so-beloved disciples? Why can you not make lamentation with the **angels**? Now, do you not see that everyone invites you to weep?

<column-break/>

To this you are called by heaven and earth; invited by the mother and the son; spurred on by the Creator and by all creation <note>The original "creatura" refers to all created beings and the natural world.</note>. Now weep, then, and delay no more. Adore your Creator, naked on the ground and dead, stretched out completely. Embrace him as a father, and kiss him as your sweet brother, and *bring forth tears for the dead*, <note>original Latin: "in mortuum produc lachrymas"</note> and *begin to wail as if you had suffered dreadfully*. <note>original Latin: "quasi dira passus incipe plorare"</note> Amen.

# Part Five

It being the day of Friday <note>Original: "giorno di Uenere." In the liturgical and early Italian calendar, Friday was often referred to by its classical name, the day of Venus.</note>, with the day of the Sabbath to follow: having bound the body of **Jesus** in the shroud together with the aromatic spices, according to the custom of the **Jews**, they buried him in a new monument, cut into stone, which was nearby in the garden where the Lord had been crucified. And having placed a large stone at the entrance of the said tomb, they departed. But the women who were sitting opposite the tomb diligently contemplated the place of the burial and in what manner they had buried him. And returning home, they prepared the perfumes and ointments, resting on the Sabbath according to the commandment. But the chief—

<column-break/>

—priests and the **Pharisees**, on the following day of the Sabbath, came to **Pilate**, saying: Lord, we have remembered that that deceiver <note>A derogatory term used by the authorities in the Gospel narrative to refer to Jesus during his life.</note>, while still alive, said: "After three days I will rise again." Command, therefore, that the tomb be guarded until the third day, lest his **disciples** come there to steal him, and then say to the people that he has risen from death to life. And so the last error would be worse than the first. To whom **Pilate** replied, saying: You have the guard; go, and guard it as you know how. And so they, going, placed the guards at the tomb, sealing the stone with seals.

Already the gates of hell are smashed and broken; already the fathers in **Limbo** <term>Limbo: A place where the souls of the righteous who died before the time of Christ were said to wait for his arrival to be brought to heaven.</term> are made glorious. On earth—that is, in the middle inhabited region where **Jerusalem** is situated and placed—signs of glorious victory begin to appear. Following behind her only son until the final and extreme burial, and with her to mourn such a cruel death, and the many impieties and atrocious persecutions committed against her innocent and pure son. Since the hour was then growing late, while the grieving mother continued her harsh, great lament, it was reminded to her by **John** <note>Saint John the Apostle, whom Jesus entrusted with the care of his mother while on the cross.</note> that it was no longer possible to delay, and that it was necessary to quickly bury her son. This was because everything was prepared, and already the Sun was about to set. To whom the poor mother responded: "Alas, how hard..."

clean shroud with myrrh and

And although the grieving

wretched mother carried, together

# Part Five

...son to the final burial. And so everyone wept bitterly with her: everyone cried: everyone sighed: having no less compassion for the grieving Mother than for the dead and departed son. And it is not to be doubted that just as innumerable Angels had accompanied the soul of Jesus to the limbo <term>limbo de santi padri: The "Limbo of the Fathers," a place where the souls of the righteous who died before Christ were said to wait for his resurrection</term> of the holy fathers: so too an innumerable multitude of them were present at these funeral rites of this sacred body: which, although it was dead and separated from the soul, was nevertheless always united and joined with divinity. And therefore, just as he was worthy of all human and celestial honor, so the eternal Father willed that he should be wonderfully and most honorably accompanied to the glorious tomb by the celestial armies and by the most worthy creatures of the world. And for this reason, He also willed that his tomb should be noble, new, and made of stone: and that no one else had ever been

<column-break/>

placed inside, so that it would be adored by the whole world: by all of which properties and conditions the prophecy of Isaiah was verified, which says:
> And his tomb shall be glorious, etc. <note>original Latin: "Et erit sepulchrum eius gloriosum ꝛc." from Isaiah 11:10</note>
In such a monument, then, was placed this sacred and venerable body of the most merciful Jesus: that wretched mother was always present, helping to arrange him in the said burial, never ceasing from continuous weeping: but when they later covered the tomb with a stone slab and it was necessary to depart from there, imagine the pain of that anguish-stricken mother. Once again the great lamentation was restarted: and she wept so bitterly that all were moved to tears anew. That completely afflicted and consumed mother said:
> O how hard is this final separation: O my son, now you are closed in the dark monument: now I can no longer see you: now it is no longer granted to me to be able to kiss and touch you...

<meta>This page begins a new section of the manuscript, a treatise on the Imitation of Christ, following the standard medieval and early modern devotional tradition.</meta>

### Here begins the treatise on the imitation of Christ.

Since our kind and dear Savior Jesus Christ is the ideal and unique exemplar of all virtues, perfections, and holy customs, he left behind such a life as a man active in this world that he remained a living mirror for the elect, a rule for every spiritual perfection, and an example of absolute holiness. And therefore, if you desire to be enlightened regarding what you must do, or what you must flee, and how to climb to the sublime and perfect state of virtue: always place before you this most clear and vital mirror—that is, the life and conduct of the Son of God, Jesus Christ our Lord. For this purpose he was sent to us from heaven by his divine Father: namely, to go before us on the path of the virtues, and to give us the law of life and of discipline by his own

<column-break/>

example. Just as we are naturally created in his blessed image, so too, by the imitation of virtues according to our power, we might be reformed into the likeness of his divine conduct. And here many err, who, being dedicated to the contemplation of the life of the Lord Jesus Christ, stop there—not caring to put that life into practice within themselves. For it is of little value to know what such a divine teacher has done if one does not execute the same in works; such a life was not ordained except to be imitated by us. As the Lord himself said to his disciples:
> For I have given you an example, that as I have done, so you should also do. <note>original Latin: "exemplum enim dedi vobis: ut quemadmodum ego feci: ita & vos faciatis"</note>

And by doing so, we deserve to be called <term>Christians</term> <note>The author explains the etymology of the word "Christian" as literally meaning "imitator of Christ."</note>, which means imitators of Christ; and this is the purpose of meditation and contemplation of his life and passion. You shall therefore make for yourself—as the beloved bride of the <note>original: "cantica," referring to the Song of Solomon (Song of Songs)</note> confesses of herself—a bundle of myrrh from the life of the Beloved

<summary>This page introduces a treatise on the Imitation of Christ, arguing that Jesus's life serves as a "living mirror" and that true Christianity requires practicing his virtues rather than merely contemplating them.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Imitation of Christ, spiritual perfection, virtues, Christian life, contemplation vs action, Song of Solomon</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Imitation of Christ," moving from the definition of a Christian to the practical gathering of Christ's virtues as examples for the soul's conduct.</meta>

that which is always held as dead within your breast <note>The author refers to "bearing the death of Christ" in one's heart, a common theme of inner devotion where the worldly self dies to live in Him.</note>: that is, you will gather together all the **virtuous works** and **divine deeds** of Him; and following His example, by subduing rebellious passions and disordered affections, you will run after Him along the immaculate path of His most splendid and heroic virtues.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' containing a portrait of a bearded man from the chest up, possibly a saint, with hands joined in prayer, set within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>
**For example** <note>original Latin: "Exempli gratia"</note>, you will gather these small branches in the divine forest of His blessed life: 
How **humble** He was among men and a lover of the humble. 
How **kind** and **gentle** He was among His poor disciples. 
How **merciful** He was toward the poor, to whom He even made Himself similar in every way; and from the poor alone He chose to select His blessed family and company until the end. 
How He despised no one, however base, nor held them in horror, even if they were struck by abominable leprosy. 
How He did not flatter the rich and powerful in

<column-break/>

any matter. 
How free He was from the cares of this world, and without anxiety for the necessities and needs of the body; for which reason, by His example, He exhorted us saying: 
> **Do not be anxious** <note>original Latin: "nolite soliciti esse," a reference to the Sermon on the Mount in Matthew 6:25</note>, etc. 
And He sent us to the consideration of the birds of the air and the flowers of the earth, etc. 
How modest He was in His divine appearance, and full of holy respect. 
How **patient** in insults and most gentle in His responses. 
How He never wished to avenge Himself with biting or bitter words, but with sweet and humble response He strove to heal the malice of others. 
How well-mannered and composed He was in all His gestures and acts; how He was solicitous for the salvation of souls, for the love of whom He even wished, with such readiness, to die such a horrendous death. 
How He gave us an example of every good throughout His entire life, and doctrine in avoiding every danger of sin or scandal, such as in fleeing excessively familiar conversations with women, especially...

How full of zeal He was where the honor of His divine and blessed Father was concerned. 
How He always fled from all boasting and ostentation of singularity <note>The act of trying to appear unique or superior to others to gain attention.</note>, and similarly taught His imitators to do these and other similar things of perfection.

Contemplating Him—who, because He was always excellent and most perfect, continually conducted Himself in an excellent and most perfect manner—you shall seek with every effort to put these virtues into practice within yourself, and to be a true imitator of Him in whose name and title you glory.

<meta>This page transitions from a general list of Christ's virtues to a specific framework for self-discipline based on the "three powers of the soul," a common concept in medieval and Renaissance psychology. It focuses on the first power: the irascible soul (how we handle anger and conflict).</meta>

...our whole life is reduced to [these three]; and if we regulate them virtuously in **imitation of Jesus Christ**, we can easily be called Christians and true imitators of Christ. One is the **irascible**; the other the **concupiscible**; the third the **rational** <term>The "three powers" refer to the Platonic and Aristotelian divisions of the soul: the rational (reason), the concupiscible (desire for pleasure/sustenance), and the irascible (emotions like anger or courage used to overcome obstacles).</term>: from which all our vices originate if they are not restrained and moderated. On the contrary, every good proceeds from them if we strive to regulate and moderate these three powers with the rules of virtue, imitating Christ—who mortified every root of our vices and evils, and gave life to the seed of all our goods. 

First, regarding the **irascible**: which by natural inclination and the persuasion of the infernal enemy <note>The devil.</note> cannot endure injuries done to it, nor crooked words, but wants to dominate and take revenge. You who are desirous of imitating Christ, look into the living mirror of every good, Jesus Christ, and consider if ever in matters pertaining to Himself He was

<column-break/>

angered <note>original: "corozato," likely a variant of *corrucciato*.</note> or disturbed; and if ever He showed revenge or a sign of revenge in deeds or words toward His persecutors. On the contrary, there you will find supreme patience, unheard-of equanimity, and endurance. The scribes and Pharisees called Him a glutton <note>original: "voratore & pachione," referring to someone who eats to excess.</note> and a wine-drinker, a companion of tax collectors and rascals, a demonic doctor, a blasphemer of God, and a seducer; and He never used a biting or strange response, but always full of equanimity He gave medicinal responses <note>Responses intended to heal the soul of the listener rather than to wound them back.</note> with all gentleness and serenity of soul. 

Similarly, in the hard beatings, wounds, and scourges suffered through the palaces and courts of the princes in **Jerusalem**, He behaved no differently than a most gentle lamb, as the **prophet Isaiah** said: "led to the slaughter." <note>A reference to the "Suffering Servant" prophecy in Isaiah 53:7.</note> In whose injuries, reproaches, and scourges, you who are desirous of imitating your Lord will attentively consider their greatness, number, and variety; everything that you suffer will be sweet to you, and 

<summary>The author explains how to achieve a Christian life by moderating the three powers of the soul, focusing here on using Christ's example of patience and gentleness to overcome the "irascible" urge for revenge.</summary>
<keywords>three powers of the soul, irascible, concupiscible, rational, imitation of Christ, patience, equanimity, Isaiah, Jerusalem, Pharisees</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instruction on how to imitate Christ's patience. Having previously discussed how Christ handled verbal insults from the Pharisees, the author now transitions to a vivid, step-by-step meditation on the physical and mental tortures of the Passion to serve as a model for the "inexperienced" soul.</meta>

...full of sweetness; and to provide a path for the inexperienced <note>The "inexperienced" refers to those new to spiritual practice or the "purgative way" of the soul.</note>, I have set aside the consideration of what He suffered during the rest of His entire life—which was one continuous cross and martyrdom—to look instead at what was done to your delicate and most sweet Lord on the last day that He lived in this mortal way. Consider how one soldier in the garden, more reckless than the others, seized the most innocent Lamb with cruel hands; another binds Him; another cries out against Him; one shoves Him; another and then another vexes Him; one blasphemes, while another spits his stinking and coarse <note>original: "roxeamẽti," likely *rozzamente* (coarsely or rudely).</note> phlegm upon that precious face; another turns Him here and there. Another interrogates Him; from another, false testimonies are sought against Him; and others accompany such an inquisitor of falsehood; one accuses Him and another gives false witness against Him; one mocks and scoffs at Him; another veils His blessed eyes. That one strikes and slaps <note>original: "colaphiza," from the Latin *colaphizare*, meaning to strike with the fist.</note> the face desired by heaven, and with heavy fists until the living flesh

<column-break/>

is bruised to the point of bleeding; another binds Him to the column; another strips Him. That one, when He is led away, shoves Him; another beats Him; another shouts against Him. This one receives Him with insults to vex Him; and upon Him, by another, a cruel command is executed. That one, in contempt, clothes Him in purple; and with very sharp thorns another crowns that venerable head; one gives the reed into His hand; and furiously another, taking it back, strikes the thorny head; one pulls Him by the venerable beard; and another, in mockery, kneels before Him; and another, mocking <note>original: "calefandolo," likely a variant of *beffandolo* (mocking) or *calunniandolo* (slandering).</note> Him, hails Him as king. See how the King of all the world is honored and treated! Here and there, up and down, He is led and led back; He is despised and rejected; turned and turned again like an impious man and a madman; and conducted like a most wicked thief and evildoer: now to Annas, now to Caiaphas; now to Pilate, now to Herod; and again to Pilate; now inside, now outside. Oh, my Lord, You are treated [like]...

<summary>This page provides a graphic, step-by-step meditation on the physical abuses Christ suffered during His Passion, from the arrest in the Garden to the trials before Annas, Caiaphas, Pilate, and Herod.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the vivid meditation on the Passion of Christ, moving from the initial physical assaults in the garden and before the high priests to the agonizing procession toward Calvary. It aims to shame the reader's own lack of patience by contrasting their minor inconveniences with Christ's extreme suffering.</meta>

...like a wind-ball. <note>An image of being tossed about or treated as an object of no value.</note> But does it seem to you, my soul, that these things are very hard and very bitter?
But hear even more:
The chief priests, the scribes, and Pharisees, and thousands of the people gather most obstinately against your Lord; and from everyone the cry is shouted: "Let the evildoer be crucified!" The heavy and massive cross is placed upon His torn and bruised <note>original: "cõfrate," likely *confrante* (shattered or bruised).</note> shoulders, upon which He must be fastened. From every side, locals and pilgrims, great and small, run toward Him. The lowest scoundrels, gamblers, and wine-drinkers also encounter Him, not to pity Him, but to taunt Him. <note>original: "calefarlo," literally to "heat Him up," used here to mean mocking or harassing Him.</note>
There is no one who recognizes Him, because He is afflicted and covered by everyone with mud and other filth. And while He carries His ignominy, He has become a byword <note>original: "i parabola." A biblical idiom meaning to become an object of mockery or a cautionary tale.</note> to them; and those who sat at the city gate spoke ill of Him, and those who drank wine sang songs in His reproach. He is exhausted, distressed, urged to walk, and pulled violently with ropes; and thus fatigued, scourged, and completely

<column-break/>

torn and worn out, and saturated to the height of insults, He is not allowed even a moment to rest or to revive His afflicted spirit until He has reached that most filthy and foul-smelling place of Mount Calvary. In which place, hear what end and what goal is given to the harsh pains of your Beloved and Giver: the bitter crucifixion and the bed of sorrow.
Behold what rest from every harsh war—more difficult than your own—is given to your God. Oh you, who rebel <note>original: "recalcitri," literally to kick back like a stubborn animal.</note> at every tiny word spoken against you, and who complain so much about the tribulations you suffer that you cannot endure them:
Look at the Passion of your Lord, and you will see what sort of thing and what comparison exists between what you tolerate and what your most mild <term>Savior</term> <note>Referring to Christ as the *Salvatore*, emphasizing His role as the redeemer through this suffering.</note> endured; and through such consideration, everything will seem light to you. If you lose your belongings, you will not be angry, seeing the Emperor of the universe naked upon the cross, having nowhere to lay His head. If you are offended in your reputation and evil is spoken of you, console [yourself...]

...[you will see] <note>This continues a word from the previous page, likely "vederai" (you will see).</note> while seeing your venerable Lord so greatly blasphemed. <note>original: "biastemato."</note> If you fall ill and find yourself abandoned by everyone, by seeing Christ—abandoned not only by men but even by the eternal God the Father—you will comfort yourself and be consoled. And so, through such considerations, by conquering the irascible <term>irascible: in medieval psychology, the faculty of the soul concerned with anger, courage, and the drive to overcome obstacles</term> you will not be able to be disturbed. You will have no fear of disdain, of anger, or of hatred; nor will you give voice to grumbling. You will banish every impatience, every sadness, melancholy, and anxiety. <note>original: "affanno."</note> And you will be able to reach such a state that with great delight you will even provoke anxieties and tribulations against yourself with all your heart. Thus, transformed into another person and united with Christ through perfect imitation, you will be a true and perfect Christian in both reality and name.

As for the concupiscible <term>concupiscible: the faculty of the soul that pursues physical pleasure and avoids pain</term> faculty, which inclines us toward delightful objects and makes us sensual and delicate (which is contrary to spiritual progress), it is necessary to tame and perfectly

<column-break/>

conquer it, following the example of Christ. It is not enough to overcome things inflicted externally; one must also triumph over oneself. Sensuality <term>sensualità: the part of human nature governed by the senses rather than reason</term> disturbs us regarding good food and drink, leisure, games, and other comforts. upon the prickling hay; and just as Saint Bernard says: "He who is not deceived chose what is most troublesome to the flesh." <note>original Latin: "qui non fallitur elegit quod carni molestius est"</note> In the progress of his life, he lay upon the bare earth and hard stones; and with bare feet and naked soles he walked. At the exit of his life, he rested upon the hardest bed of the cross: behold, these are the exquisite riches <note>original: "dititie" (delights or riches)</note> of our God. Who then is that lover of Christ who, having such an example before them, would still strive to seek out delicate, superfluous, and soft clothes, seeing that our Love, Christ, not only refused superfluous clothing, but even rejected what was necessary, ascending entirely naked upon the high and hard cross? Who will complain about living in narrow rooms that are not well adorned or crafted, seeing his Lord staying in an inn, wandering as a pilgrim in Egypt, staying in the desert in the open air, and finally placed upon the cross in the open, in the wind, and in the scorching sun? And he did so in such a case and time when a base scandal was much to be feared; as for example: when those three Eastern Kings and Magi—who with such fervor and labor and dangers had sought him out to worship him as King of the Jews and true God—let himself be found in such a lowly place as the inn where he was born in Bethlehem, shared with unclean animals.
One might have doubted that such great masters, seeing such things and signs of misery, would consider themselves deluded and deceived, and would depart scandalized without faith or devotion. But for this, the lover of humility did not leave...

<meta>Following the conclusion of the previous spiritual instruction, this page introduces the organizational structure of the work. The text transitions from the general goals of a holy life to a specific, five-part curriculum designed to guide the reader through a deep meditation on the Passion of Christ.</meta>

# Table of Contents of the Book of Thinking Well

In the first part are described twelve beautiful and useful <term>instructions</term> <note>original: "documenti" — foundational teachings or guidelines for spiritual practice</note> and notes that those meditating on the life-giving Passion of our Redeemer must know.

In the second part are described twelve wonderful fruits acquired by the devout who meditate upon the bitter Passion of our Lord.

In the third part are set forth twelve urgent incentives by which every person, no matter how <term>unlearned</term> <note>original: "idiota" — in this historical context, this refers to laypeople or those without formal theological education, rather than an insult</note>, will be led to devoutly mourn the cruel death of Christ Jesus our Redeemer.

In the fourth part are described twelve golden rules that the devout must observe while meditating and contemplating the Passion of our kind Jesus.

In the fifth and final part, the history of the Passion of the Lord is divided into twelve articles according to the text of the holy Evangelists; and following these are many pious meditations along with the bitter weeping of the Virgin Mary.
